# The Gauntlet — Volume I

## A challenge-mode research guide — twenty-five feature-sized builds, zero handed answers
---

## How to read this document

This is not a tutorial. It will never show you the build. It is a **research guide and interview coach** for twenty-five small builds, each chosen to make you fluent — fast, and deeply — across the surface that jobs and freelance work are actually asking for right now.

The rule that governs every page:

> **You do all the implementation yourself. This document hands you the questions, the tradeoffs, the fences, and the way to prove you're done — never the code.**

The one exception is a _contract_: a type signature, an API shape, a database schema, a state enum. Those are specifications, not solutions — the same way a job ticket is not the same as the commit that closes it. Where a genuinely hard problem needs a nudge, you'll find a three-tier hint ladder folded inside a `<details>` block. Opening it is your choice, and the third tier only after two real attempts.

**Use it à la carte.** The twenty-five are grouped into seven parts by theme, and the parts have a suggested order, but the grouping is a convenience, not a mandate. You picked these because you keep seeing them in job descriptions. So when a specific posting lands in front of you — "must have GraphQL," "WordPress a plus," "experience with data migrations" — jump straight to that module and run it. The shared machinery in this preamble makes every module self-contained.

**Run it under the coach.** This document is designed to be driven by the `/challenge-phase-guide` command: it will walk you one step at a time, ask the _Think_ questions before the _Do_, refuse to advance until a _Check_ is demonstrated, and give hints only when you ask. Everything below assumes that runtime. You don't have to use it — but the pacing was written for it.

---

## Why this works — the premise

You said something worth taking literally: that you make your own luck, that you got your first job because you were going to study hard anyway and the bootcamp was just the vehicle, and that you want to show the universe you're ready by getting ready _now_.

Good. This document is that idea turned into a machine. **Readiness is not a mood you wait to feel. It is an inventory you build** — a stack of things you have made, broken, fixed, and can talk about without notes. Luck, in the version that actually shows up, is what happens when that inventory meets an opportunity. So we are going to manufacture the inventory on a schedule. Six ideas hold the whole thing up:

**Features, not projects.** A feature is the smallest unit of work that still forces real engineering decisions — what shape is the data, what happens when it fails, how does it wire together, is it fast enough — without the dead weight of scaffolding a whole application. Twenty-five features touch twenty-five territories in the time two apps would take to boot. Each fits in one folder and one conversation. You are building `gauntlet/` with a subfolder per module; nobody needs a repo tour to see what you did.

**Decisions are the curriculum.** The artifact is just the receipt. The learning is the _decision record_. No interviewer, design reviewer, or person at a meetup ever probes your keystrokes — they probe your choices. "Why cursor pagination?" "Why open addressing?" "When does an Observable earn its weight over a promise?" So every module is built around four to six **named decisions**, each presented as a live option space with the tradeoff axes exposed and the answer withheld. You close it yourself, in writing, before you build. That written commitment is the point; the code just confirms it.

**Every feature sits on a trap.** Each build is chosen twice over: it passes through the subject's **center of gravity** — the knowledge that generalizes to everything else in that territory — _and_ it makes the subject's **classic trap** unavoidable. The trap is the famous pitfall every practitioner has a scar from: the stale-response race, the index the planner refuses to use, the `volatile` the optimizer deletes, the sync that was secretly a data-loss policy. You cannot finish the feature without meeting the trap. And meeting the trap is exactly what makes you conversational — because war stories are the currency of technical talk. "I once shipped a search box that showed results for the wrong query half the time, and here's why" is worth more in a room than any amount of recited theory.

**Bounded depth beats unbounded curiosity.** Here is your actual failure mode, named plainly: not shallowness — you go _deep_. You go three links deep into something fascinating and lose the morning. Curiosity is an asset with a scheduling bug. The fix is not to read less. It is to _fence_ the reading — put a timebox on the question, a "done when" on the exit, and a parking lot beside the desk for everything wonderful and off-topic. Depth is then reached by **deriving within the fence**, not by wandering wider. The next section is the whole protocol.

**Measurement closes every loop.** Every module ends with one tool producing one number: a `valgrind` clean run, an `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` delta, a DevTools performance trace, a convergence demo, a `shellcheck` with zero findings, a memory reading. This is the SRE reflex applied to your own learning — and it doubles as evidence. A story with a number in it ("the generator version held peak memory flat while the list version blew past a gigabyte") lands; a story without one evaporates.

**Conversation is the deliverable.** The real output of the Gauntlet is not twenty-five folders. It is the ability to walk into three different rooms and hold your own in each: the **interview** (the ninety-second answer with a decision and a number), the **design review** (defending one choice against a named alternative, like a peer), and the **meetup** (the thirty-second "here's what I'm hacking on" with one hook). Every module drills all three. When you can do that across this surface, you are — by the only definition that matters — ready.

---

## The anti-rabbit-hole protocol

This is the load-bearing wall. Read it once; every module below just invokes it. Five parts.

**Timeboxes.** Every research target carries a clock. Default is **25 minutes**; targets flagged _deep_ get **45**. Hunting for your own sources counts as legitimate research inside the box — finding the material is part of the craft, and part of what you enjoy. The box governs the _question_, not the reading list. When it rings, you stop and answer the done-when with whatever you have. An eighty-percent answer now beats a perfect answer that eats the build block.

**Done-whens.** Every target ends with a capability, not a page count: _"Done when you can answer X out loud, tab closed."_ If you can't yet, the fix is one more focused pass at that exact question — not a wander into the next interesting thing. The exit is always a thing you can _say_, because saying it is the actual skill.

**The Parking Lot.** Keep a `parking-lot.md` open beside the work. Anything fascinating but out-of-fence gets **one line** — the question and a link — and then you move on immediately. You are not abandoning it; you are deferring it. Once a week, a 30-minute **parking-lot review** is where those lines get their day in court: read one, close it or re-park it, done. Parking is how curiosity gets honored _without_ getting to hijack the schedule.

**Rabbit-hole tells.** Learn to feel the pull before it's got you. The tells: you're three links from the question you started with; you're reading the source of a library you're not even using; you're fifteen minutes past the timebox; you just opened a second video before answering the first one's done-when. **The move is always the same:** write the parking-lot line, answer the current done-when with what you have, proceed. No exceptions, no "just one more."

**Fences.** Every module names its **Out of Scope** — the adjacent, genuinely interesting territory you are _not_ entering this time. The fence is not a judgment that the territory is unimportant. It is the thing that lets you finish. Everything past the fence goes in the parking lot by default.

---

## How every module is shaped

Eleven parts, the same every time, so you always know where you are:

1. **The story** — the module as a Jira-style ticket: the persona, the want, the why, an MVP, and acceptance criteria. It's the definition of done, stated before any theory.
2. **Briefing** — the concept and why it matters, in half a page or less, starting from zero. Every term is defined at first use; the jargon is earned, not assumed (see _Voice_, below). Points you at the anchor book in this project where one covers the ground.
3. **The trap** — the classic pitfall this feature walks straight into, named in two or three sentences, _without_ the resolution. You should recognize it when it bites.
4. **The feature** — requirements and contract only. Acceptance criteria written as observable behavior. No code.
5. **Decision map** — four to six named decisions. For each: the options (at least two, all live), the axis they trade off on, and two or three questions whose answers force the choice. Never a verdict. **Before you build, you write a short DSA** — your chosen option, your reasons, and one alternative you rejected and why. Commit it before you compare it to anything.
6. **Research targets** — each one a question · a timebox · a done-when · and zero-to-two _optional_ seed resources. You find your own sources; the seeds are just a nudge when a nudge helps. Some targets carry none on purpose.
7. **Out-of-scope fence** — plus the parking-lot reminder.
8. **Measure ritual** — one tool, one number, framed as a _question_ ("flat or linear when N grows a thousandfold?"), never as a target to hit.
9. **Hints ladder** — only for the one or two genuinely hard problems, direction-level, inside `<details>`. Three tiers: orient (name the concept or the analogous thing you already built) → approach (the shape, in prose) → near-spoiler (pseudocode, after two real attempts).
10. **The slow walk** — the module run as a sequence of small numbered steps, each with an objective, the questions to answer before touching the keyboard, and a **Check** that must pass before the next step begins. This is the research-guide spine: work it top to bottom.
11. **Prove it — the three rooms.** First a demonstration: run it, curl it, trace it, break it, and describe what you saw. Then the same build narrated three ways — **interview** (90 seconds: decision, tradeoff, the number), **design review** (one peer-level exchange: defend a choice against a named alternative), **meetup** (30 seconds: what you're hacking on, one hook).

Every build block ends with a **rubber-duck debrief**: two minutes, out loud, no notes — _what did I decide today, and why._ That's it. That habit is where "I built a thing" quietly becomes "I can talk about the thing."

---

## The threads — how the twenty-five connect

These builds are not twenty-five islands. Five storylines run through them, and part of the work is feeling a later module reach back and shake hands with an earlier one. The reach-back questions in the _Prove it_ sections are where this gets tested. Name them now so you can watch for them:

1. **The contract thread.** One resource contract — an `incidents` API — written once and then honored, reshaped, guarded, and consumed across ecosystems: Spring writes it (M1), FastAPI + Postgres re-serves it against a real database (M2), GraphQL bends it from a resource into a graph (M3), an auth layer guards it (M4), a mobile client consumes it (M23), and a WebSocket pushes a live version of it (M7). _The lesson: a contract is a promise that outlives any one implementation._
    
2. **The tree thread.** The same move — walk a hierarchy, know your base case — wearing three costumes: the recursive bill-of-materials that prices a product from its raw parts (M11), the search parser that turns `a AND (b OR c)` into a tree and evaluates it (M20), and the filesystem tool that descends nested folders to measure and reorganize them (M25). _The lesson: recursion is one idea; the domain is just paint._
    
3. **The time thread.** Data arriving _over time_, handled four ways: RxJS observables as the abstraction (M5), a WebSocket chat as the concrete bidirectional stream (M7), an IIoT telemetry feed turned into live analytics (M14), and MRP scheduling that walks _backward_ through time from a due date (M12). _The lesson: when time is a first-class dimension, everything gets harder and more interesting._
    
4. **The floor thread — "every convenience is a receipt."** This is your own _Machine Under Everything_ thesis, walked in both directions. C's manual memory is the floor under all the high-level comfort (M18); a game in C makes you live there (M19); and up at the top, pretext refuses to pay the DOM's reflow tax by measuring text itself (M16), which is the same instinct at a different altitude. _The lesson: the magic is always machinery, and you can choose whether to pay for it._
    
5. **The data-lifecycle thread.** Programs are temporary; data is permanent. Watch data move through its whole life: generated and modeled (M2, M11), migrated without loss (M10), queried and made fast (M2's finale, M9), and analyzed for meaning (M8, M9). _The lesson: the people trusted with the data are the last ones shown the door._
    

---

## The scheduling layer

The unit of planning is the **90-minute protected block** — your morning deep-work slot. Modules are sized in blocks:

- **S ≈ 2 blocks** · **M ≈ 3 blocks** · **L ≈ 4 blocks**

Roughly one block of research, one to two of building, half a block of _prove-it_. That's a guideline; a module you have prior reps in will run short, a genuinely new territory will run long, and that's information, not failure.

Twenty-five modules is a **campaign, not a sprint** — plan in months, not weeks, and treat each of the seven parts as a coherent stopping point that leaves you more hireable than the last. At four to five blocks a week you complete a part every couple of weeks; the full Gauntlet is a season of work. Two things make that scale humane:

- **Cherry-pick against live job descriptions.** The fastest path to your next role is the module that matches the posting on your screen this week. Let the market reorder the list.
- **The rep circuit runs underneath everything.** The algorithm-pattern work in M6 is not a one-time module — it's two timed problems at the _top_ of build blocks, spaced across the whole campaign, so recognition becomes reflex instead of a cram. Never batch it; always thread it.

Set your own weekly block budget and write it at the top of your `parking-lot.md`. That one number is the dial that turns this from a wish into a plan.

---

## Voice — the jargon ladder

Every briefing starts at the floor, even for subjects you half-know. Zero-start is cheap for you where you're strong and load-bearing where you're new, so it costs little and protects a lot. Then each one _climbs_: floor-zero definition → mechanism → the tradeoff vocabulary → the words a lead engineer actually uses in a design review. No jargon before its rung. By the time you reach _Prove it_, you are wielding vocabulary you were not assumed to have when the module opened — and the three rooms are where you prove you own it out loud, not just on the page.

That is the whole method. Now, the builds.

---

# PART I — APIs & Contracts

_Four modules, one shared artifact. In M1 you write a resource contract — an `incidents` API — down as a real document. Then you honor it in a second ecosystem with a real database (M2), bend it into a graph (M3), and guard it (M4). The contract is the through-line; write it once, well, because three modules hold it to account._

---

## M1 — One Honest Endpoint · Java Spring Boot → React · (M)

> **Story.** As an engineer re-activating my enterprise Java, I want one honest REST endpoint — validated, paginated, with a designed error shape — consumed by a typed React hook, so that the `incidents` contract exists as a written document that three later modules hold to account. **MVP.** `GET /api/incidents` with a status filter, pagination, structured errors, and a React view with distinct loading/empty/error states. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - A valid filter returns only matching incidents; the next page never repeats or skips a row.
> - A malformed parameter returns the documented error shape and status code — never a stack trace.
> - The React view shows visibly distinct loading, empty, and error states.
> - The contract exists as a standalone document before the code does.

**Anchors:** the Java book and _Data & Objects_ in this project; _Where the Data Lives_ Part 10 (ORMs and the object–relational mismatch); the function-first React/TypeScript guide.

### Briefing

A web API is a contract between two programs that agree never to meet. The server promises: "send me a request shaped like _this_, and I'll send back a response shaped like _that_, including when things go wrong." Spring Boot is the Java framework that has, for two decades, been the default way large institutions write that server — which is exactly why reactivating it is worth a module. The center of gravity here is not Spring's annotations; it's **the boundary**: what the outside world is allowed to see, what shape errors take, and how a client pages through more data than fits in one response. Get the boundary right and the framework is almost incidental.

### The trap

Two classics, and this feature walks into both. First, **entity leakage**: returning your database rows directly feels free and fast — until the first schema change silently reshapes your public API and breaks every client. Second, **the error shape nobody designs**: happy-path JSON is easy; it's the 400 and the 404 that reveal whether you thought about the contract at all, and most people don't until a frontend developer is yelling.

### The feature

One resource, one vertical slice, no persistence required (an in-memory list is fine — persistence is M2's job):

> `GET /api/incidents` returns a paginated list of incident records. It accepts filter parameters (at minimum a status filter) and a pagination parameter. It validates its inputs and rejects bad ones with a structured error. It is consumed by a typed React hook that renders loading, empty, and error states distinctly.

The contract you must **write down as a document** before coding (three later modules read it):

- The incident shape: `{ id, title, status, severity, createdAt }` with `status` a fixed enum (e.g. `OPEN | ACK | RESOLVED`).
- The list response envelope: the items, plus how the client asks for the next page.
- The error shape: one consistent JSON structure for every failure, carrying enough for a client to act.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- A request with a valid filter returns only matching incidents.
- A request for the next page returns the next slice, never a repeat or a gap.
- A request with a malformed parameter returns your documented error shape and a correct status code — not a stack trace.
- The React view shows three visibly different states for loading, empty, and error.

### Decision map

Write your DSA (chosen option + reasons + one rejected alternative) before building.

1. **Entity vs DTO at the boundary.** Options: expose the persistence object directly · map to a dedicated Data Transfer Object. _Axis:_ coupling vs boilerplate. _Forcing:_ When you rename a database column next month, what breaks for the client? Where would you put a computed field that has no column?
2. **Offset vs cursor pagination.** Options: `?page=3&size=20` · an opaque `?after=<cursor>`. _Axis:_ simplicity vs correctness under inserts. _Forcing:_ If a new incident is created between the client fetching page 1 and page 2, which scheme shows a duplicate or skips a row? Which one can't jump to "page 50" directly, and do you care?
3. **Error shape.** Options: the `application/problem+json` standard (RFC 7807) · your own envelope. _Axis:_ convention vs control. _Forcing:_ What's the minimum a client needs to distinguish "you sent bad input" from "that incident doesn't exist" from "I broke"? Does a field-level validation error fit your shape?
4. **Where validation lives.** Options: framework bean-validation annotations · hand-written checks in the handler. _Axis:_ declarative concision vs explicit control. _Forcing:_ What may the React client safely _assume_ has already been checked by the time data arrives?
5. **Client types: hand-written vs generated.** Options: write the TypeScript types by hand · generate them from an OpenAPI spec. _Axis:_ speed now vs drift later. _Forcing:_ When the server contract changes, how does the client find out — a red compile, or a runtime surprise in production?
6. **CORS in dev vs prod.** Options: permissive dev config · locked-down explicit origins. _Axis:_ convenience vs a real security boundary. _Forcing:_ What actually differs between the two environments, and what breaks if you ship the dev setting?

### Research targets

- _What is the object–relational mismatch, and why does a DTO exist?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain, tab closed, one concrete thing a DTO lets you do that returning the entity cannot. _Seed:_ Where the Data Lives, Part 10.
- _Offset vs cursor pagination — the failure mode of each._ — **25 min.** Done when you can draw the duplicate-row scenario on a whiteboard. _Seed:_ search "cursor pagination keyset" — read one primary source, not five blog rehashes.
- _What does RFC 7807 actually specify?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can name its required fields and decide whether you'll adopt it.
- _Bean validation basics in Spring._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name the annotation that rejects a null field and describe where the rejection surfaces.

### Out of scope (fence)

Authentication (that's M4). Real persistence and migrations (M2, M10). Rate limiting, caching headers, HATEOAS/hypermedia. Anything past a single resource. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

`curl` the happy path and three distinct failure paths against your written contract. **The number:** do all four responses' status codes and bodies match the document exactly? Then a one-line shell loop firing 100 requests — what's the rough p50 latency, and is it what you'd expect for an in-memory read?

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>Cursor pagination — I can't see how "next page" works without page numbers</summary>

**Orient:** the array you already index into has a natural ordering — think about what uniquely and monotonically identifies a row's position in that order.

**Approach:** instead of "skip N, take M," the client says "give me M rows _after this specific marker_." The marker is the last item the client already has. The server's job is to translate that marker into "everything ordered after it." Consider what makes a good marker when two incidents share a `createdAt`.

**Near-spoiler (after two attempts):** the marker is typically the sort key of the last row, made unique by appending the id as a tiebreak; the query becomes "where (createdAt, id) > (lastCreatedAt, lastId) order by the same, limit M"; the response hands back the new last marker for the _next_ call.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** Read the briefing and the trap only. _Ask:_ what concretely breaks for a client when an entity leaks through the boundary — and who designs the 400 body if nobody does? **Check:** both answered aloud in a sentence each.
2. **Research.** The four targets, in order, timeboxed. _Objective:_ every done-when closed with the tab closed.
3. **Write the contract.** _Objective:_ the document — incident shape, list envelope, error shape. _Ask:_ which lines are the contract, and which are Spring leaking in? **Check:** someone could implement it in another stack from the document alone (M2 will).
4. **Commit (DSA).** Close all six decisions in writing. **Check:** each has a chosen option, a reason, and a named rejected alternative.
5. **Build — happy path.** The list endpoint with filter and pagination, in memory. **Check:** two curls — a filtered list, then the next page with no repeat and no gap.
6. **Build — the boundary.** Validation, the error shape, then the typed React hook and its three states. **Check:** a malformed request returns your documented error, and the UI visibly cycles loading → data → empty → error.
7. **Measure & debrief.** The four-curl suite against the document, then the 100-request loop for a rough p50. _Ask (rubber duck, two minutes, no notes):_ what did I decide today, and why?

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** hit all four paths with curl; screen-record the React view cycling loading → data → empty (filter that matches nothing) → error (kill the server mid-request).

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a paginated incidents endpoint. The decision I'd flag is cursor over offset pagination — here's the duplicate-row scenario offset can't handle, and here's the tradeoff I accepted, which is losing random page access." Name your error-shape choice and why.
- **Design review:** a peer says "just return the entities, DTOs are boilerplate." Defend the boundary — or concede where they're right for a truly internal, single-consumer API.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I'm rebuilding my Spring reflexes by writing one really honest endpoint — the fun part turned out to be designing the error contract, which nobody teaches."
- **Reach-back (the contract thread):** you'll re-serve this exact contract on Postgres next. Which parts of your document are about the _contract_ and which leaked in from this _implementation_? The clean ones survive the port.

---

## M2 — The Same Contract, On Postgres · FastAPI + PostgreSQL · (M)

> **Story.** As a data-minded backend engineer, I want to re-serve the M1 contract from FastAPI on a real Postgres table, so that I prove the contract was real and learn to read the database's mind — plans, indexes, and the round trip. **MVP.** The same `GET /api/incidents`, backed by Postgres with one real join, plus one deliberately slow query made fast with an index. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - The M1 curl suite passes byte-for-byte on the contract.
> - The list includes related-table data (the service name) without an N+1.
> - You can produce the query plan before and after your index and name what changed.

**Anchors:** _Where the Data Lives_ (the whole SQL and indexing spine); the Python and data-science books; `Databases_and_DBMS_info.md`. Your M1 contract document is the primary spec.

### Briefing

Now the contract meets a real database. FastAPI is Python's modern, type-hint-driven web framework; PostgreSQL is the open-source relational database that has quietly become the default serious choice. Re-serving M1's `incidents` contract here does two things at once: it proves the contract was real (a good contract ports; a bad one turns out to have been Spring trivia in disguise), and it drops you onto the database floor where the data-lifecycle thread lives. The center of gravity is **the round trip**: an HTTP request becomes a SQL query becomes rows becomes JSON — and every layer is a place to be careless.

### The trap

The **N+1 query**: your list endpoint runs one query for the incidents, then — invisibly, one per row, inside a loop or a lazy relationship — another query for each incident's related data. Ten incidents, eleven queries; a thousand, a thousand and one. It works perfectly in development with three rows and falls over in production, and it is completely invisible in the Python source unless you watch the SQL.

### The feature

> Serve M1's `GET /api/incidents` contract — same shape, same envelope, same error structure — backed by a real Postgres table you design and load, with at least one related table (e.g. each incident belongs to a `service`) so the join is real. Then make it fast: write one deliberately slow query, read its plan, add an index, read the plan again.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The curl suite from M1 passes here **byte-for-byte on the contract** (the implementation differs; the promise doesn't).
- The list includes data from the related table (the service name), fetched without N+1.
- You can produce, on demand, the query plan before and after your index, and name what changed.

### Decision map

1. **Raw SQL vs an ORM vs a query builder.** Options: hand-written SQL · an ORM (SQLAlchemy) · a lightweight builder. _Axis:_ control and transparency vs safety and speed. _Forcing:_ Which one lets the N+1 trap hide from you most easily? Which one do you learn more from this month, and is "learn more" the right goal for _this_ endpoint?
2. **How you defeat N+1.** Options: a join · a batched second query (`WHERE id IN (...)`). _Axis:_ one round trip vs simpler queries. _Forcing:_ At what result-set size does the join's duplicated parent data start to cost more than the extra round trip saves?
3. **Index choice for the filter+sort.** Options: no index (seq scan) · a single-column index · a composite index matching your `WHERE` + `ORDER BY`. _Axis:_ write cost and storage vs read speed. _Forcing:_ Why might Postgres _refuse_ to use your index? What does putting a function around the column in your `WHERE` clause do to it? (This reaches straight back to M1's hash-map-vs-btree distinction — see M18.)
4. **Timestamp storage.** Options: naive local time · `timestamptz` (UTC with zone awareness). _Axis:_ simplicity vs never getting a 3am-during-DST bug. _Forcing:_ Two incidents created "at the same time" in two zones — are they equal in your storage?
5. **Where pagination happens.** Options: `LIMIT/OFFSET` in SQL · fetch-all-then-slice in Python. _Axis:_ pushing work to the database vs pulling it into the app. _Forcing:_ For a million-row table, which one loads a million rows into Python memory?

### Research targets

- _What is an N+1 query and how do you see it?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can turn on SQL logging and point at the eleven queries. _Seed:_ your ORM's echo/logging flag.
- _How do I read `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` output?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can find, in a plan, the difference between a Seq Scan and an Index Scan and say which is which and why one is faster here. _Seed:_ Use the Index, Luke — the "EXPLAIN" chapter.
- _btree vs hash vs partial indexes in Postgres — when each._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say why the default is a btree and when a partial index wins.
- _`timestamptz` vs `timestamp` — what's actually stored._ — **25 min.** Done when you can state what Postgres does to a value on the way in and out.

### Out of scope (fence)

Connection pooling internals, migrations (M10), async database drivers vs sync (note it, park it), full-text search, materialized views. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

`EXPLAIN ANALYZE` on the slow query, before and after your index. **The number:** the actual millisecond delta — and can you name the plan-node change out loud ("sequential scan became an index scan on the composite index")? A number without the _why_ doesn't count.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My index exists but the planner ignores it</summary>

**Orient:** an index is an ordering; the planner only uses it if your query asks for data in a way that ordering can serve.

**Approach:** look at three things — is your `WHERE`/`ORDER BY` on the exact columns, in the exact leading order, as the index? Is there a function wrapping the column (`lower(name)`) that the plain-column index can't match? Is the table so small the planner correctly judges a full scan cheaper? Rule those out in order.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ where in the request → SQL → rows → JSON round trip can an N+1 hide, and why won't the Python source show it? **Check:** you can name the layer you'll have to watch (the SQL log, not the code).
2. **Research.** The four targets; give `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` its full deep box. _Objective:_ you can tell a Seq Scan from an Index Scan in a real plan.
3. **Commit (DSA).** ORM vs raw SQL first — it decides everything downstream. **Check:** five decisions written, each with the rejected road.
4. **Build — schema and seed.** The incidents table plus a related `service` table, loaded with enough rows to matter. **Check:** a join query returns sensible rows in `psql`.
5. **Build — the contract.** Re-serve M1's endpoint. **Check:** the M1 curl suite passes byte-for-byte on the contract.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Turn on SQL logging and load the list. _Ask:_ how many queries fired, and why that number? **Check:** you've seen the N+1 with your own eyes before you kill it — then kill it and count again.
7. **Measure & debrief.** One deliberately slow query: plan, index, plan again. **Check:** you can say the millisecond delta and the plan-node change in one sentence. Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** run the M1 curl suite against this server and show it passing on the contract; show the two query plans side by side.

- **Interview (90s):** "Same contract as my Spring endpoint, on Postgres — the story is the N+1 I introduced on purpose and killed, and the index that took the slow query from _X_ to _Y_ milliseconds, which I can show you in the plan."
- **Design review:** defend raw SQL over the ORM (or vice versa) for this endpoint. What did your choice cost you, honestly?
- **Meetup (30s):** "I re-served the same API on Postgres just to prove the contract was real — and it was a great excuse to finally get comfortable reading query plans."
- **Reach-back (data-lifecycle thread):** this table becomes the source for the migration in M10 and, exported, the dataset behind the analytics work. What did you learn here about _shape_ that will make the migration harder or easier?

---

## M3 — The Contract, As A Graph · GraphQL + Node.js · (M)

> **Story.** As a full-stack engineer who has now served the same domain twice as REST, I want it as a GraphQL graph in Node, so that I feel the paradigm's tradeoff directly and meet its structural N+1 with a batching loader. **MVP.** A schema with `Incident` and `Service`, a filtered list query, one-request traversal to the service, N+1 solved. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - One GraphQL query fetches incidents and each one's service name in a single HTTP request.
> - Asking for `{ id title }` returns only those fields.
> - Service data for many incidents arrives in a bounded number of database hits (proven by logging).
> - A malformed query is rejected by the schema before any resolver runs.

**Anchors:** the function-first React/TypeScript guide; your M1/M2 contract as the thing being _reshaped_.

### Briefing

REST serves _resources_: you ask for `/incidents` and you get incidents, in a shape the server decided. GraphQL inverts that — the client sends a query describing exactly the fields and relationships it wants, and the server returns precisely that graph, no more, no less. It exists to solve two REST pains: over-fetching (the mobile client gets fifty fields to show three) and under-fetching (the client makes six round trips to assemble one screen). Building the _same_ incident domain as a graph, in Node, teaches the paradigm by contrast — you already know what the REST version cost. The center of gravity is the **schema as a type system** and the **resolver as the thing that fetches one field**.

### The trap

GraphQL makes N+1 _worse and easier to hit_ than REST did. Because each field can have its own resolver, a query for "100 incidents and each one's service" naively fires one resolver per incident for the service — the same N+1 from M2, but now structural and hidden inside the resolver graph. The standard defense (a batching loader) is a thing you must know exists, because without it a public GraphQL endpoint is a denial-of-service waiting to happen.

### The feature

> Expose the incident domain as a GraphQL endpoint in Node: a schema with an `Incident` type and a related `Service` type, a query that lists incidents with filter arguments, and the ability to traverse from an incident to its service in a single query. Solve the N+1 with a batching loader. Point one typed client query at it.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- A single GraphQL query can fetch incidents _and_ each one's service name in one HTTP request.
- Asking for only `{ id title }` returns only those fields — no over-fetch.
- Under a query for many incidents, the service data is fetched in a bounded number of database hits, not one-per-incident (you can prove this by logging).
- A malformed query is rejected by the schema _before_ any resolver runs.

### Decision map

1. **Schema-first vs code-first.** Options: write the SDL schema by hand, generate types · define types in code, generate the schema. _Axis:_ the schema as source-of-truth artifact vs staying in one language. _Forcing:_ Which produces a schema document your _frontend_ teammate can read without running your server?
2. **How you batch (defeat N+1).** Options: a per-request DataLoader that coalesces and caches · a join in the root resolver. _Axis:_ composability across arbitrary query shapes vs simplicity for known shapes. _Forcing:_ If a client can nest arbitrarily (`incidents → service → incidents`), which approach survives shapes you didn't anticipate?
3. **Error model.** Options: GraphQL's partial-success `errors` array (data _and_ errors together) · throw and fail the whole query. _Axis:_ resilience vs simplicity. _Forcing:_ If one field of one incident fails to resolve, should the client get the other 99 incidents or nothing? What does that mean for the loading states your React code writes?
4. **Pagination in a graph.** Options: the Relay cursor-connections spec (`edges`/`node`/`pageInfo`) · a simple limit/offset field. _Axis:_ ecosystem convention and tooling vs less ceremony. _Forcing:_ You already chose cursors in M1 for a reason — does that reason still hold here, and does the extra Relay structure earn itself for your scale?
5. **Guarding query cost.** Options: query depth/complexity limits · nothing (trust callers). _Axis:_ safety vs effort. _Forcing:_ What stops a client from sending a query nested twenty levels deep and melting your database? Is "it's internal" a real answer?

### Research targets

- _What problem does GraphQL solve that REST didn't?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can state over- and under-fetching with a concrete example from your own incident screen.
- _What is a resolver, and when does each one run?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can trace, for a two-level query, the order resolvers fire in.
- _What is DataLoader and what exactly does it batch?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain how "collect all the ids requested in one tick, then fetch them in one query" maps onto the resolver graph. _Seed:_ the DataLoader README — read _that_, not a tutorial rehash.
- _GraphQL error semantics — partial success._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe what a response with both `data` and `errors` means.

### Out of scope (fence)

Subscriptions (real-time GraphQL — note the overlap with M7, park it), federation/stitching, persisted queries, caching layers, mutations beyond the minimum. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Turn on query logging. Fire one GraphQL query for 100 incidents-with-services **without** the loader, count the database hits; add the loader, fire it again, count again. **The number:** hits-without vs hits-with. The drop from ~101 to a small constant _is_ the lesson.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>I understand DataLoader conceptually but not why it needs a "tick"</summary>

**Orient:** the batching only works if the loader can _wait_ for all the individual `.load(id)` calls to arrive before it fetches — so it needs a moment where it knows no more calls are coming this round.

**Approach:** think about the event loop from _Waiting, All the Way Up_. The loader schedules its actual fetch on the next microtask, so every synchronous `.load()` call fired while resolvers run in the current tick gets collected first, _then_ the one batched query goes out. The tick is the collection window.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what did over-fetching and under-fetching cost your own M1 incident screen, concretely? **Check:** one example of each, from your code, aloud.
2. **Research.** The four targets; DataLoader gets the deep box, and read its README, not a rehash. _Objective:_ you can trace resolver firing order for a two-level query.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Schema-first vs code-first, then the batching plan. **Check:** five written decisions.
4. **Build — schema and root query.** `Incident`, `Service`, a filtered list query. **Check:** a selective query returns only requested fields; a malformed one dies at the schema.
5. **Build — the traversal.** Incident → service in one request, naively. **Check:** it works — and the log shows the ugly hit count.
6. **Walk into the trap.** _Ask:_ exactly which resolver multiplies the queries, and what does the loader's "tick" collect? **Check:** loader in; hit count drops from ~N+1 to a small constant, logged.
7. **Measure & debrief.** The before/after hit counts are the number. _Ask:_ which paradigm made the client's job easier, and whose budget paid for it? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** run one query fetching selective fields (prove no over-fetch); show the hit-count before/after the loader.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built the incident domain as a graph to feel the REST/GraphQL tradeoff directly. The real story is the N+1 — GraphQL made it structural, and I fixed it with a per-request batching loader that took my hit count from 101 to 2."
- **Design review:** a peer wants GraphQL for a small internal service with one known client. Argue _against_ your own module — when is REST the right call and GraphQL over-engineering?
- **Meetup (30s):** "I rebuilt the same API three ways — REST twice, then as GraphQL — and the batching-loader trick was the thing that finally made GraphQL click."
- **Reach-back (contract thread):** you've now served this domain as a resource and as a graph. Which paradigm made the _client's_ job easier, and did that come out of the _server's_ budget?

---

## M4 — The Login That Holds · authentication flow with tokens · (M)

> **Story.** As someone on the Security+ track, I want a token login flow guarding my own incidents API, so that I understand what a token proves, where it lives, and what happens when it's stolen — from both sides. **MVP.** Register (safe password storage), login (issue token), protected routes, and a refresh path. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - A stored password is not recoverable from the database.
> - No-token, expired-token, and tampered-token requests are rejected distinguishably.
> - A valid login grants access; an expired access token renews via refresh without re-entering credentials.

**Anchors:** the networking primer in this project; _Where the Data Lives_ Part 14 (the security material); your Security+ track. Guards the M1/M2/M3 contract.

### Briefing

Authentication answers "who are you?"; authorization answers "what may you do?" — and confusing them is the source of a large share of real breaches. A token-based flow is how modern APIs remember a user across stateless requests: you prove who you are once (username + password), the server hands you a signed token, and you present that token on every subsequent request instead of your password. Building this against your own API is non-negotiable interview and job territory — nearly every backend role probes it. The center of gravity is **what a token is, what it proves, and what happens when it's stolen**.

### The trap

Two, tightly linked. **Storing the token wrong on the client**: put a session token in `localStorage` and any cross-site-scripting bug hands it to an attacker; the choice between storage locations _is_ a security decision, not a convenience one. And **treating a signed token as a secret** when it's actually just tamper-evident: a standard JWT is readable by anyone — signing stops forgery, it does not stop reading — so putting a secret in the payload leaks it to the client and beyond.

### The feature

> A login flow guarding your `incidents` API: a registration endpoint that stores a password _safely_, a login endpoint that verifies it and issues a token, protected routes that reject requests without a valid token, and a token-refresh mechanism so sessions can outlive a short-lived access token without asking for the password again.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- A stored password is not recoverable from the database (it's a slow one-way hash, not encryption, not plaintext).
- A request to a protected route with no token, an expired token, or a tampered token is rejected with the right status — and the three cases are distinguishable.
- A valid login returns a working token; presenting it grants access.
- An expired access token can be renewed via the refresh path without re-entering credentials.

### Decision map

1. **Session cookies vs JWTs.** Options: server-side session with a session-id cookie · stateless signed JWT. _Axis:_ easy revocation (delete the session) vs stateless scale (no server lookup). _Forcing:_ When a user clicks "log out everywhere," which design makes that trivial and which makes it genuinely hard? Which one are you actually storing on the server?
2. **Where the token lives on the client.** Options: `localStorage`/memory · an `HttpOnly` cookie. _Axis:_ XSS exposure vs CSRF exposure. _Forcing:_ Which storage is readable by injected JavaScript? Which one gets sent automatically and therefore needs CSRF defense? (There is no free option — name the threat you're accepting.)
3. **Password storage.** Options: a purpose-built slow hash (bcrypt/argon2) · a fast general hash · anything reversible. _Axis:_ resistance to offline cracking vs speed. _Forcing:_ Why is a _fast_ hash the wrong tool here? What does a "work factor" buy you as hardware gets faster?
4. **Access + refresh token lifetimes.** Options: one long-lived token · short access + longer refresh. _Axis:_ blast radius of theft vs how often the user is interrupted. _Forcing:_ If an access token leaks, how long is the attacker's window under each design? Where does the refresh token get stored, and does that reintroduce problem #2?
5. **Revocation strategy for stateless tokens.** Options: a short expiry and live with it · a server-side denylist of revoked tokens. _Axis:_ true statelessness vs the ability to kill a token _now_. _Forcing:_ You chose JWTs partly for statelessness — does a denylist quietly hand that back? Is that an acceptable trade for "log out everywhere"?

### Research targets

- _What is inside a JWT, and what does the signature actually protect?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can state, tab closed, why putting a secret in the payload is a mistake. _Seed:_ jwt.io — decode a sample and read its three parts.
- _Why bcrypt/argon2 and not SHA-256 for passwords?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain "slow on purpose" and what a work factor is.
- _The `localStorage` vs `HttpOnly` cookie debate — the real threat model._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name which storage each of XSS and CSRF attacks, and what defense pairs with each. _Seed:_ the OWASP cheat sheets on session and JWT handling.
- _Access/refresh token rotation — the pattern._ — **25 min.** Done when you can draw the sequence: access expires → client presents refresh → new access issued.

### Out of scope (fence)

OAuth2/OpenID Connect and third-party login ("Sign in with…"), multi-factor auth, password-reset email flows, rate-limiting the login endpoint (real, but note-and-park), full RBAC/permissions modeling. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Attempt each attack on your own endpoint: a tampered token, an expired token, a no-token request, and a look at the stored password in the database. **The number:** how many of the four are correctly refused/opaque? (Target is all four, but the point is _demonstrating_ each refusal, not asserting it.) Optionally, run one static-analysis pass over the auth code.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>Refresh tokens feel circular — if the refresh token can be stolen too, what's the point?</summary>

**Orient:** the two tokens have different jobs, lifetimes, and exposure surfaces on purpose; the win is in the _asymmetry_, not in either token being unstealable.

**Approach:** the access token is sent on _every_ request (high exposure) but dies fast (small window if stolen). The refresh token is sent _rarely_, only to one endpoint (low exposure), so it can live longer. You've shrunk the blast radius of the common case. Then consider refresh-token _rotation_ — issuing a new refresh token each time and invalidating the old — so a stolen-and-used refresh token gets detected.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what exactly does a signature on a JWT prevent — and what does it explicitly not prevent? **Check:** the sentence "signed, not secret" explained in your own words.
2. **Research.** The four targets; the storage-threat-model target gets the deep box. _Objective:_ XSS vs CSRF mapped to the storage each one attacks.
3. **Commit (DSA).** All five, in writing — the storage decision names the threat you're accepting. **Check:** you can say which attack you chose to live with, and why.
4. **Build — identity.** Register with slow-hash storage; login issuing a token. **Check:** the database row is opaque; a wrong password fails.
5. **Build — the guard.** Protect the incidents routes; distinguish no-token, expired, tampered. **Check:** three curls, three distinguishable refusals.
6. **Build — the refresh.** Short access token, refresh path, rotation if your DSA chose it. **Check:** an expired access token renews without credentials.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Attack yourself: tampered, expired, missing, and a look at the stored hash — four for four refused/opaque. _Ask:_ which of your five decisions would an attacker be happiest you'd botched? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** show the four refusals; decode your own JWT in front of yourself and confirm nothing secret is in it.

- **Interview (90s):** "I put a token auth flow in front of my incidents API. The decision I'd defend is short access tokens plus rotating refresh tokens — here's the theft blast-radius argument, and here's the statelessness I traded away to be able to revoke."
- **Design review:** a peer says "just use `localStorage`, it's simpler." Walk them through the exact XSS scenario, then say honestly when their choice is fine.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I finally built token auth from scratch instead of reaching for a library — the eye-opener was realizing a JWT isn't secret, it's just tamper-proof."
- **Reach-back (contract thread):** M9-style thinking — you'll attack your own code deliberately elsewhere. Which of your five decisions here is the one an attacker is happiest to find you got wrong?

---

# PART II — Change Over Time

_Three ways the browser keeps a UI in sync with data that won't hold still: reactive streams as the abstraction (M5), cached state under pressure (M6), and state pushed from a server in real time (M7). This is the time thread at browser altitude._

---

## M5 — Two Typeaheads · async/await vs RxJS · (M)

> **Story.** As a front-end engineer, I want the same typeahead built twice — async/await vs RxJS — against a slow, jittery endpoint I control, so that I meet the stale-response race and can argue, from experience, when Observables earn their weight. **MVP.** Two implementations, identical contract: debounce, loading/empty/error states, and provably no stale result. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Fast typing fires far fewer requests than keystrokes.
> - A staged race (earlier request returns later) never shows the earlier result.
> - Both versions present identical loading/empty/error behavior.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (the event-loop and Observable floors — this is the module that book was written to enable); the function-first React/TypeScript guide.

### Briefing

"Reactive programming" sounds like a paradigm you need a philosophy degree for. It isn't. It's one idea: instead of _asking_ for a value when you want it (pull), you _declare_ what should happen whenever a new value arrives (push), and something else runs your declaration each time. A stream of keystrokes, a stream of mouse moves, a stream of server responses — RxJS models all of them as an **Observable**, a thing you can filter, delay, and cancel with the same vocabulary. The way to learn whether it earns its complexity is to build the same feature twice — once with plain async/await, once reactive — and feel the difference in your hands. The center of gravity is **cancellation and time**: what a program does when answers arrive out of order.

### The trap

The **stale-response race**, and it is the single most instructive bug in async UI. You type "rea," a request goes out; you type "react," a second goes out; the _first_ request happens to be slower and returns _last_, overwriting your "react" results with "rea" results. The UI now shows answers to a question the user already changed. Every naive typeahead has this bug. Fixing it is the whole point, and the two implementations fix it in tellingly different ways.

### The feature

> The same typeahead search, built twice against one deliberately slow, jittery endpoint you control: **(a)** `fetch` + `AbortController` + async/await, with a debounce you write by hand. **(b)** RxJS: a keystroke stream through `debounceTime` → `distinctUntilChanged` → `switchMap` into the request. Identical user-facing contract: a loading state, an empty state, an error state, and — provably — never a stale result on screen.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Typing quickly fires far fewer requests than keystrokes (debounce works).
- A staged race — where an earlier request is artificially slower than a later one — never shows the earlier request's results.
- Both versions present identical loading/empty/error behavior to the user.

### Decision map

1. **Debounce vs throttle.** Options: debounce (wait for a pause) · throttle (at most one per interval). _Axis:_ fewest requests vs steady responsiveness. _Forcing:_ For a search box, do you want to fire _during_ fast typing or only after the user pauses? Which one would you pick for a scroll handler instead, and why is that different?
2. **How you defeat the race.** Options: _cancel_ the stale request (abort) · _ignore_ the stale response when it lands. _Axis:_ saving server work vs client simplicity. _Forcing:_ If you only ignore stale responses, did the server still do the work? If you abort, what has to be true about your fetch layer?
3. **The switchMap family (the classic interview question).** Options: `switchMap` · `mergeMap` · `concatMap` · `exhaustMap`. _Axis:_ what to do when a new input arrives mid-flight — cancel the old, run both, queue, or ignore the new. _Forcing:_ For a typeahead you want `switchMap`'s "cancel the old" — but can you say which of the other three you'd want for a "submit button that must not double-fire," and which for "upload these files in order"?
4. **Retry and backoff.** Options: no retry · retry with increasing delay. _Axis:_ resilience vs hammering a struggling server. _Forcing:_ If the endpoint fails once, should you retry immediately? What does exponential backoff protect?
5. **The verdict you must argue.** _When do Observables earn their weight over promises?_ This is not a decision I answer for you — it's the one you write a paragraph on after building both, and defend in the design-review room.

### Research targets

- _Push vs pull — what makes something "reactive"?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain, tab closed, the difference between calling a function for a value and declaring a reaction to a stream. _Seed:_ Waiting, All the Way Up — the Observables section.
- _`debounceTime`, `distinctUntilChanged`, `switchMap` — what each does to a stream._ — **25 min.** Done when you can narrate what happens to the keystroke stream at each stage. _Seed:_ rxmarbles.com — watch the marble diagrams move.
- _`AbortController` — how a fetch is actually cancelled._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what abort does to the in-flight request and how you wire it to a new keystroke.
- _switchMap vs mergeMap vs concatMap vs exhaustMap._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can match each to a real scenario without looking. _Seed:_ one good marble-diagram comparison.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full RxJS operator catalogue, Subjects and multicasting, schedulers, building your own Observable from scratch (fascinating — park it), state-management libraries. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Open the Network tab; type a word fast. **The number:** keystrokes vs requests actually sent (debounce working?), and cancelled-request count (race defense working?). Then stage the 3-seconds-then-1-second response race and confirm, on screen, that the stale result never wins.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My hand-rolled debounce fires anyway / fires twice</summary>

**Orient:** debounce is a timer that gets _reset_ on every new event; the bug is almost always in the reset, not the timer.

**Approach:** each keystroke must cancel the previously scheduled call before scheduling a new one. If you're storing the timer handle somewhere that resets between keystrokes (a fresh closure each render, say), you've lost the handle you needed to cancel. Think about _where_ the pending-timer reference has to live to survive across keystrokes.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ in your own words, what sequence of events puts wrong results on screen in the stale-response race? **Check:** you can draw the two-request timeline on paper.
2. **Research.** The four targets; the switchMap-family question gets the deep box and rxmarbles gets real time. _Objective:_ each operator matched to a scenario, no tab.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Debounce vs throttle, cancel vs ignore, and the operator choice. **Check:** written, with rejected alternatives.
4. **Build — the rig.** The slow, jittery endpoint you control. **Check:** you can dial latency per request — you'll need it to stage the race.
5. **Build — version (a).** fetch + AbortController + hand-rolled debounce. **Check:** keystrokes ≫ requests in the Network tab; staged race never shows stale results.
6. **Build — version (b).** The Rx pipeline: `debounceTime` → `distinctUntilChanged` → `switchMap`. **Check:** same two proofs, same user-facing states as (a).
7. **Measure & debrief.** Count keystrokes vs requests vs cancellations; run the 3s-then-1s race in both. _Ask:_ so when do Observables earn their weight? Write the paragraph — it's the module's real deliverable. Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** show request-vs-keystroke counts; run the staged race in both versions and show neither shows stale data.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a typeahead twice to compare async/await with RxJS. The core problem both solve is the stale-response race — here's the scenario — and `switchMap`'s cancel-the-previous behavior expresses it in one operator, which is the argument for Rx here."
- **Design review:** a peer says "RxJS is overkill, just debounce a fetch." Given your two working versions, when are they _right_? Name the complexity threshold where Observables start paying for themselves.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I finally get RxJS — I built the same search box with and without it, and the stale-request race is the thing that makes the reactive version worth the learning curve."
- **Reach-back (time thread):** the WebSocket chat in M7 is another stream. Which of these operators would help you handle a flood of incoming messages?

---

## M6 — The Cache You Can Explain · LRU in React · (M) · _hosts the rep circuit_

> **Story.** As an engineer drilling for interviews while building real tools, I want a genuinely O(1) LRU cache wired into my React app, so that I can whiteboard the two-structures-as-one trick and prove the constant-time claim with a benchmark — and open the rep circuit that runs under the rest of the campaign. **MVP.** Typed `get`/`put`, correct eviction, a flatness benchmark, and a React fetch hook that serves hits without the network. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - `get` returns and bumps recency; `put` beyond capacity evicts exactly the least-recently-used entry.
> - The benchmark stays flat from 10³ to 10⁶ entries.
> - A cache hit in the React app skips the network (visible in DevTools).

**Anchors:** `leetcode_thinking_toolbox.md` (the primary text — this module is where its pattern vocabulary goes live); `DATA_STRUCTURES___ALGORITHMS.md`.

### Briefing

A cache trades memory for time: keep recent answers around so you don't recompute or re-fetch them. But memory is finite, so a cache needs an _eviction policy_ — a rule for what to throw out when it's full. **LRU** (Least Recently Used) throws out whatever hasn't been touched in the longest time, on the bet that recently-used things will be used again. Building one that is genuinely O(1) — constant time for both read and write, no matter how full — is a rite-of-passage data-structures problem _and_ a real tool you'll drop into the React app to cache API responses. The center of gravity is **the two data structures working as one**: a hash map for instant lookup, a linked list for instant reordering.

This module also **hosts the rep circuit**: from here on, start build blocks with two timed problems (20–25 min) drawn from the toolbox's pattern vocabulary — two-pointer, sliding window, hashing, BFS/DFS, and the rest — spaced across the whole campaign, never crammed. The circuit is not extra; it's how recognition becomes reflex.

### The trap

The **O(1) claim that is secretly O(n)**. It's easy to build an LRU that _works_ and quietly does a linear scan to find the least-recently-used item, or to shuffle an array on every access. It passes every correctness test and is slow exactly when it matters. And in React specifically: JavaScript's `Map` preserves insertion order, which _tempts_ you to lean on that ordering and skip building the linked list — which teaches you nothing about why the data structure is shaped the way it is.

### The feature

> A generic, typed LRU cache with `get(key)` and `put(key, value)`, both O(1), evicting the least-recently-used entry when capacity is exceeded — plus a small benchmark proving the constant-time claim empirically. Then wire it into the React app as the cache behind a data-fetching hook (e.g. caching the incident lookups from M1).

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- `get` on a present key returns the value _and_ marks it most-recently-used.
- `put` beyond capacity evicts exactly the least-recently-used entry.
- The benchmark shows operation time staying flat as the cache grows from a thousand to a million entries.
- The React hook serves cache hits without a network request (visible in the Network tab).

### Decision map

1. **Exploit `Map` insertion order vs build an explicit doubly-linked list.** Options: lean on `Map` ordering (delete-and-reinsert to bump recency) · hand-build a DLL + hash map. _Axis:_ fewer lines vs understanding what's underneath. _Forcing:_ Which one could you _explain on a whiteboard_ in an interview? Which one hides the mechanism the interviewer is actually asking about? (For learning, the harder path is the point — but name the trade honestly.)
2. **Eviction API design.** Options: silent eviction · an eviction callback/event. _Axis:_ simplicity vs letting the caller react (e.g. to clean up). _Forcing:_ Does anything need to _happen_ when an entry is evicted, or is dropping it enough for your React use?
3. **Key equality and generics.** Options: string keys only · generic keys with a constraint. _Axis:_ simplicity vs reuse. _Forcing:_ If keys can be objects, what does "same key" mean, and does that reach back to the hashability problem from the hash-map thread?
4. **Invalidation in the React layer.** Options: never invalidate (pure LRU) · time-to-live on entries. _Axis:_ max hit rate vs never serving stale data. _Forcing:_ For cached incident data, is a five-minute-old answer acceptable? Who decides — the cache or the caller?
5. **Where the cache instance lives in React.** Options: module-level singleton · `useRef` per component · context. _Axis:_ sharing across components vs isolation and testability. _Forcing:_ Should two components fetching the same incident share a cache hit? What happens to a `useRef` cache on unmount?

### Research targets

- _Why does LRU need both a hash map and a linked list?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what each structure gives you and why neither alone is O(1) for both operations. _Seed:_ your toolbox doc's data-structure section.
- _What is a doubly-linked list and why doubly?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why you need the _previous_ pointer to remove a node in O(1).
- _How do you empirically test an O(1) claim?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe a benchmark that would _expose_ a hidden O(n).
- _Rep-circuit orientation (ongoing):_ skim the toolbox's pattern list and its recognition signals. Done when you can name the 8–10 patterns and one trigger phrase for each. _Seed:_ leetcode_thinking_toolbox.md.

### Out of scope (fence)

Other eviction policies (LFU, ARC, clock — note them, park them), thread-safety/concurrency, cache-stampede handling, a full React-Query-style fetching library. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Benchmark `get`/`put` at 10³ vs 10⁶ entries. **The number:** does per-operation time stay flat (O(1)) or climb (you've got a hidden linear scan)? Plus the rep-circuit scoreboard each session: solved cold / needed a hint / needed to look it up.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>I can do O(1) lookup or O(1) reordering, not both at once</summary>

**Orient:** you already met this shape — a hash map gives instant _find_, a linked list gives instant _splice_; the trick is making them point at the same nodes.

**Approach:** the hash map maps key → the _linked-list node_ holding that entry (not the value directly). Lookup finds the node instantly; because you hold the node, you can unlink and move it to the front instantly (that's why doubly-linked — you need its neighbors). Eviction removes the tail node, and — the step people forget — also deletes that key from the hash map.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why is a cache that scans for its victim still "correct" — and when would you ever notice it isn't O(1)? **Check:** you can describe the test that would expose it.
2. **Research.** The three targets plus the rep-circuit orientation. _Objective:_ you can say why neither a map nor a list alone gives O(1) for both operations.
3. **Commit (DSA).** The `Map`-ordering shortcut vs the explicit doubly-linked list — decide like it's an interview. **Check:** five decisions written.
4. **Build — the structure.** `get`/`put` with eviction. **Check:** a scripted sequence proves recency bumping and exact LRU eviction.
5. **Walk into the trap.** Benchmark 10³ vs 10⁶ entries. _Ask:_ flat or climbing — and if it climbs, which line of yours is the hidden scan? **Check:** flat curve in hand.
6. **Build — the wiring.** The React fetch hook backed by your cache. **Check:** second fetch of the same incident makes no network request.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Keep the flatness chart; start the rep-circuit scoreboard (cold / hinted / looked-up). _Ask:_ could you whiteboard the node-pointing-map diagram right now? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** show the flat benchmark curve; show a cache hit skipping the network in the React app.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an O(1) LRU with a hash map over a doubly-linked list. The subtle part is that the map points at list _nodes_, which is what makes eviction and recency-bump both constant time — and I benchmarked it to prove it stays flat at a million entries."
- **Design review:** a peer used the `Map`-insertion-order shortcut. Is it wrong? Discuss when the shortcut is fine and when you'd insist on the explicit structure.
- **Meetup (30s):** "Classic LRU cache, but I actually benchmarked mine to catch the sneaky O(n) version — then dropped it into a React fetch hook."
- **Reach-back (hash thread):** your cache's hash map is doing exactly what a database index does. Why does Postgres reach for a _btree_ index instead of a hash one for range queries? (You'll answer this properly around M2/M18.)

---

## M7 — The Room That Talks · WebSocket chat · (M)

> **Story.** As a full-stack engineer, I want a minimal WebSocket chat with presence and history, so that I own the concrete bidirectional stream under every "live" feature and the reconnect/broadcast decisions that come with it. **MVP.** Multiple clients, near-instant fan-out, presence, and history for late joiners; two browser windows are the demo rig. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - A message from one client appears near-instantly in the others.
> - Presence reflects connects and disconnects.
> - A new joiner sees what was said before them.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (the event-loop and async floors); the networking primer.

### Briefing

Ordinary HTTP is a series of one-shot exchanges: the client asks, the server answers, the line goes quiet. That's fine for fetching a page, useless for a chat, where the _server_ needs to speak first ("someone else just messaged you") without being asked. A **WebSocket** is a single connection, held open, that either side can send down at any time — a real two-way pipe instead of a call-and-response. Building a chat is the canonical way to learn it, and it teaches a distinction that reaches far past chat: **ephemeral state vs persistent state**. The center of gravity is **the persistent connection and what travels over it**.

### The trap

**Putting everything in the persistent store, including things that shouldn't persist** — and its cousin, forgetting connections die. "Who is currently online" and "who is typing" are _ephemeral_: true only while a connection lives, meaningless after it drops. Message history is _persistent_: it must survive disconnects and restarts. Conflate them and you get ghosts — users shown online forever because nobody cleaned up when their socket closed — and a presence list that grows without bound.

### The feature

> A minimal real-time chat: multiple clients connect over a WebSocket, messages sent by one appear near-instantly for all, a presence indicator shows who's currently connected, and message history persists so a new joiner sees what was said before them. Two browser windows are your demo rig.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- A message typed in window A appears in window B without a refresh.
- Closing window A removes that user from window B's presence list (no ghost).
- A window joining late sees prior message history; presence shows only the currently-connected.

### Decision map

1. **Raw WebSocket vs a library (Socket.IO et al.).** Options: the browser's `WebSocket` API directly · a library with reconnection/fallbacks. _Axis:_ learning the primitive vs production niceties for free. _Forcing:_ What does the library do for you that you'd otherwise hand-write — and is learning that hand-work the goal this month?
2. **Ephemeral vs persistent state — the split.** Options: keep presence in server memory, messages in a database · everything in one store. _Axis:_ correctness vs uniformity. _Forcing:_ When the server restarts, what _should_ vanish and what _must_ remain? Where does each kind of state therefore have to live?
3. **Message delivery guarantees.** Options: fire-and-forget · acknowledged delivery. _Axis:_ simplicity vs reliability. _Forcing:_ If a message is sent while a client is momentarily disconnected, is losing it acceptable for a chat? What would change your answer for, say, a trading app?
4. **Handling reconnection.** Options: ignore drops · detect and reconnect, re-sync missed messages. _Axis:_ simplicity vs real-network survival. _Forcing:_ Phones lose connection constantly — what does your UI show during a drop, and how does it catch up after?
5. **Broadcast scope.** Options: broadcast to everyone · rooms/channels. _Axis:_ trivial vs scalable structure. _Forcing:_ Does every message go to every connected client? At what point does that become a problem?

### Research targets

- _How does a WebSocket differ from HTTP, and how does the connection start?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain the upgrade handshake in one breath. _Seed:_ MDN's WebSocket page.
- _Ephemeral vs persistent state — where each lives._ — **25 min.** Done when you can classify presence, typing indicators, and history correctly and say why.
- _What happens on the server when a client's socket closes?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe the close event and what cleanup it must trigger to avoid ghosts.
- _Reconnection and missed-message re-sync — the pattern._ — **25 min.** Done when you can sketch how a reconnecting client asks "what did I miss?"

### Out of scope (fence)

Horizontal scaling across multiple server instances (the pub/sub-backplane problem — note it, park it), end-to-end encryption, typing indicators as a full feature, read receipts, WebRTC peer-to-peer. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Two windows. Send a message: **the number** is the visible latency to appearance (should feel instant). Then close one window and confirm presence updates in the other within a beat — no ghost. Optionally inject artificial latency and watch behavior.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>Ghost users — closing a tab doesn't remove someone from the online list</summary>

**Orient:** presence is only true while a connection is alive, so the list has to be maintained by connection _lifecycle events_, not by messages.

**Approach:** the server holds a live set of connected users. Two events mutate it: a connection opening (add), and a connection _closing_ (remove) — and it's the close handler people forget to wire. On any change, broadcast the new presence set. If tabs still ghost, your close event isn't firing or isn't finding the right user to remove.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what does HTTP's request/response shape make awkward that a socket makes natural — and what new failure modes do you inherit in exchange? **Check:** two of each, aloud.
2. **Research.** The module's targets, timeboxed. _Objective:_ you can narrate a WebSocket handshake and a broadcast without notes.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Message shape, history storage, reconnect policy. **Check:** written with rejected alternatives.
4. **Build — the room.** Server plus two browser windows exchanging messages. **Check:** a message in one window appears near-instantly in the other.
5. **Build — presence.** Joins and leaves visibly tracked. **Check:** closing a window updates the other's presence list.
6. **Build — memory.** History persisted; late joiners catch up. **Check:** a third window opened late sees the conversation.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Kill the server mid-chat and watch both clients; reconnect. _Ask:_ what did your reconnect policy silently promise about missed messages? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the two-window exchange; the clean presence update on disconnect; a late joiner seeing history.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a WebSocket chat, and the design decision I care about is the ephemeral/persistent split — presence lives in server memory and dies with the connection, history lives in the database and survives restarts. Conflating them is how you get ghost users."
- **Design review:** a peer put presence in the database "to be safe." Walk through why that's the wrong store, and when persistence of presence-like data _would_ be justified.
- **Meetup (30s):** "Real-time chat over WebSockets — the fun bug was ghost users lingering online forever, which turned out to be a lesson about what state deserves to persist."
- **Reach-back (contract thread):** this is your `incidents` domain going live — imagine pushing incident updates over this socket instead of chat messages. What in your M1 contract would need to change to become a _stream_ instead of a _fetch_?

# PART III — Data: Analysis & Migration

_Data at rest and data in motion between shapes. Analyze it in two languages (M8), do a research-grade analytics slice aimed at the kind of work the Penn role wants (M9), and move it from one schema to another without losing a row (M10). This is the back half of the data-lifecycle thread._

---

## M8 — Two Languages, One Question · data analysis in Python + R · (S)

> **Story.** As an analyst who wants range, I want one real question answered twice — pandas and tidyverse — to the same number, so that I can move between the two data dialects and trust a notebook only when it reproduces. **MVP.** Two notebooks, one dataset, one headline figure, each with a grouped aggregation and a chart. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Both notebooks, restarted and run top-to-bottom, produce the same headline figure.
> - Each includes a grouped aggregation and a chart that supports a claim in the prose.
> - A reader who knows neither dialect can follow the story from the markdown alone.

**Anchors:** the Python and data-science books in this project (the pandas/SQL material); `Databases_and_DBMS_info.md`.

### Briefing

A data-analysis notebook is a document that interleaves prose, code, and its output — you think in it, and the reader watches you think. Two languages own this space for different reasons: **Python** (with pandas) is the general-purpose workhorse that also does everything else; **R** (with the tidyverse) was built by statisticians for exactly this and often expresses a transformation more directly.
