Doing the _same_ analysis in both, in one project, teaches the shape of data work independent of either dialect — and being able to read both is a real edge, because data teams are mixed. The center of gravity is **the split-apply-combine pattern**: group the data, do something per group, stitch the results back — the move under almost every real analysis.

### The trap

The **notebook that doesn't reproduce**. Cells can be run out of order, and a notebook carries hidden state from every cell you ran and then deleted or edited. So a notebook can show a beautiful result that _cannot be regenerated_ from a fresh top-to-bottom run — the analysis equivalent of a lie. Restart-and-run-all is the only honest test, and most notebooks fail it the first time.

### The feature

> One real question answered over one real dataset (public or synthetic, but not toy-tiny), carried out **twice** — once in a Python/pandas notebook, once in R/tidyverse — reaching the same numeric answer. The analysis must include at least one group-by aggregation and one visualization, and the notebook must pass restart-and-run-all cleanly.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Both notebooks, restarted and run top-to-bottom, produce the same headline figure.
- Each includes a grouped aggregation and at least one chart that actually supports a claim in the prose.
- A reader who knows neither dialect can follow the _story_ from the markdown alone.

### Decision map

1. **pandas idiom vs tidyverse idiom for the same transform.** Options: method-chaining in pandas · pipe-and-verb in tidyverse. _Axis:_ which expresses _this_ transformation more legibly. _Forcing:_ Take your group-by — which language says it in fewer moving parts, and can you articulate _why_ (the tidyverse was built for this shape)?
2. **How the two languages meet.** Options: run them as fully separate notebooks · bridge (reticulate/rpy2) and call one from the other. _Axis:_ clean separation vs a single integrated document. _Forcing:_ Is the goal to _compare_ the dialects (keep them apart) or to _use each for its strength_ in one flow (bridge them)?
3. **Handling missing data.** Options: drop rows · impute · carry NULLs through. _Axis:_ simplicity vs not silently distorting the result. _Forcing:_ How does each language _represent_ missing (NaN vs NA), and does your aggregation silently skip or silently corrupt when it hits one?
4. **Where the transformation happens.** Options: pull raw data, transform in the notebook · push the heavy aggregation into SQL first. _Axis:_ notebook flexibility vs not loading a million rows into memory. _Forcing:_ This reaches back to M2 — at what data size does "load it all and group in pandas" stop being viable?
5. **Reproducibility discipline.** Options: trust cell order · enforce restart-and-run-all + pinned data. _Axis:_ speed of exploration vs a result you can stand behind. _Forcing:_ If a hiring manager ran your notebook fresh, would it produce your number?

### Research targets

- _What is split-apply-combine?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe the pattern and point to it in both `groupby` and `group_by`. _Seed:_ the data-science book's grouping chapter.
- _How does missing data differ between pandas (NaN) and R (NA)?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what each does inside a `mean()`.
- _Why do notebooks break reproducibility, and what's the fix?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain hidden state and name restart-and-run-all as the test.
- _(Optional) reticulate or rpy2 — how the bridge works._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say how a data frame crosses the language boundary.

### Out of scope (fence)

Statistical modeling and machine learning, interactive dashboards (that's more M12/M14 territory), big-data tooling (Spark, Dask — note, park), notebook-to-production pipelines. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Restart-and-run-all on both notebooks. **The number:** do both produce the identical headline figure from a cold start? (A mismatch means hidden state somewhere — find it.)

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ how can a notebook show a result that cannot be regenerated — where does the hidden state live? **Check:** you can name two ways a notebook lies.
2. **Research.** The targets, timeboxed. _Objective:_ split-apply-combine explained once, in neither dialect's syntax.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Dataset, the one question, and where each transform happens. **Check:** the question written as a sentence with a measurable answer.
4. **Build — Python.** Load, group, aggregate, chart, prose. **Check:** Restart & Run All passes; the figure supports the claim.
5. **Build — R.** The same question in tidyverse idiom — not a transliteration. **Check:** Restart & Run All passes.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Compare the two headline figures. _Ask:_ if they differ, is it missing-data handling (NaN vs NA), grouping, or something you assumed? **Check:** they match, and you know why they didn't at first.
7. **Measure & debrief.** The number is the match itself. _Ask:_ which dialect fought you, where, and what does each optimize for? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** cold-run both notebooks; show the matching number and the supporting chart.

- **Interview (90s):** "I ran the same analysis in pandas and tidyverse to stay fluent in both — the interesting part was seeing where R's verb pipeline reads more clearly than method chaining, and enforcing restart-and-run-all so the result is actually reproducible."
- **Design review:** a peer's notebook has a great result but won't cold-run. Explain the hidden-state problem without condescension and the one habit that fixes it.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been doing the same analysis in Python and R side by side — good way to feel what each language is actually _for_."
- **Reach-back (data-lifecycle thread):** if the dataset were a million rows, which step would you push back into SQL (per M2), and why?

---

## M9 — The Reproducible Result · research-data analytics (Penn track) · (M)

> **Story.** As a research-track analyst (the Penn lane), I want a pipeline from raw data to a defensible figure that anyone can regenerate with one command, so that my results carry provenance, not vibes. **MVP.** Ingest, documented cleaning, one grouped comparison, a chart, and a one-command reproduction path. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - A colleague on a clean machine goes from raw data to the final figure with a single documented command.
> - Every transformation and dropped record is documented with a why.
> - The headline claim survives a sanity check you designed to break it.

**Anchors:** the Python/data-science books; the honest-verb discipline (this module touches a domain you don't claim expertise in — the discipline matters here more than anywhere).

### Briefing

Research software engineering is a distinct craft: you support _scientists_ by turning their data and questions into reliable, reproducible analysis and tooling. The Penn role you applied to lives in computational pathology — a platform for annotating cells and tissue in medical images, where the tabular _outputs_ of imaging (measurements, annotations, labels) become datasets that must be analyzed carefully and reproducibly. You don't need to be a pathologist to do this well; you need to handle a domain scientist's data faithfully, make the analysis reproducible, and communicate it clearly. This module builds a representative slice of that work on a public or synthetic biomedical-style dataset. The center of gravity is **reproducible, honest analysis of data you didn't generate and don't fully own**.

An honesty note, because it's the whole point of the honest-verb discipline: this module makes you _fluent in the shape of the work_, not an expert in the domain. In conversation you say "I built a reproducible analysis pipeline over a representative dataset" — never "I have computational pathology experience." The distinction is exactly what keeps you credible in the interview.

### The trap

**The result that can't be traced back to the data.** In research, a number is worthless if you can't say precisely which inputs and which steps produced it — and worse, subtle data issues (a mislabeled category, a unit mismatch, a filtered-out subgroup) can produce a _plausible_ wrong answer that survives right up until someone tries to reproduce or build on it. The trap is confident output over untrustworthy lineage.

### The feature

> A reproducible analysis pipeline over a representative dataset (e.g. a public dataset of image-derived measurements, cell counts, or clinical-style tabular records): ingest the raw data, document and handle its quirks, compute a defensible summary result with at least one grouped comparison, visualize it, and package it so a colleague can regenerate the exact result from the raw input with one command.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- A colleague (or you, on a clean machine) can go from raw data to your final figure with a single documented command.
- Every transformation and every dropped/filtered record is documented with a _why_.
- The headline claim is supported by a chart and survives a sanity check you designed to try to break it.

### Decision map

1. **Notebook vs script pipeline.** Options: a narrative notebook · a parameterized script/pipeline. _Axis:_ exploratory storytelling vs one-command reproducibility. _Forcing:_ Who's the audience — a reader following your reasoning, or a colleague who needs to _rerun_ it on new data? Can you get both?
2. **Environment reproducibility.** Options: "works on my machine" · pinned dependencies (lockfile/container). _Axis:_ speed now vs regenerable next year. _Forcing:_ If someone runs this in six months with newer library versions, does your number survive? What's the cheapest way to guarantee it?
3. **Data provenance.** Options: transform in place · keep raw immutable, derive everything. _Axis:_ convenience vs traceability. _Forcing:_ Can you point at any figure and name every step from raw input to it? What did you _remove_ from the data, and would a reviewer agree with each removal?
4. **How you handle domain quirks you don't fully understand.** Options: guess and proceed · document assumptions explicitly and flag them. _Axis:_ speed vs honesty. _Forcing:_ When a column's meaning is ambiguous, do you silently assume, or surface the assumption for a domain expert to correct? Which behavior gets you hired onto a research team?
5. **Sanity-check design.** Options: trust the pipeline · build a check that would catch a plausible error. _Axis:_ faster vs trustworthy. _Forcing:_ What's a wrong-but-plausible result this analysis could produce, and what check would catch it?

### Research targets

- _What does reproducible research actually require?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name the layers (data, environment, code, one-command run) and why each matters. _Seed:_ search "reproducible research checklist" — read one authoritative source.
- _What is data provenance and why does research live or die on it?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain tracing a result to its inputs.
- _Representative domain framing:_ skim what computational-pathology annotation data looks like as tabular output. — **25 min.** Done when you can describe, honestly and roughly, the _kind_ of data the Penn platform produces — without overclaiming expertise. _(Parking-lot everything deeper; this is orientation, not a rabbit hole.)_
- _(Optional) one-command reproducibility tooling_ (Make, a simple pipeline runner). — **25 min.** Done when you can wire raw→result behind a single target.

### Out of scope (fence)

Actual medical/clinical interpretation, statistical significance testing beyond the basics, image processing itself (you're working with derived tables, not pixels — note, park), ML modeling. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

On a clean checkout, run your one command. **The number:** does it regenerate the exact final figure from raw data, unattended? Reproducibility is binary here — it does or it doesn't.

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what makes a number defensible in research — what question must you be able to answer about every figure? **Check:** "which inputs, which steps" recited with a straight face.
2. **Research.** The targets; provenance gets your attention. _Objective:_ you can define reproducibility as a property of the pipeline, not the person.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Notebook vs script pipeline, environment pinning, quirk policy. **Check:** written DSA, plus the sanity check you'll later use to attack your own claim.
4. **Build — ingest and clean.** Raw data untouched in `data/raw/`; every drop and transform logged with a why. **Check:** the quirk log is honest and non-empty.
5. **Build — the analysis.** The grouped comparison and its chart. **Check:** the figure regenerates from raw with one command.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Run your pre-designed sanity check against your own result. _Ask:_ what would this look like if I were wrong? **Check:** the claim survives, or the pipeline is fixed and rerun.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Clean-machine (or clean-clone) reproduction, one command, same figure. _Ask:_ which step would a skeptical reviewer poke first? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** clean-machine one-command regeneration; walk the provenance from raw file to final chart.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a reproducible analysis pipeline over a representative research dataset — one command takes raw data to the final figure, every filtering step is documented, and I built a sanity check to catch a plausible wrong answer. I'd be careful to say this is the _shape_ of research-data work, not domain expertise I'm claiming."
- **Design review:** a peer's analysis isn't reproducible and drops rows without explanation. Raise it the way you'd want it raised on a research team — collaboratively, provenance-first.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been practicing research-software-engineering habits — the whole game is that someone else can regenerate your exact result from the raw data."
- **Reach-back (honest-verb thread):** write the one sentence you'd say about this project to a hiring manager. Does it claim exactly what you did and nothing more?

---

## M10 — The Migration That Fights Back · a data migration with real snags · (M)

> **Story.** As the engineer who wants to be trusted with the data, I want to run a migration that real-world mess actively resists — and be able to roll it back — so that I've earned the war story every senior data person has. **MVP.** A backfilled new required column plus a constraint the messy data violates, handled; forward idempotent, rollback working. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - The migration succeeds against the messy dataset; every offending row is fixed, quarantined, or rejected with a logged reason — never silently dropped.
> - Running the forward migration twice does not corrupt or double-apply.
> - The rollback returns a working prior state; the new required column is correctly backfilled.

**Anchors:** _Where the Data Lives_ Part 8 (migrations — the book flags this as the skill interviews probe hardest); your M2 Postgres table as the source.

### Briefing

A data migration changes the shape of data that already exists — and often, data that is _live_, being read and written while you change it. This is categorically harder than designing a schema fresh, because you inherit every mess the old schema allowed: nulls where you now need values, duplicates where you now need uniqueness, three spellings of the same category, encodings from a decade ago. Experienced engineers respect migrations the way sailors respect weather. Doing one with deliberate snags — the kind that only bite on real data — is how you earn that respect before a production incident teaches it to you. The center of gravity is **changing data safely while it's in use, and being able to undo it**.

### The trap

**The migration that passes on a clean dev copy and detonates on production data.** Your three test rows are tidy; the ten-year-old table has a row with a null in the column you're about to make `NOT NULL`, a duplicate in the column you're about to make unique, and a value that violates the foreign key you're adding. The migration ran green in staging and takes down production at 2am. Its evil twin: **the irreversible migration** — you dropped the old column, the new logic has a bug, and there is no way back.

### The feature

> Take your M2 `incidents` table (or a copy), seed it with _deliberately messy_ real-world-style data, then perform a non-trivial migration end to end: a schema change that requires backfilling a new required column from existing data, plus a change that the messy data _violates_ — and handle it. Produce both a forward migration and a working rollback, and make the forward migration _idempotent_ (safe to run twice).

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The migration succeeds against the _messy_ dataset, not just a clean one — every offending row is handled (fixed, quarantined, or explicitly rejected with a logged reason), not silently dropped.
- Running the forward migration a second time does not corrupt or double-apply.
- The rollback returns the database to a working prior state.
- A new required column is correctly backfilled from existing data.

### Decision map

1. **In-place vs expand-contract (parallel-change) migration.** Options: alter the column directly · add new alongside old, backfill, switch reads, then drop old. _Axis:_ simplicity vs zero-downtime safety and reversibility. _Forcing:_ If the app is _running_ during the migration, which approach lets old and new code coexist? Which one can you undo after you've dropped the old column? (You can't — that's the point of expand-contract.)
2. **What to do with rows that violate the new constraint.** Options: fail the whole migration · fix them programmatically · quarantine them for review · reject with logged reasons. _Axis:_ strictness vs progress vs data loss. _Forcing:_ Is silently dropping a bad row ever acceptable? Who needs to _know_ a row was bad?
3. **Backfill strategy for a large table.** Options: one big `UPDATE` · batched updates. _Axis:_ simplicity vs locking the table and blocking the live app. _Forcing:_ What does a single `UPDATE` over ten million rows do to everyone else trying to use the table meanwhile?
4. **Idempotency.** Options: assume it runs once · make it safe to re-run. _Axis:_ less code vs surviving a mid-migration crash. _Forcing:_ If the migration dies halfway and you rerun it, does it resume cleanly or double-apply? Which failure would you rather debug at 2am?
5. **Reversibility.** Options: forward-only · paired rollback. _Axis:_ speed vs a safety net. _Forcing:_ When the new code has a bug in production, what's your move if there's no rollback? What data would a rollback _lose_ if writes happened in between?

### Research targets

- _What is the expand-contract (parallel-change) migration pattern?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can narrate the five phases and say which ones can coexist with running code. _Seed:_ Where the Data Lives, Part 8.
- _Why can a single big `UPDATE` take down a live app?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain locking/blocking and why batching helps.
- _What makes a migration idempotent?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe a check that makes re-running safe.
- _Backfilling a `NOT NULL` column on an existing table — the safe sequence._ — **25 min.** Done when you can order the steps so the constraint is never violated mid-flight.

### Out of scope (fence)

Cross-database migrations (Postgres→something else), migration frameworks' internals, online schema-change tools (gh-ost, pt-osc — note, park), data warehousing ETL at scale, blue-green deployment mechanics. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run the forward migration against the messy dataset; then run it _again_. **The number:** did it handle every offending row (count them — fixed/quarantined/rejected, zero silently lost) and survive the second run unharmed? Then run the rollback and confirm a working prior state.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>I can't make the column NOT NULL — existing rows have nulls</summary>

**Orient:** you can't add the constraint until the data satisfies it, so the constraint is the _last_ step, not the first.

**Approach:** the safe order is add-the-column-nullable → backfill the nulls with a real value (computed from other columns, or a documented default) → _verify_ no nulls remain → _then_ add the `NOT NULL` constraint. If you can't compute a sensible value for some rows, that's a decision (default? quarantine? reject?), not an error to paper over.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why does a migration that passed on dev detonate on production data — what specifically differs? **Check:** three concrete examples of "messy" named before you seed them.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ expand-contract explained as a sequence, and "idempotent" defined without the word "again".
3. **Commit (DSA).** In-place vs expand-contract, the violating-rows policy, backfill strategy. **Check:** written, including what "handled" means per bad row.
4. **Build — the mess.** Seed the copy with the nulls, duplicates, and orphans your plan fears. **Check:** the mess provably violates the target constraints.
5. **Build — forward.** The backfill plus the constraint change, handling every offender. **Check:** it succeeds on the messy data; the quarantine/fix log accounts for every bad row.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Run the forward migration a second time. _Ask:_ what would double-apply if you hadn't guarded it? **Check:** second run is a no-op.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Roll back; verify the prior state works; forward again. _Ask:_ what would you do differently on a table 1000× this size, live? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** migrate the messy data (show the offending-row handling), rerun for idempotency, roll back to a working state.

- **Interview (90s):** "I ran a migration against deliberately messy data — the real lesson was expand-contract, because it's the only pattern that stays reversible and coexists with running code. I backfilled a new required column in the safe order and made the whole thing idempotent so a mid-run crash is survivable."
- **Design review:** a peer wrote a forward-only migration with one big `UPDATE`. Name the two production risks and the safer shapes, as a colleague who's been burned.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been practicing migrations against intentionally dirty data — turns out the hard part isn't the schema change, it's the ten years of mess the old schema allowed."
- **Reach-back (data-lifecycle thread):** the data you migrated started life shaped by M2's design decisions. Which of those decisions made this migration easier or harder? (That's the argument for getting schema right early.)

---

# PART IV — Supply-Chain Systems

_Four modules that compose into a mini manufacturing system, and a domain that hires steadily and technically. Build the part hierarchy (M11), the ordering brain (M12), the shop-floor workflow (M13), and the machine-monitoring feed (M14). Together they're a tour of MRP/MES software — the software that runs factories. Each stands alone, but they interlock: M11's lead times feed M12; M13 tracks the orders M12 says to build; M14 watches the machines doing the building._

---

## M11 — The Infinite BOM Engine · recursive data modeling · (M)

> **Story.** As a supply-chain systems engineer, I want a self-referential components schema and a traversal that prices a product from its raw parts, so that recursion stops being a leetcode trick and becomes how I model the world — cycles included. **MVP.** The given `Components` schema; total cost and total build time for a top-level item, respecting quantities and parallel subassemblies. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Multi-level cost rolls up quantities correctly at every level (2 trucks × 4 wheels = 8 wheels' cost).
> - A shared subcomponent (diamond) is counted correctly, not doubled or dropped.
> - A cyclic BOM is detected and refused, not looped on forever.

**Anchors:** _Where the Data Lives_ (schema design, recursive queries); the tree material implied across the DSA docs.

### Briefing

A **bill of materials** (BOM) is the recipe for a manufactured product: a skateboard is a deck plus trucks plus wheels; the deck is wood plus glue plus grip tape; and so on, down to raw materials. This is a _tree_ (sometimes a graph): parts contain parts contain parts. Storing and pricing it is the foundational problem under all MRP and ERP software, because everything else — cost, scheduling, purchasing — starts from "what is this thing actually made of, all the way down?" The center of gravity is **hierarchical data and tree traversal**, the same move as M20's parser and M25's filesystem walk.

### The trap

**The cycle that never terminates.** A BOM should be acyclic — nothing contains itself — but nothing in a naive schema _enforces_ that, and one bad data entry (part A lists part B, part B lists part A) turns your recursive cost calculation into an infinite loop and a stack overflow. Its quieter cousin: the **diamond**, where one component is used by two different subassemblies, which a careless traversal double-counts or under-counts.

### The feature

> A self-referential schema and a traversal that prices a product from its raw parts. The contract (this is a schema, so it's a spec you may take as given):
> 
> ```
> Components(
>   id            primary key,
>   name          text,
>   parent_id     foreign key → Components.id,   -- the self-reference
>   cost          numeric,      -- cost of this component itself
>   lead_time_days integer,     -- days for this component to arrive
>   quantity_required integer   -- how many of this go into its parent
> )
> ```
> 
> Given a top-level item id, produce its **total cost** and **total build time from raw materials** — traversing the whole tree, multiplying by quantities, and (for time) respecting that subassemblies can be built in parallel but a parent can't start until its children exist.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Total cost of a multi-level product correctly rolls up quantities at every level (2 trucks × 4 wheels each = 8 wheels' worth of cost).
- A diamond (shared subcomponent) is counted correctly, not doubled or dropped.
- A cyclic BOM is _detected and refused_, not looped on forever.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Recursion in the database vs recursion in application code.** Options: a recursive SQL query (`WITH RECURSIVE`) · a recursive function in your backend language. _Axis:_ one query, database-optimized vs debuggable, language-native logic. _Forcing:_ Which one can you _step through in a debugger_? Which one avoids pulling the whole tree into app memory? Does the answer change if the tree is enormous?
2. **Cycle detection.** Options: trust the data · track the visited path and detect a repeat. _Axis:_ simplicity vs not crashing on bad data. _Forcing:_ What do you carry through the recursion to know you've seen a node before? Where in the descent do you check?
3. **Cost roll-up semantics.** Options: sum children only · sum children × quantity-required at each edge. _Axis:_ naive vs correct. _Forcing:_ If a deck needs 2 trucks and each truck needs 4 wheels, how many wheels' cost is in the skateboard, and does your multiplication happen at the right level?
4. **Time roll-up (this is subtle).** Options: sum all lead times · take the _critical path_ (longest dependency chain), since independent subassemblies proceed in parallel. _Axis:_ wrong-but-easy vs how manufacturing actually works. _Forcing:_ If the wheels take 5 days and the deck takes 10 days and they're made in parallel, is the total 15 or 10? What does that make the "total time" — a sum or a max-of-paths?
5. **Handling depth.** Options: unbounded recursion · a depth guard. _Axis:_ simplicity vs protection against pathological trees. _Forcing:_ Even acyclic, could a maliciously deep tree blow your stack? (Ties to M18 — the call stack is finite.)

### Research targets

- _What is a self-referential foreign key and how does it model a tree?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can draw the skateboard→deck→wood chain as rows pointing at rows.
- _Recursive CTEs (`WITH RECURSIVE`) — how they work._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain the anchor-plus-recursive-term structure. _Seed:_ Postgres docs on recursive queries.
- _Cycle detection in graph traversal._ — **25 min.** Done when you can state what "visited set" or "path stack" buys you.
- _Critical path vs total sum for parallel work._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why parallel subassemblies make total time a max, not a sum.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full ERP concepts, versioned/effective-dated BOMs (real in industry — note, park), phantom assemblies, cost accounting nuance, the actual purchasing (that's M12). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Price a known multi-level product by hand, then run your engine. **The number:** does the computed total cost and critical-path time match your hand calculation exactly? Then feed it a cyclic BOM — does it refuse rather than hang?

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My cost roll-up is off — quantities aren't multiplying right</summary>

**Orient:** the quantity lives on the _edge_ (how many of the child go into the parent), so it must multiply the child's _entire subtree cost_, not just the child's own cost.

**Approach:** think of each node returning its fully-rolled-up cost. A parent's contribution from a child is `quantity_required × (child's total subtree cost)`. If you're only multiplying by the child's own `cost` and ignoring the child's children, the multiplication is happening at the wrong level.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what stops `parent_id` from pointing in a circle — and what does your traversal do when it does? **Check:** the honest answer ("nothing, and it hangs") said aloud.
2. **Research.** The four targets; recursive CTEs get real attention even if your DSA picks app-side recursion. _Objective:_ critical path vs total sum distinguished with the truck-and-wheels example.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Database vs application recursion, cycle policy, roll-up semantics. **Check:** written, with the time-vs-cost asymmetry named (sum vs max).
4. **Build — schema and seed.** The given `Components` table plus one multi-level product including a shared (diamond) part. **Check:** the seed data draws as the tree you intended.
5. **Build — cost roll-up.** Total cost with quantity multiplication at every level. **Check:** the hand-computed truck number matches; the diamond counts correctly.
6. **Build — time roll-up.** Build time respecting parallel children. **Check:** the answer is the longest path plus assembly, not the sum of everything.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Insert the A-contains-B-contains-A row. **Check:** detection and refusal, not a hang. _Ask:_ where does your defense live — schema, query, or code — and why there? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** hand-check vs engine on a 3-level product; feed it a cycle and show the refusal.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a recursive BOM cost engine on a self-referential schema. Two things I'd flag: cost roll-up has to multiply quantity by the _whole_ subtree, and total build time is the critical path, not a sum, because subassemblies run in parallel — plus cycle detection so bad data can't loop forever."
- **Design review:** a peer used recursive SQL; you used app-code recursion (or vice versa). Defend your choice on debuggability _and_ memory.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I built the guts of ERP software — a recursive bill-of-materials engine — and the fun subtlety was that build time is a longest-path problem, not addition."
- **Reach-back (tree thread):** this is the same traversal as the search parser (M20) and the filesystem walk (M25). What's identical across all three, and what's just domain paint?

---

## M12 — The Time-Phased MRP Calculator · the core scheduling algorithm · (M)

> **Story.** As a supply-chain systems engineer, I want the core MRP move — net demand, then schedule backward from the due date — as a small pure engine, so that I own the calculation that ERP systems are wrapped around. **MVP.** The given input/output contract: gross-to-net against inventory, order date = due date minus lead time, late orders flagged. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Demand 50 with 10 in stock orders 40, not 50; zero net demand yields no order.
> - The order date equals due date minus lead time.
> - An order date landing in the past is flagged late, not silently emitted.

**Anchors:** _Where the Data Lives_ (data modeling); M11's `lead_time_days` is an input here.

### Briefing

This is the literal heartbeat of a manufacturing system. **MRP** (Material Requirements Planning) answers: given a customer order due on a date, and given how long each material takes to arrive, _when must I order each material_ so everything converges on time? It works _backwards_ through time from the due date, subtracting lead times, while accounting for what you already have in stock. Mastering time-phasing and inventory allocation makes you immediately employable in supply-chain tech, because this logic is the same in a garage and in SAP. The center of gravity is **backward scheduling through time, netted against inventory** — the time thread, run in reverse.

### The trap

**Gross-versus-net, and the calendar.** The naive version orders the full demanded quantity, forgetting you have some in stock (ordering 50 when you have 10 and need 40 wastes money and space). And "subtract 5 days" is a landmine: subtract days naively and you'll schedule an order for a weekend or a holiday when no supplier ships, or go negative past today — meaning the order is already _late_ the moment it's placed, which the system must flag, not hide.

### The feature

> A calculation engine that takes demand and works backward to purchasing instructions. The contract:
> 
> ```
> Input:   [ { item: "Deck", quantity: 50, due_date: "2026-10-15" } ]
> Inventory (given): a table of on-hand quantities, e.g. { "Deck": 10 }
> Lead times (given): from M11, e.g. Deck lead_time_days = 5
> Output:  [ { order_item: "Deck", order_quantity: 40, order_by_date: "2026-10-10" } ]
> ```
> 
> The engine must: net demand against inventory (gross-to-net), then subtract the lead time from the due date to get the order date (backward scheduling), and flag any order whose computed order date is in the past (already late).

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Demand of 50 with 10 in stock yields an order quantity of 40, not 50.
- The order date equals due date minus lead time.
- An order whose date lands before today is flagged as late, not silently emitted.
- Zero net demand (enough stock) yields _no_ order, not an order for zero.

### Decision map

1. **Working days vs calendar days for lead time.** Options: subtract raw calendar days · subtract business days (skip weekends/holidays). _Axis:_ simplicity vs real-world correctness. _Forcing:_ If lead time is 5 days and the due date is a Wednesday, does your order land on the prior Friday or the prior Thursday — and which is right if suppliers don't ship weekends?
2. **Netting policy.** Options: net against current stock only · account for already-inbound orders too. _Axis:_ simple vs realistic. _Forcing:_ If you already ordered 20 last week that haven't arrived, should today's calculation count them? What's double-ordering cost you?
3. **Multi-level explosion.** Options: single-item · explode through the BOM (ordering a deck means ordering its wood, timed to its _own_ lead time). _Axis:_ toy vs real MRP. _Forcing:_ This reaches into M11 — if building a deck itself takes time and needs wood, when must the _wood_ be ordered relative to the deck's order date?
4. **The late-order signal.** Options: silently emit a past date · flag/raise it. _Axis:_ dangerous vs safe. _Forcing:_ An order dated in the past means you _cannot_ meet the due date — is hiding that ever acceptable? What should the system tell the human?
5. **Output shape.** Options: flat order list · a time-phased plan (what to order, when, why). _Axis:_ minimal vs auditable. _Forcing:_ Can a planner look at your output and understand _why_ each order date was chosen?

### Research targets

- _What is time-phasing and backward scheduling in MRP?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain working backward from a due date through lead times, out loud.
- _Gross-to-net logic._ — **25 min.** Done when you can state the netting formula and what "net requirement" means.
- _Business-day / calendar arithmetic — the pitfalls._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name why naive day subtraction breaks on weekends and how a working-calendar fixes it.
- _(If doing explosion) BOM explosion with lead-time offsetting._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can describe how a parent's order date sets its children's due dates.

### Out of scope (fence)

Lot sizing (economic order quantity), safety stock, capacity planning (that's a different algorithm), forecasting demand, the full MRP-II scope. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run the given example. **The number:** does it output exactly "order 40 Decks by 2026-10-10"? Then push the due date close to today and confirm the late-order flag fires when the math demands it.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>Multi-level timing confuses me — when do I order the wood?</summary>

**Orient:** each level's _order date_ becomes the _due date_ for the level below it — the wood must arrive by the day you start building the deck.

**Approach:** you've already computed "order the deck by day X" (working back from the customer due date). If building the deck consumes wood, the wood's _due date_ is day X, and you apply the same backward-scheduling to the wood: order-wood-by = X minus wood's lead time. The offset cascades down the tree. This is M11's traversal carrying a date instead of a cost.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what two mistakes does the naive "order what's demanded, when it's due" version make? **Check:** gross-vs-net and the calendar named without notes.
2. **Research.** The targets. _Objective:_ time-phasing explained backward from a due date, on paper, with real dates.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Working vs calendar days, netting policy, output shape. **Check:** written; the calendar decision includes what you're deliberately ignoring.
4. **Build — netting.** Gross-to-net against inventory. **Check:** 50 demanded, 10 on hand → 40; full stock → no order at all.
5. **Build — scheduling.** Due date minus lead time, per your calendar policy. **Check:** hand-verified dates, including one that crosses a weekend if you chose working days.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Feed it a due date too close to today. **Check:** the late order is flagged, not silently emitted.
7. **Measure & debrief.** A table of hand-computed cases, all matching. _Ask:_ what changes when demand has ten lines and the BOM has three levels (the M11 handshake)? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the worked example producing the expected order line; the late-order flag firing on a tight due date.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a time-phased MRP engine — it nets demand against inventory, then schedules backward through lead times, and critically it flags orders whose date is already in the past instead of silently emitting an impossible plan. The subtle bit was business-day arithmetic."
- **Design review:** a peer subtracts raw calendar days. Show the weekend failure and the working-calendar fix.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I coded the actual heartbeat of MRP software — scheduling material orders backward from a due date. The gotcha is that subtracting days naively lands you on a Sunday nobody ships on."
- **Reach-back (time thread):** M5's RxJS and this both deal with time, but in opposite directions — one reacts to time flowing forward, this plans backward. What does "backward through time" force you to compute that forward streaming never does?

---

## M13 — The Shop-Floor State Machine · MES workflow control · (M)

> **Story.** As a supply-chain systems engineer, I want a state machine governing a manufacturing order's path through the floor, so that illegal jumps are impossible by construction and rework is a modeled transition, not a hack. **MVP.** The given states enum, `POST /order/{id}/move-next`, skip attempts rejected, failed inspection rolling back to ASSEMBLY with rework notes. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - A legal advance updates state; a skip attempt is refused with a clear error and no state change.
> - A failed inspection transitions back to ASSEMBLY and appends rework notes.
> - State and history are queryable at any time.

**Anchors:** _Data & Objects_ (modeling state and behavior); the API contract discipline from Part I.

### Briefing

A **manufacturing execution system** (MES) tracks a production order as it moves down a digital assembly line, and its whole job is _preventing human error_ by refusing illegal moves. An order goes `KITTING → ASSEMBLY → INSPECTION → READY`, and the software's value is that it will not let an operator skip inspection or ship something that failed. This is a **state machine**: a set of allowed states and strictly-enforced rules about which transitions are legal. Mapping physical, real-world boundaries into backend validation logic is the core skill of all shop-floor software. The center of gravity is **encoding rules as enforced state transitions**, not suggestions.

### The trap

**The illegal transition nobody guarded.** It's easy to build a `move-next` that just advances a status field — and thereby lets an order jump from ASSEMBLY straight to READY, skipping INSPECTION, exactly the human error the system exists to prevent. And the subtler one: a _failed_ inspection has _side effects_ (roll the order back to ASSEMBLY and attach rework notes), so a transition isn't always "forward" and isn't always side-effect-free.

### The feature

> A state machine governing one manufacturing order. The contract:
> 
> - **States** (a fixed enum): `KITTING`, `ASSEMBLY`, `INSPECTION`, `READY`.
> - **Endpoint:** `POST /order/{id}/move-next` advances the order to the next legal state.
> - **Guardrails** (rules, stated — you implement, I don't): an order may only advance along the legal path; attempting to skip a state (e.g. ASSEMBLY → READY) must be _rejected with an error_, not performed. If `INSPECTION` _fails_, the order transitions _back_ to `ASSEMBLY` and a `rework_notes` string is appended — an automatic, conditional, backward transition.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- A legal advance succeeds and the order's state updates.
- An attempt to skip a state is refused with a clear error; the state does _not_ change.
- A failed inspection rolls the order back to ASSEMBLY and records rework notes.
- The order's state and history are queryable at any time.

### Decision map

1. **Where the transition rules live.** Options: scattered `if`-checks in the endpoint · a single explicit transition table/map (from-state → allowed to-states). _Axis:_ quick vs a single source of truth you can't forget a case in. _Forcing:_ When a fifth state is added next quarter, how many places must change? Which design makes an illegal transition _impossible to express_ rather than merely _checked_?
2. **Modeling the "inspection failed" branch.** Options: a separate endpoint · a parameter on `move-next` (pass/fail) · an event that triggers the rollback. _Axis:_ clarity vs flexibility. _Forcing:_ Is "fail inspection" really a "move-next," or a different kind of event? How does that shape your API?
3. **State + history: overwrite vs append.** Options: store only the current state · append every transition to a history log. _Axis:_ simplicity vs auditability (which manufacturing demands). _Forcing:_ When a quality auditor asks "when did this order fail inspection and who reworked it," can your data answer? (Reaches back to the append-vs-overwrite instinct from M7's chat history.)
4. **Enforcement location: app vs database.** Options: guard only in application code · also constrain in the database (enum type, check constraints). _Axis:_ one layer vs defense in depth. _Forcing:_ If someone writes to the database _not_ through your endpoint, what stops an illegal state then?
5. **Concurrency on one order.** Options: assume one operator at a time · guard against two simultaneous `move-next` calls. _Axis:_ simplicity vs correctness on a busy floor. _Forcing:_ Two operators hit `move-next` at once — could the order advance twice or into an inconsistent state?

### Research targets

- _What is a finite state machine, in plain terms?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can define states, transitions, and "illegal transition" with your order example.
- _Modeling state machines in a backend — transition table vs conditionals._ — **25 min.** Done when you can articulate why a central table beats scattered `if`s.
- _Enum types and check constraints in your database._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say how the database itself can reject an invalid state.
- _Auditability via event/history logging._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain append-only history and what it buys an auditor.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full workflow-engine frameworks, parallel/nested states (statecharts — note, park), multi-order scheduling, operator authentication and roles (that's M4-flavored), UI for the shop floor. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Attempt the illegal skip (ASSEMBLY → READY) and a legal advance. **The number:** is the illegal move refused and the legal one accepted — and after a failed inspection, is the state ASSEMBLY with rework notes attached? Every guardrail either holds or it doesn't; count the holds.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My transition logic is becoming a nest of if-statements</summary>

**Orient:** the set of legal moves is _data_, not control flow — represent it as a lookup, and the endpoint becomes "is this move in the allowed set? if yes, do it; if no, reject."

**Approach:** define a map from each state to the set of states it may legally transition to (KITTING → {ASSEMBLY}, ASSEMBLY → {INSPECTION}, INSPECTION → {READY, ASSEMBLY}, …). The endpoint's whole job collapses to a membership check against that map, plus the special side effects (rework notes) for specific transitions. Adding a state is now a data edit, not a logic rewrite.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what human error does this system exist to prevent, and what's the cost when the guard is missing? **Check:** the ASSEMBLY→READY skip story told in two sentences.
2. **Research.** The targets. _Objective:_ a transition table sketched on paper — states down the side, events across the top, illegal cells marked.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Where rules live, history model, enforcement location. **Check:** written; overwrite-vs-append decided with a reason.
4. **Build — the legal path.** `move-next` walking KITTING → READY. **Check:** four advances succeed in order; state queryable at each step.
5. **Walk into the trap.** Attempt the skip. **Check:** refused with a clear error, state unchanged — proven by re-query.
6. **Build — the branch.** Failed inspection → back to ASSEMBLY with appended rework notes. **Check:** the backward transition fires only on failure, and the notes accumulate.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Fire two conflicting requests at one order (your concurrency decision, exercised). _Ask:_ what did the database enforce vs what did the app promise? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the refused skip, the accepted advance, the inspection-fail rollback with notes.

- **Interview (90s):** "I modeled a manufacturing order as a state machine with a central transition table, so illegal moves like skipping inspection are _unrepresentable_, not just checked. The interesting case was inspection-fail — a conditional _backward_ transition with side effects, which broke my initial 'always move forward' assumption."
- **Design review:** a peer guards transitions with scattered conditionals. Argue the transition-table refactor on the "add a new state" cost.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I built shop-floor software whose entire job is refusing to let humans skip steps — a state machine that won't ship an order that failed inspection."
- **Reach-back (contract thread):** this endpoint refuses bad _transitions_; M4's endpoints refuse bad _identity_; M1's refuse bad _input_. What's common to how all three shape an error response?

---

## M14 — The Machine Heartbeat Processor · IIoT stream → live analytics · (L)

> **Story.** As a supply-chain systems engineer, I want a simulated machine heartbeat flowing into an ingestion endpoint and out as a live availability figure, so that I've handled data that never stops, arrives dirty, and has window edges. **MVP.** The given contracts: a ~5s simulator, an append-only log, and an availability query over the last hour. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - The simulator streams continuously; every ping persists with its timestamp.
> - Availability reflects the actual RUNNING/STOPPED mix and updates as data arrives.
> - Missing and out-of-order pings are handled by a decision you made, not ignored.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (streams and the event loop); _Where the Data Lives_ (time-series storage, OLTP vs OLAP).

### Briefing

Modern manufacturing runs on **industrial IoT**: machines emit a constant heartbeat of status, and software turns that firehose into operational insight — chiefly, _is this machine actually running or sitting idle?_ This module simulates a machine pinging its status every few seconds, ingests that stream, and computes **availability** — the fraction of recent time the machine was running. It teaches the real skill: turning dirty, high-frequency stream data into clean business analytics. The center of gravity is **ingesting a stream and aggregating it over a time window** — the time thread meeting the data thread.

### The trap

**The stream is dirty and never stops, and windows have edges.** Real telemetry has missing pings (the network hiccuped), out-of-order arrivals, and bursts — so code that assumes clean, evenly-spaced, in-order data computes confidently wrong numbers. And "availability over the last hour" hides a boundary question: what about a machine that's been RUNNING since before the hour started, or a gap where _no_ ping arrived — is that downtime or unknown?

### The feature

> Two pieces plus a query. The contracts:
> 
> - **Simulated machine** (a loop you write): every ~5 seconds, POST `{ machine_id: "robot_arm_1", status: "RUNNING", timestamp: <now> }`, with a random chance the status flips to `"STOPPED"`.
> - **Ingestion endpoint:** accepts that payload and appends it to a log table `(machine_id, status, timestamp)`.
> - **Availability query/function:** over the last hour of logs for a machine, compute `availability = (RUNNING pings / total pings) × 100`.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The simulator streams status pings continuously; the endpoint persists each with its timestamp.
- The availability figure reflects the actual RUNNING/STOPPED mix and updates as new data arrives.
- The system behaves sanely when pings are missing or arrive out of order (you decide _how_ — but decide, don't ignore).

### Decision map

1. **Ping-count availability vs time-weighted availability.** Options: fraction of _pings_ that were RUNNING · fraction of _time_ spent RUNNING (weight each status by how long it held). _Axis:_ trivial vs accurate. _Forcing:_ If pings are evenly spaced, are these the same? If a STOPPED status held for an hour but only pinged twice, does ping-counting understate the downtime?
2. **Handling missing pings / gaps.** Options: ignore gaps · treat a gap as unknown · assume last-known status persists through the gap. _Axis:_ simple vs honest vs a modeling assumption. _Forcing:_ If no ping arrives for ten minutes, was the machine down, or was the _sensor_ down? Does your availability number lie either way?
3. **Storage: raw log vs pre-aggregated.** Options: keep every ping and aggregate on read · roll up into per-minute summaries as data arrives. _Axis:_ flexibility and audit vs query speed at scale (OLTP vs OLAP, from _Where the Data Lives_). _Forcing:_ At millions of pings, does aggregating-on-read stay fast? What does pre-aggregation cost you in flexibility?
4. **Ingestion under load.** Options: synchronous write-per-ping · buffer and batch-insert. _Axis:_ simplicity vs throughput. _Forcing:_ At a thousand machines pinging every 5 seconds, does a database write per ping keep up? (Reaches back to M10's "big writes hurt.")
5. **Out-of-order arrivals.** Options: trust arrival order · order by the ping's own timestamp. _Axis:_ simple vs correct. _Forcing:_ If a delayed ping arrives after a newer one, does your windowing use _arrival_ time or _event_ time? Which is right?

### Research targets

- _What is availability (and OEE) in manufacturing?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can define availability as a time fraction and name why it matters.
- _Event time vs processing time in stream data._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain why aggregating by arrival order corrupts results when data is late or out of order. _Seed:_ Waiting, All the Way Up (streams) plus one stream-processing article.
- _Time-series storage patterns — raw vs rollup, OLTP vs OLAP._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say when you'd pre-aggregate. _Seed:_ Where the Data Lives (OLTP/OLAP).
- _Windowed aggregation over a time range._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe computing a metric over "the last hour" and the boundary questions it raises.

### Out of scope (fence)

Real message brokers (Kafka/MQTT — note, park; this is the natural next step), stream-processing frameworks, alerting/thresholding, multi-machine dashboards as UI, predictive maintenance ML. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Let the simulator run; watch availability. **The number:** does the computed availability track the actual RUNNING/STOPPED ratio you're generating? Then _stop_ sending pings for a stretch and see whether your metric handles the gap the way you decided it should (not the way you forgot to).

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>Time-weighted availability confuses me — how is it different from counting pings?</summary>

**Orient:** counting pings assumes every ping represents an equal slice of time; time-weighting asks how _long_ each status actually held before the next ping changed it.

**Approach:** sort the pings by their own timestamp. Each status "owns" the interval from its ping until the next ping. RUNNING time is the sum of intervals whose status was RUNNING; availability is RUNNING-time over total-time. Now a status that held for an hour counts as an hour whether it pinged twice or two hundred times. The subtlety you'll hit: the interval at the very edges of your window.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what three kinds of dirt does a real telemetry stream carry, and what wrong number does each produce if ignored? **Check:** gaps, disorder, bursts — with a consequence each.
2. **Research.** The targets; event time vs processing time is the load-bearing one. _Objective:_ you can say whose clock every timestamp in your design belongs to.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Ping-count vs time-weighted availability, gap policy, storage shape. **Check:** written; the gap policy states what a missing ping means.
4. **Build — the machine.** The simulator loop, flipping status randomly. **Check:** it runs unattended; the log grows on schedule. Leave it running from now on.
5. **Build — ingestion.** The endpoint appending to the log table. **Check:** rows match pings one-for-one, timestamps intact.
6. **Build — the window.** Availability over the last hour. **Check:** the figure moves as you watch, and matches a hand count over a small window.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Kill the simulator for ten minutes; replay a late batch out of order. _Ask:_ did the number lie, degrade honestly, or refuse? **Check:** it did what your DSA said it would. Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** live availability tracking your generated ratio; sane behavior across a deliberate ping gap.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an IIoT heartbeat processor computing machine availability from a status stream. The decision I'd defend is time-weighted over ping-count availability — here's the case where counting pings understates downtime — and I had to decide explicitly what a missing-ping _gap_ means, because pretending it's clean data gives you a confident wrong number."
- **Design review:** a peer aggregates by arrival order. Walk through the out-of-order/late-ping failure and the event-time fix.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I'm turning a simulated machine's status stream into a live availability metric — the whole challenge is that real telemetry is dirty and never stops, so your windowing has to handle gaps honestly."
- **Reach-back (time thread):** you've now handled streams four ways across the Gauntlet (M5 reactive, M7 WebSocket, M12 backward-scheduled, this). Which one's core difficulty — out-of-order data — shows up in the others, and which is unique to telemetry?

---

# PART V — Frontend Craft

_Three modules where the browser stops being a form-renderer and becomes a medium. Editorial layout that aligns the unalignable (M15), text rendering that refuses the DOM's reflow tax (M16), and a 3D interaction at award-site polish (M17). UI/UX exploration happens in Figma; these modules are about turning an intended design into an honest implementation. This is the floor thread at the top of the stack — "every convenience is a receipt," seen from the visual layer._

---

## M15 — Aligning the Unalignable · a magazine-grade editorial layout · (L)

> **Story.** As a design-engineer, I want a magazine-grade editorial layout that holds a visible grid across breakpoints despite unruly content, so that alignment comes from the system, not from fixed heights that lie. **MVP.** A lead story plus a mixed grid of secondary cards, three breakpoints, no overlap, no horizontal scroll, no hardcoded content heights. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - At every breakpoint, edges and baselines align to a coherent grid despite varied content lengths.
> - Resizing reflows smoothly; nothing overlaps or overflows.
> - No block has a fixed height that breaks if content grows; images keep their aspect ratios.

**Anchors:** _Pixels With Intent_ (the browser rendering and layout material); the function-first React/TypeScript guide for the component structure.

### Briefing

An editorial homepage — think a serious newspaper front page — is one of the genuinely hard layout problems, and it's hard for a reason that reveals what CSS layout _is_. You have a lead story, a cluster of secondary stories, mixed blocks of image and text, all of _different natural sizes_ — and they must nonetheless lock to a coherent visual grid, then reflow gracefully from a five-column desktop masthead to a single mobile column. The center of gravity is **CSS Grid and intrinsic sizing**: two-dimensional layout, and the difference between a size you _set_ and a size the content _decides_. Get this and most "how do I lay this out" questions dissolve.

### The trap

**Different-height content breaking the grid** — and its partner, **the responsive reflow that turns to mush.** Blocks whose text and images are different lengths won't line up if you lean on fixed heights (which lie the moment content changes) or on floats and margins (which fight you). And a layout hand-tuned for desktop columns frequently collapses into overlap or overflow at the first narrow breakpoint, because the sizing was pixel-thinking, not grid-thinking. The tell of the trap: you're reaching for a magic pixel height or a negative margin.

### The feature

> A responsive editorial homepage, layout only (no backend; placeholder content is fine, but content of _realistically varied_ lengths): a prominent lead story, a grid of secondary stories mixing image-led and text-led cards, all aligned to a visible underlying grid, reflowing cleanly across at least three breakpoints (wide, tablet, phone) with no horizontal scroll, no overlap, and no hardcoded content heights.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- At every breakpoint, cards align to a coherent grid — edges and baselines line up, despite varied content lengths.
- Resizing the viewport reflows the layout smoothly; nothing overlaps or overflows the viewport width.
- No block has a fixed height that would break if its content grew; the grid absorbs variation.
- Images maintain their intended aspect ratios without distortion.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building. (Do the visual design in Figma first; then decide how to _implement_ it.)

1. **CSS Grid vs Flexbox vs a mix.** Options: Grid for the whole page · Flexbox · Grid for the 2D page skeleton, Flexbox inside cards. _Axis:_ two-dimensional control vs one-dimensional simplicity. _Forcing:_ Is this layout fundamentally 2D (rows _and_ columns that must align) or 1D (a single flow)? Which tool was built for which, and where does mixing them actually help?
2. **Fixed vs intrinsic track sizing.** Options: fixed column widths (px) · fractional/`minmax()`/`auto-fit` tracks. _Axis:_ precise control vs content- and viewport-responsiveness. _Forcing:_ When the viewport shrinks, do your columns _know_ how to give way, or do they overflow? What does `minmax(0, 1fr)` prevent that a bare `1fr` doesn't?
3. **Aligning different-height cards.** Options: force equal heights · let the grid rows size to the tallest · use subgrid so inner elements align across cards. _Axis:_ uniformity vs honoring content vs cross-card baseline alignment. _Forcing:_ Should two cards with different text lengths have equal height, or aligned _internal_ elements (image tops, headline baselines)? Which does subgrid make possible that nesting grids doesn't?
4. **Responsive strategy.** Options: media-query breakpoints · container queries · intrinsic auto-fit/minmax that reflows with no breakpoints at all. _Axis:_ explicit control per width vs components that respond to their own space vs "let the grid do it." _Forcing:_ Do you want to hand-design each breakpoint, or express _rules_ that reflow continuously? When would a container query beat a media query for a reusable card?
5. **Image handling.** Options: fixed dimensions · `aspect-ratio` + `object-fit` · art-directed `<picture>` with different crops per breakpoint. _Axis:_ simplicity vs no-distortion vs editorial control. _Forcing:_ When the same image appears in a wide lead and a narrow card, should it be the same crop scaled, or a different crop? What stops it from squashing?

### Research targets

- _CSS Grid vs Flexbox — the actual dividing line._ — **25 min.** Done when you can state the 2D-vs-1D rule and give one layout each is clearly right for. _Seed:_ the CSS-Tricks grid and flexbox guides (skim, don't memorize).
- _Intrinsic sizing: `fr`, `minmax`, `auto-fit`/`auto-fill`._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain how `repeat(auto-fit, minmax(200px, 1fr))` reflows a card grid with _no_ media queries. _Seed:_ one good auto-fit demo.
- _Subgrid — what problem it solves._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say why nested grids _don't_ align across siblings and subgrid does.
- _`aspect-ratio` and `object-fit` for images._ — **25 min.** Done when you can prevent distortion without knowing pixel dimensions.

### Out of scope (fence)

Animations and transitions, a CSS framework (Tailwind/etc. — do this one in raw CSS to actually learn the layout), theming/dark mode, real content management, print stylesheets. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Open DevTools' grid overlay and resize the viewport slowly through all breakpoints. **The number:** at how many widths does something break — overlap, overflow, or fall off the grid? (Zero is the target; each break is a bug to name.) Confirm no horizontal scrollbar appears at any width.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My cards won't align — different text lengths push everything out of line</summary>

**Orient:** the misalignment is because each card is sizing _itself_ independently; alignment across cards has to be owned by a _shared_ grid, not by the cards.

**Approach:** put the cards as items of one grid so the grid's row tracks (not each card) decide heights — now a row is as tall as its tallest card and all cards in it share that height. For aligning _inside_ the cards (all headlines on one line, all images the same top), the cards need to share the _parent's_ grid lines — that's what subgrid is for. If you're setting heights on the cards themselves, remove them and let the grid tracks do the work.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why do fixed heights lie, and what does "the grid absorbs variation" actually require of the CSS? **Check:** you can name the two failure smells (fixed heights, magic numbers) in someone else's layout.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ the Grid-vs-Flexbox dividing line stated as a rule you'd defend.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Grid strategy, track sizing, alignment approach, breakpoint plan. **Check:** written; includes which content variation each choice absorbs.
4. **Build — the skeleton.** The underlying grid, visible (turn on dev-tools grid overlay), with the lead story placed. **Check:** tracks are intentional, not accidental.
5. **Build — the cards.** Secondary stories, image-led and text-led, real varied lengths. **Check:** edges and baselines align despite the variation.
6. **Build — the reflow.** Three breakpoints. **Check:** resize is smooth; no overlap, no horizontal scroll, aspect ratios held.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Double one card's text and swap one image's aspect ratio. **Check:** the grid absorbs it. _Ask:_ which alignment survived by design and which by luck? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the grid overlay showing alignment; a screen-recorded resize through all breakpoints with nothing breaking.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a magazine-grade editorial layout in raw CSS Grid. The hard part is aligning cards of genuinely different content heights without hardcoding anything — I used the grid's row tracks for cross-card height and subgrid for internal alignment, and made it reflow with intrinsic sizing so most breakpoints need no media query at all."
- **Design review:** a peer built it with fixed heights and floats. Show where it breaks when content changes, and make the grid-thinking case.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been rebuilding a newspaper-style front page in pure CSS Grid — the satisfying part is getting different-sized blocks to lock to one grid without a single magic pixel value."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** the browser had to _lay this out_ — compute every box's position. M16 is about measuring text _without_ triggering that layout. Why is layout expensive enough that avoiding it is a whole library's reason to exist?

---

## M16 — Text Without the DOM Tax · a pretext rendering playground · (M)

> **Story.** As a design-engineer chasing the floor under text, I want a pretext-powered rendering playground that does one thing the DOM can't do gracefully, so that I understand what measurement costs and why reflow is a tax I can refuse. **MVP.** One hero effect (flow-around-a-shape or shrink-wrapped headlines), measured with pretext, rendered to canvas/SVG, verified against the browser's own line breaks. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Layout is computed without ever calling `offsetHeight`/`getBoundingClientRect` to measure.
> - On resize, only the cheap layout step reruns — the expensive prepare step does not.
> - pretext's line breaks match the browser's for the same text and font; emoji and non-Latin input don't corrupt layout.

**Anchors:** _Pixels With Intent_ (its rendering-pipeline and reflow material is the exact backdrop for this module; its Part 7 paper-build of a layered card stack is the spirit of "derive the layout yourself"). Library: **pretext** — `github.com/chenglou/pretext`.

### Briefing

Here's a thing most web developers never question: when you ask the browser how tall a paragraph is (`offsetHeight`, `getBoundingClientRect`), it may have to **reflow** — recompute layout for a chunk of the page — which is one of the most expensive things the browser does. **pretext** is a pure-JavaScript library that sidesteps this entirely: it measures and lays out multiline text _without touching the DOM_, using the browser's own canvas text-measurement as ground truth, then does line-breaking as pure arithmetic over cached widths. That unlocks things the DOM makes painful — text that flows around a shape, headlines that shrink-wrap to their tightest width, masonry sized without guessing, rendering to canvas/SVG/WebGL. The center of gravity is a mind-bender worth internalizing: **text is not characters — it's graphemes, measured segments, and widths — and layout is arithmetic you can do yourself.**

This module is about _using_ the library to make something expressive. Read pretext's README API to know the tools; but resist reading other people's demo source until you've drafted your _own_ layout approach — the point is to build the instinct, not to transcribe a solution.

### The trap

**Measurement drift and the hot-path mistake.** pretext is only accurate if the font, line-height, and letter-spacing you _measure_ with exactly match what you _render_ with — the moment your canvas font string diverges from your actual CSS, every line break is subtly wrong and you'll chase a ghost. And the performance trap the library exists to avoid: re-running the expensive `prepare()` step on every resize instead of only the cheap `layout()` — which throws away the entire point. Underneath both: treating text as a string of characters, so an emoji or an Arabic run (which are multiple code units but one _grapheme_) breaks your measurement.

### The feature

> A text-rendering playground that does at least one thing the DOM can't do gracefully, built on pretext. Pick a hero effect:
> 
> - **Flow text around a floated shape** — lines beside the shape are narrower, lines below it are full width (pretext's variable-width per-line layout), rendered to canvas.
> - **Shrink-wrap / balanced headlines** — find the tightest width that still fits the text on the intended number of lines (pretext's line-walking to search widths).
> 
> Whichever you pick: measure with pretext (no `offsetHeight`), render the result yourself (canvas or SVG), and verify your line breaks match what the browser would actually do.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Text lays out correctly _without_ your code ever calling `offsetHeight`/`getBoundingClientRect` for measurement.
- On resize, only the cheap layout step reruns — the expensive prepare step does not.
- The line breaks pretext computes match the browser's own rendering of the same text at the same font (accuracy check).
- Non-Latin text or emoji in the input doesn't corrupt the layout (graphemes handled).

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Which pretext use case.** Options: measure-height-only (`prepare`/`layout`) · full manual per-line layout (`prepareWithSegments` + line-walking). _Axis:_ simplicity vs the power to place each line yourself. _Forcing:_ Does your hero effect just need a _height_ (masonry, overflow-check), or does it need to place _each line at a different width_ (flow-around, canvas render)? That answer picks your API tier.
2. **Render target.** Options: canvas · SVG · positioned DOM. _Axis:_ pixel control and WebGL-readiness vs accessibility and selectability. _Forcing:_ Canvas text isn't selectable or screen-reader-visible — is that acceptable for your effect, and if not, what's your fallback for real users?
3. **Keeping measurement and rendering in sync.** Options: hardcode the font in two places and hope · derive one font descriptor used for _both_ measure and render. _Axis:_ convenience vs correctness. _Forcing:_ Where could your measured font and rendered font diverge, and what's the one source of truth that prevents it? (This _is_ the trap — design against it.)
4. **prepare vs layout on the hot path.** Options: re-prepare on every change · prepare once, layout on resize. _Axis:_ less thinking vs the entire performance premise. _Forcing:_ What work does `prepare()` do that you must _not_ repeat when only the width changed? What's cheap enough to run every frame?
5. **Shrink-wrap search (if you pick that effect).** Options: linear scan of widths · binary search on width using line-count. _Axis:_ simple vs efficient. _Forcing:_ You want the narrowest width that keeps the text at N lines — how do you search that space, and why is line-count a monotonic signal you can binary-search on? (Reaches back to the rep circuit.)

### Research targets

- _What is reflow, and why is `offsetHeight` sometimes expensive?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain layout thrashing and why measuring text without the DOM is worth a library. _Seed:_ Pixels With Intent (rendering pipeline); one "layout thrashing" article.
- _pretext's two use cases — the API shape._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what `prepare`/`layout` give you vs `prepareWithSegments` + the line-walkers, tab closed. _Seed:_ the pretext README (read _this_, not demos).
- _Graphemes vs code points vs code units — why text isn't characters._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why an emoji is one grapheme but several code units, and why measurement must respect that. _Seed:_ search "JavaScript Intl.Segmenter grapheme."
- _Canvas `measureText` as the measurement ground truth._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say why measuring on a canvas doesn't trigger layout the way the DOM does.

### Out of scope (fence)

Building your own text-measurement engine (that's pretext's job — note the fascination, park it), full internationalization/bidi rendering correctness, font loading and FOUT/FOIT, rich-text editing, WebGL text rendering (note, park). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Render the same string at the same font two ways — via pretext, and via a real DOM element — and compare where the lines break. **The number:** do pretext's line breaks and computed height match the browser's actual rendering? Then open the Performance panel during a resize: **how many forced reflows does your code trigger?** (The answer you want is zero — that's the whole point.)

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My pretext line breaks don't match the browser's</summary>

**Orient:** pretext is accurate _by construction_ if it measures with the same font as you render — a mismatch means your measure-font and render-font differ somewhere, even subtly.

**Approach:** check that the exact font string you pass to `prepare()` (family, size, weight, style) is identical to the CSS actually painting the text, that your `lineHeight` argument matches your CSS `line-height`, and that `letterSpacing` matches. The README calls out specific gotchas — `system-ui` is unsafe for accuracy on some platforms; use a named font. Also confirm you're not comparing against a DOM element with different padding or box-sizing affecting the available width.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what exactly does the browser do when you read `offsetHeight`, and why is that sometimes a tax? **Check:** reflow explained in three sentences.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ pretext's prepare-vs-layout split explained — what's expensive once vs cheap per-frame.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Which hero effect, render target, and the sync strategy between measured and rendered font. **Check:** written; the font-string discipline is explicit.
4. **Build — measurement floor.** Text measured and drawn at one width, no DOM reads. **Check:** your rendered line breaks match the browser's for the same text and font, side by side.
5. **Build — the hero.** Flow-around-shape or shrink-wrap search. **Check:** the effect works on real prose, not just one test string.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Resize continuously. _Ask:_ what reruns per frame — and is prepare in the hot path? **Check:** only layout reruns on resize; throw emoji and non-Latin text at it without corruption.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Time prepare vs layout; show the ratio. _Ask:_ where else does "measure once, lay out many" apply (M17's loop, M28's scroll)? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the hero effect running; the line-break match against the DOM; the Performance panel showing zero forced reflows on resize.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a text-flow effect with pretext, which measures multiline text without touching the DOM — so no reflow. The insight I'd share is that `offsetHeight` can force expensive layout, and pretext does the measurement on canvas as pure arithmetic instead. The trap is keeping the measure-font and render-font perfectly in sync, or the line breaks drift."
- **Design review:** a peer measures text with `getBoundingClientRect` in a resize handler. Explain layout thrashing and why moving measurement off the DOM is the fix.
