- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been playing with pretext — it lays out text without the DOM, so you can flow text around shapes and shrink-wrap headlines, things CSS makes weirdly hard. The mind-bender is that 'text' is really graphemes and measured widths."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** M15's layout was _expensive_ precisely because of what pretext avoids. And M18's C memory is the deepest version of "every convenience is a receipt." State the floor-thread thesis in one sentence using all three.

---

## M17 — Sixty Frames, Held · an award-tier 3D interaction · (L)

> **Story.** As a design-engineer, I want one award-tier 3D interaction that holds sixty frames and cleans up after itself, so that craft means measured performance and a graceful floor, not a machine-melting demo. **MVP.** One polished interactive 3D element: input-responsive, smooth, steady frame rate, disposed resources, reduced-motion/no-GPU fallback. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Steady ~60fps during use, measured, with nothing heavy allocated inside the render loop.
> - Repeated mount/unmount does not grow memory (resources disposed).
> - With reduced motion or no WebGL, the page still works — a sensible fallback, not a void.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (the `requestAnimationFrame` heartbeat is the same event-loop idea); _Pixels With Intent_ (the GPU/compositing material). Library: Three.js (with React Three Fiber if you choose — you have prior reps there).

### Briefing

The interactive 3D pieces that win awards look like magic and are, underneath, a tight loop and a strict performance budget. Three.js is the library that wraps WebGL — the browser's door to the GPU — into a workable **scene graph**: a `scene` holds `mesh`es (shapes) with `material`s (surfaces) lit by `light`s, viewed through a `camera`, drawn by a `renderer` — sixty times a second. Building one award-tier _interaction_ (not a whole site — a hero element, a scroll-driven effect, a responsive 3D object) teaches the two things that separate a portfolio piece from a laptop-melting embarrassment. The center of gravity is **the render loop and the performance floor**: what runs every frame, and what the GPU will and won't do in 16.7 milliseconds.

### The trap

**Jank, and the machine-melting portfolio piece.** Do the wrong work inside the per-frame loop — allocate objects, recompute geometry, touch the DOM — and the frame rate collapses into stutter. Forget to _dispose_ geometries and materials when you're done and memory climbs until the tab dies. And the cruel one for a _portfolio_: it runs at 60fps on your machine and turns a reviewer's older laptop into a space heater, because you never set a budget or a fallback. The tell: it's beautiful and your fan is loud.

### The feature

> One polished, interactive 3D element at genuine craft level: it responds to input (pointer, scroll, or both), animates smoothly, holds a steady frame rate, cleans up after itself, and degrades gracefully where 3D isn't available or isn't wanted (reduced-motion, no-GPU). Study the _effect_ you admire from award sites — but draft your own approach before reading anyone's source.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The interaction holds a steady ~60fps on your hardware during use (measurable, not asserted).
- Mounting and unmounting the scene repeatedly does _not_ grow memory without bound (resources are disposed).
- With `prefers-reduced-motion` set, or on a context without WebGL, the page still works — a sensible static fallback, not a broken void.
- Nothing heavy is allocated inside the render loop.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building. (Design the intended effect in Figma / as a reference board first.)

1. **Raw Three.js vs React Three Fiber.** Options: imperative Three.js · declarative R3F. _Axis:_ seeing the machinery vs React-native composition and lifecycle. _Forcing:_ You have R3F reps — is the goal this month to go _deeper_ into the underlying Three.js you'd otherwise abstract away? Which choice teaches you more about the render loop itself?
2. **What runs per-frame vs once.** Options: rebuild/allocate in the loop · create once, mutate cheaply per frame. _Axis:_ naive vs the entire performance premise. _Forcing:_ Which operations (geometry creation, material setup, object allocation) must happen _outside_ the loop? What's the only kind of work cheap enough to do every frame? (This _is_ the trap.)
3. **Animation driver.** Options: time-based (delta since last frame) · frame-count-based · scroll-position-based. _Axis:_ frame-rate independence vs simplicity vs scroll-coupling. _Forcing:_ If the frame rate dips, does your animation _slow down_ (frame-based) or stay correct (time-based)? Why does multiplying by delta-time matter?
4. **Performance budget.** Options: no budget · explicit limits on polygon count, draw calls, texture size. _Axis:_ freedom vs running on a normal laptop. _Forcing:_ What's your draw-call count, and what happens to it as you add objects? What texture resolution is enough vs vanity? (Reaches back to M18 — the GPU, like memory, is finite.)
5. **Graceful degradation.** Options: assume WebGL and motion-tolerance · detect and fall back. _Axis:_ simplicity vs real users. _Forcing:_ What does a reviewer on a low-end device or with reduced-motion enabled see? Is "nothing" or "jank" an acceptable answer for a _portfolio_?
6. **Resource lifecycle.** Options: create and forget · dispose geometries/materials/textures on teardown. _Axis:_ less code vs no memory leak. _Forcing:_ What holds GPU memory after your component unmounts, and what has to explicitly release it?

### Research targets

- _The Three.js mental model: scene, camera, renderer, mesh, material, light._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name each part's job and how they combine into one rendered frame. _Seed:_ the Three.js "Creating a scene" doc; three.js-journey (skim the free intro).
- _The render loop and `requestAnimationFrame` — and delta-time._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain frame-rate-independent animation. _Seed:_ Waiting, All the Way Up (the rAF/event-loop section).
- _Draw calls and the GPU performance budget._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what a draw call is and why fewer is faster.
- _Disposing Three.js resources — the leak._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name what must be `.dispose()`d and when.

### Out of scope (fence)

Writing custom shaders/GLSL (genuinely deep — note, park it hard), physics engines, loading complex external 3D models, post-processing pipelines, a full multi-section site. Keep it to _one_ interaction. → parking lot. **And: reading award-site source is a post-commit reward** — draft and build your own approach first, then diff yours against theirs to learn.

### Measure ritual

Turn on a frame-rate meter (browser FPS counter or Three.js stats) and interact hard. **The number:** does it hold ~60fps (≤16.7ms/frame), and what's your draw-call count? Then mount/unmount the scene many times watching the memory graph — **does memory return to baseline, or climb?** (Climbing = a disposal leak.)

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My animation stutters / frame rate tanks under interaction</summary>

**Orient:** something expensive is running inside the per-frame loop that shouldn't be — the loop should _mutate_ cheap values, never _build_ things.

**Approach:** audit what executes every frame. Creating geometries or materials, allocating vectors/objects, or reading layout from the DOM inside the loop are the usual culprits — move all of them to run _once_ at setup, and per-frame only update positions/rotations/uniforms on already-created objects. If it's still janky, check your draw-call and polygon counts against a budget; the GPU may simply be asked for too much per frame.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what work is forbidden inside a per-frame loop, and what happens to a scene you unmount but never dispose? **Check:** both answered with mechanisms, not vibes.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ the scene/camera/renderer/mesh model drawn from memory; delta-time explained.
3. **Commit (DSA).** All six decisions, including the performance budget as a number and the degradation plan. **Check:** written before any npm install of a 3D library.
4. **Build — the stage.** Scene, lighting, the element, static. **Check:** renders at your budget's draw-call count.
5. **Build — the life.** Input-driven animation on delta time. **Check:** the FPS meter holds ~60 during interaction; the frame callback allocates nothing.
6. **Build — the exits.** Reduced-motion fallback, no-WebGL fallback, disposal on unmount. **Check:** both fallbacks render something sensible.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Mount/unmount in a loop while watching memory. **Check:** flat, not stairs. _Ask:_ which resource would have leaked, and who owns its lifetime? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the interaction at a held frame rate (show the meter); the memory graph returning to baseline across mount/unmount; the reduced-motion fallback.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an award-tier 3D interaction in Three.js and held 60fps by keeping the per-frame loop to cheap mutations — no allocation, no geometry rebuilds. Two things I'm proud of: time-based animation so it stays correct if frames drop, and disposing GPU resources on teardown so it doesn't leak. And it degrades for reduced-motion and no-WebGL, because a portfolio piece can't melt a reviewer's laptop."
- **Design review:** a peer's scene is gorgeous but janky and leaks. Diagnose per-frame allocation and missing disposal as a colleague, not a critic.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been building an award-site-style 3D interaction — the real craft turned out to be the performance budget, keeping it at 60fps without cooking the GPU."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** the render loop is the same `requestAnimationFrame` heartbeat behind smooth UI everywhere, and the GPU is as finite as M18's memory. What does "the machine is finite" force on you here that it also forced in C?

---

# PART VI — The Machine

_Two modules on the floor everything else stands on. A memory allocator in C, where you personally own every byte (M18), and a small game that makes you live in that world while also running a loop that never stops (M19). This is the deepest point of the floor thread — "every convenience is a receipt," and here you pay in full, on purpose. You already have a dynamic-array lesson compiling from your C curriculum; this builds on that footing._

---

## M18 — Every Byte Is Yours · a memory allocator in C · (M)

> **Story.** As a builder who wants to own the floor, I want an arena allocator in C that survives a memory checker on every path, so that stack-vs-heap, ownership, and the famous four bugs are things I've lived, not read. **MVP.** An arena allocator plus a driver that exercises it, checker-clean including failure paths. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Correct across several input paths and sizes; allocation failure handled, not ignored.
> - The checker reports zero leaks and zero errors on every path.
> - No path reads uninitialized memory or writes out of bounds.

**Anchors:** _The Machine Under Everything_ (this is the module that book exists to make legible); your `CURRICULUM_C_RUST.md` and the dynamic-array lesson you already have compiling (`dyn_array`).

### Briefing

In JavaScript and Python, memory is invisible: you make objects, they vanish when unneeded, and a garbage collector you never see does the bookkeeping. C removes the collector and hands _you_ the ledger. Memory comes in two regions: the **stack** (automatic, scoped, freed when a function returns — fast and safe but rigid) and the **heap** (manual, via `malloc`/`free` — flexible but entirely your responsibility). Building a small **allocator** — a program that manages heap memory itself — is the most direct way to _see_ the model that everything higher up is hiding. The center of gravity is exactly this: **the stack/heap distinction, pointers, lifetime, and ownership** — who is responsible for freeing what, and when.

### The trap

The famous four, and this feature can hit all of them: the **memory leak** (you `malloc` and never `free` — invisible until it exhausts memory under load), **use-after-free** (you `free` then read the freed pointer — sometimes works, sometimes corrupts, always a bug), **double-free** (freeing the same block twice — undefined behavior), and the **buffer overflow** (writing past a block's end — the classic security hole). Every one of them can _pass a casual test run_ and detonate later. The tell: "it works," but you haven't run a memory checker.

### The feature

> A program that manages heap memory manually and survives scrutiny. The recommended target is a small **arena (pool) allocator**: request one big block from the system up front, then hand out and track smaller chunks from it, with a way to reset or release the whole arena at once — used by a tiny driver program that exercises it. (If you'd rather, a generic dynamic structure with explicit ownership works too — but the goal is the same: non-trivial manual memory management you can _prove_ correct.)

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The program runs correctly across several input paths and sizes.
- A memory checker reports **zero leaks and zero errors** on every path — including the failure paths.
- Allocation failure (the system says no) is handled, not ignored.
- No path reads uninitialized memory or writes out of bounds.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Arena/pool vs individual `malloc`/`free`.** Options: many small `malloc`/`free` calls · one big arena carved up yourself. _Axis:_ simplicity and fine-grained freeing vs speed and bulk cleanup. _Forcing:_ If you allocate ten thousand small things with the same lifetime, is freeing them one-by-one the right model, or freeing the whole arena at once? What does the arena give up (individual frees) to gain speed?
2. **Ownership model.** Options: caller owns and frees · the structure owns and frees · reference counting. _Axis:_ clarity vs flexibility vs complexity. _Forcing:_ For every allocation, can you name _exactly one_ owner responsible for freeing it? Where ownership is ambiguous, that's where leaks and double-frees breed — can you make it unambiguous?
3. **Handling `realloc` and moved memory.** Options: fixed capacity · grow with `realloc`. _Axis:_ simplicity vs dynamic sizing. _Forcing:_ When `realloc` moves a block to a new address, what happens to every pointer that was pointing into the old location? (This is a dangling-pointer factory — how do you avoid it?)
4. **Allocation-failure policy.** Options: assume success · check every allocation and handle NULL. _Axis:_ less code vs not crashing on out-of-memory. _Forcing:_ `malloc` can return NULL — what does your program do then, and what happens if you don't check and dereference it?
5. **Alignment and sizing (arena path).** Options: hand out raw byte offsets · respect alignment requirements. _Axis:_ naive vs correct on real hardware. _Forcing:_ Why might returning an unaligned pointer for certain types be a problem, and how does that shape how you carve the arena?

### Research targets

- _Stack vs heap — what actually differs._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain, tab closed, why stack allocation is automatic and fast and heap allocation is manual, and when you're forced onto the heap. _Seed:_ The Machine Under Everything (the memory sections).
- _`malloc`/`free`/`realloc` — the contract with the system._ — **25 min.** Done when you can state what each returns, what ownership it transfers, and what `realloc` may do to your pointer.
- _The four memory bugs — how each happens and how a checker finds it._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can describe leak, use-after-free, double-free, and overflow, and what valgrind/ASan reports for each. _Seed:_ the valgrind quick-start.
- _Pointer basics: dereferencing, dangling, and NULL._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain what a dangling pointer is and how `free` creates one.

### Out of scope (fence)

Writing a full general-purpose allocator to compete with the system one (fascinating — park it), custom garbage collectors, thread-safe allocation, memory-mapped files, the deep internals of how the OS gives you pages. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run under **valgrind** (or AddressSanitizer) on every input path, including the ones designed to fail. **The number:** leaks and errors — and the target here genuinely _is_ zero, "All heap blocks were freed — no leaks are possible." Then deliberately introduce one bug (skip a `free`, or read after free) and confirm the checker _catches_ it — so you trust the tool.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>realloc broke my program — pointers into the block stopped working</summary>

**Orient:** `realloc` is allowed to _move_ your data to a new address and free the old one; any pointer you were holding into the old block is now dangling.

**Approach:** after a `realloc`, the _only_ valid pointer to the block is the one `realloc` returned — reassign it, and treat every previously-stored pointer into that block as invalid. The design fix is to not hold long-lived raw pointers _into_ a growable block; hold an index or an offset, and compute the pointer from the current base when you need it. (This is exactly why your dynamic array stores a base pointer and length rather than scattered interior pointers.)

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ for each of the famous four bugs, what does the failure look like from the outside? **Check:** four bugs, four observable symptoms, no notes.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ the malloc/free contract recited, including what the system never checks for you.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Arena semantics, ownership model, failure policy, alignment. **Check:** written; ownership stated as "who frees what, when."
4. **Build — the arena.** Init, alloc, reset, destroy. **Check:** the driver exercises normal paths clean under the checker.
5. **Build — the edges.** Exhaustion, zero-size, alignment-hostile sizes. **Check:** allocation failure is handled, not ignored; still checker-clean.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Deliberately write one of the four bugs in a scratch branch of the driver. _Ask:_ what exactly does the checker report, and would you have caught it without it? **Check:** you can read the tool's accusation fluently. Revert.
7. **Measure & debrief.** Zero leaks, zero errors, every path — the number is 0/0. _Ask:_ what does the arena make impossible that per-object `free` invites? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the valgrind-clean run across all paths; then the introduced-bug run where the checker catches it.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an arena allocator in C to actually see the memory model the higher-level languages hide. The core discipline is single-owner-per-allocation, which is what prevents leaks and double-frees, and the subtle bug was `realloc` moving a block and dangling every interior pointer. I proved the whole thing valgrind-clean, including failure paths."
- **Design review:** a peer uses scattered `malloc`/`free` where an arena fits. Discuss when bulk lifetime management beats individual frees — and when it doesn't.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been writing a memory allocator in C — the humbling part is that 'it works' means nothing until valgrind agrees; four whole classes of bug hide behind a passing test run."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** every GC'd `new` in JavaScript, every Python object, is doing _this_ underneath. Having built it by hand, what convenience are those languages selling you, and what's the receipt (name it for M16 and M17 too)?

## M19 — A Loop That Never Stops · a small game in C (Scratch optional) · (M)

> **Story.** As a builder who wants to live at the floor for a while, I want a small real-time terminal game in C, so that the game loop — steady time, non-blocking input, clean redraw — becomes muscle memory, with no leaks over a whole session. **MVP.** A playable Snake-class game: world updates on its own clock, responsive controls, flicker-free rendering. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - The world moves whether or not a key is pressed; controls never stall the loop.
> - The screen redraws cleanly each frame — no flicker, no leftovers.
> - valgrind-clean over a full play session.

**Anchors:** _The Machine Under Everything_ (memory and the machine); _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (the loop-that-never-stops idea, shared with M14 and M17).

### Briefing

A game is the friendliest hard project in programming, because it forces a dozen skills to cooperate at once and then _shows you_ whether they did. Under every game is the **game loop**: read input → update the world → draw the world → repeat, forever, fast enough to feel alive. Building a small one **in C** ties together everything from M18 — state, memory, pointers — with real-time input and output, in a language that won't catch you. The optional on-ramp: prototype the _logic_ in **Scratch** first (the rules, the state transitions, the win condition) where you can see it move without fighting memory and terminal I/O — then port the _thinking_ to C, where the machine reasserts itself. The center of gravity is **the game loop and mutable state**, running on the memory floor you just built.

### The trap

**The loop that blocks on input** — and terminal rendering that fights back. The obvious way to read a keypress _waits_ for one, which freezes the entire loop: nothing moves until the player presses a key, so there's no animation, no timing, no life. Making the loop keep running while _also_ checking for input (non-blocking input, raw terminal mode) is the thing every first game gets wrong. Its partner: redrawing the screen naively causes flicker and leftover garbage, because the terminal remembers what you drew last.

### The feature

> A small, playable real-time game in C — a terminal game is ideal (a Snake, a falling-block puzzle, a grid dodger). It must have a running game loop that updates on a steady clock _without_ waiting for input, responsive controls that don't freeze the action, a clean screen render each frame, and — because it's C — no memory leaks. (Optional first step: build the rules and state machine in Scratch to validate the _logic_, then implement in C.)

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The game world updates on its own timing — things move whether or not a key is pressed.
- Controls respond promptly without stalling the loop.
- The screen redraws cleanly each frame — no flicker, no leftover characters.
- valgrind-clean: the game leaks no memory over a full play session (reaches back to M18).

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Scratch-prototype-first vs straight-to-C.** Options: model the logic in Scratch, then port · start in C. _Axis:_ de-risking the _rules_ separately from the _systems_ vs one integrated grind. _Forcing:_ Are the game's _rules_ the uncertain part (prototype them cheaply first) or is the _C machinery_ the whole challenge (go straight in)? Which unknown is bigger for _you_?
2. **Blocking vs non-blocking input.** Options: wait-for-keypress · poll input without blocking (raw/non-canonical mode). _Axis:_ trivial vs a game that actually animates. _Forcing:_ If reading a key _waits_, what happens to everything else in the loop meanwhile? This _is_ the trap — how do you check for input and keep moving?
3. **Rendering strategy.** Options: clear-and-redraw the whole screen each frame · update only changed cells. _Axis:_ simplicity vs no-flicker performance. _Forcing:_ Redrawing everything every frame — does it flicker, and what does the terminal do with what you drew _last_ frame if you don't clear it right?
4. **Timing / frame pacing.** Options: run as fast as possible · fixed timestep (sleep to a target frame time) · time-based updates. _Axis:_ uncontrolled vs steady, portable speed. _Forcing:_ On a fast machine your game runs unplayably fast — how do you pace it so it feels the same everywhere? (Same delta-time idea as M17.)
5. **State representation.** Options: scattered globals · a struct holding the game state, passed explicitly. _Axis:_ quick vs clean ownership (and testability). _Forcing:_ Where does "the world" live, and who's allowed to mutate it? How does that interact with the memory discipline from M18?

### Research targets

- _What is a game loop?_ — **25 min.** Done when you can state input→update→render→repeat and why it must not block. _Seed:_ search "game loop pattern" — read one canonical write-up.
- _Non-blocking / raw terminal input in C._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain how to read a keypress _without_ waiting (the terminal-mode change that enables it). _Seed:_ a "raw mode terminal C" primer.
- _Terminal rendering without flicker._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe clearing/redrawing cleanly (and what ANSI escape codes or a curses-style approach buys you).
- _Frame pacing / fixed timestep._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain sleeping to a target frame time so speed is consistent.

### Out of scope (fence)

Graphics libraries and real 2D/3D rendering (that's M17's world), sound, networked multiplayer, a game engine, save files, complex physics. Keep it small and terminal-based. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Play a full session. **The number:** does it run at a steady, playable pace with responsive input and a clean screen (no flicker, no garbage)? Then run the session under **valgrind** — zero leaks over real play (the M18 discipline, now in a program that runs for minutes).

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My game freezes waiting for a key — nothing moves until I press something</summary>

**Orient:** your input read is _blocking_ — it halts the whole loop until a key arrives, so the loop can't update the world in the meantime.

**Approach:** you need input that returns _immediately_ whether or not a key was pressed, so the loop can check "any key?" and then get on with updating and rendering regardless. On a terminal that means switching it out of its default line-buffered ("canonical") mode into a raw/non-blocking mode so a read doesn't wait. The loop then becomes: poll input (maybe nothing) → update world on the clock → render → repeat. Remember to restore the terminal's normal mode when the game exits, or you'll leave the user's shell in a weird state.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why does the obvious way to read a key freeze the world, and what has to be true instead? **Check:** blocking explained; the loop's three jobs (input, update, render) named.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ a fixed-timestep loop sketched in pseudocode on paper (yours, not copied).
3. **Commit (DSA).** Scratch-first or straight to C, input strategy, render strategy, timing. **Check:** written. If Scratch: rules prototyped in an afternoon, lessons noted.
4. **Build — the heartbeat.** A loop that moves one thing on its own clock, no input yet. **Check:** motion is steady and frame-paced, not CPU-speed-paced.
5. **Build — the hands.** Non-blocking input steering the thing. **Check:** holding a key doesn't stall motion; no key still means motion.
6. **Build — the game.** Rules, collisions, score, game over — flicker-free redraws. **Check:** a full session plays cleanly; the terminal is restored on exit.
7. **Measure & debrief.** valgrind over a whole session: zero leaks. _Ask:_ where did M18's ownership discipline show up here uninvited? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** play it live (movement independent of input, responsive controls, clean render); show the valgrind-clean session.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a real-time terminal game in C. The lesson that transfers everywhere is the game loop — input, update, render, repeat — and the trap is blocking input, which freezes the loop; I used non-blocking raw-mode input so the world keeps moving. Plus fixed-timestep pacing so it plays the same on any machine, and it's valgrind-clean over a full session."
- **Design review:** a peer's game runs at different speeds on different machines. Explain frame-rate dependence and the time-based/fixed-timestep fix (same idea you'd defend in M17).
- **Meetup (30s):** "I wrote a little game in C — the eye-opener was that reading a keypress the obvious way freezes everything, so the whole trick is non-blocking input keeping the loop alive."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** you've now built three never-stopping loops — the IIoT stream (M14), the 3D render loop (M17), and this game loop — all on the memory floor of M18. What's the shared skeleton of all three, and what does each add on top?

# PART VII — Range

_Six standalones, each a distinct territory a job description might demand. They don't share a theme — that's the point; range is the deliverable. But three of them reach back: the search parser and the filesystem tool close the tree thread, and the mobile app closes the contract thread by consuming your `incidents` API from a third kind of client._

## M20 — From Text to Tree · a boolean search-query parser · (M)

> **Story.** As an engineer who keeps meeting little languages, I want a boolean search-query parser with correct precedence and honest errors, so that tokenize → parse → evaluate is a move I own, not a library I import. **MVP.** `AND`/`OR`/`NOT`, parentheses, quoted phrases; matching document sets; malformed queries refused with a clear message. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - `a OR b AND c` matches per correct precedence; parentheses override it.
> - `NOT` and quoted phrases compose with the rest.
> - Unbalanced parens and dangling operators are refused usefully; hand-verified queries produce exactly the expected sets.

**Anchors:** the DSA docs (`DATA_STRUCTURES___ALGORITHMS.md`, `leetcode_thinking_toolbox.md`); the tree thread from M11.

### Briefing

A real search box understands more than words: `(cat OR dog) AND NOT fish`, `"exact phrase" OR bird`. Turning that flat string into something a computer can _evaluate_ is a compact, beautiful version of what every programming language does to your source code. It runs in three stages: **tokenize** (split the string into meaningful pieces — words, operators, parens), **parse** (arrange those tokens into a tree that captures structure and precedence), **evaluate** (walk the tree to decide what matches). The center of gravity is **turning flat text into a tree and walking it** — the same move as the BOM (M11) and the filesystem walk (M25), which is why it's here.

### The trap

**Operator precedence and nesting** — the reason you cannot just split on spaces. `a OR b AND c` does not mean `(a OR b) AND c`; by convention AND binds tighter, so it means `a OR (b AND c)` — and parentheses must be able to override that. A naive left-to-right or split-based approach gets simple queries right and silently mangles anything with mixed operators or nesting, which is _most real queries_.

### The feature

> A parser-evaluator for a boolean search grammar supporting: `AND`, `OR`, `NOT`, parentheses for grouping, and quoted phrases — with correct precedence. Given a query and a set of documents (or just a set of terms per document), it returns the matching set. It must reject malformed queries (unbalanced parentheses, dangling operators) with a clear error rather than crashing or guessing.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- `a OR b AND c` matches per correct precedence (AND before OR), and parentheses override it.
- `NOT` and quoted phrases work and compose with the rest.
- A malformed query (e.g. unbalanced parens) is refused with a useful message.
- A set of hand-verified queries produces exactly the expected match sets.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Recursive-descent parser vs shunting-yard vs a parser generator.** Options: hand-written recursive descent · Dijkstra's shunting-yard · a generator/library. _Axis:_ learning the mechanism and readable grammar vs clever compactness vs not writing it yourself. _Forcing:_ Which teaches you what a parser _is_? Which maps most directly onto the grammar's precedence rules so you can reason about it? (For this module, hand-writing is the point.)
2. **Grammar and precedence.** Options: define explicit precedence (NOT > AND > OR) · flat/ambiguous. _Axis:_ correct and predictable vs broken on mixed operators. _Forcing:_ What does `a AND b OR c AND d` mean, and does your grammar make that unambiguous? Where do parentheses fit in the precedence ladder?
3. **AST node design.** Options: a uniform node type with a kind field · distinct types per operator. _Axis:_ simplicity vs type-safety. _Forcing:_ What are your node kinds (AND/OR/NOT/TERM), and how does each know its children? Can you draw the tree for a nested query?
4. **Evaluation strategy.** Options: interpret the tree directly against each document · compile it once into a reusable predicate. _Axis:_ simplicity vs efficiency over many documents. _Forcing:_ If you evaluate the same query against a million documents, do you re-walk the tree a million times, or build the matcher once?
5. **Error handling.** Options: crash/guess on malformed input · detect and report precisely. _Axis:_ less code vs a usable tool. _Forcing:_ Unbalanced parens, an operator with no operand — where in tokenize/parse/evaluate does each get caught, and what do you tell the user?

### Research targets

- _Tokenize → parse → evaluate — the three stages._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain what each stage takes in and hands out, tab closed.
- _Recursive-descent parsing and precedence._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can describe how a grammar with precedence levels becomes a set of mutually-recursive parse functions. _Seed:_ search "recursive descent parser precedence" — one clear tutorial.
- _What an abstract syntax tree is._ — **25 min.** Done when you can draw the AST for `(a OR b) AND NOT c`.
- _(Alternative) the shunting-yard algorithm._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say how it uses a stack to respect precedence without recursion.

### Out of scope (fence)

Fuzzy matching, ranking/relevance scoring, stemming/lemmatization, a full query language with fields and ranges, actually building an inverted index (note — park; that's the search-engine rabbit hole). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run your hand-verified query suite (include precedence and nesting cases). **The number:** how many of the tricky cases produce the correct match set / correct AST? (All of them is the bar; each miss is a precedence or nesting bug to name.)

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>Precedence is beating me — AND and OR come out in the wrong order</summary>

**Orient:** precedence is expressed by the _shape of the grammar_ — lower-precedence operators sit _higher_ in the tree (parsed "outermost"), higher-precedence operators sit _deeper_.

**Approach:** in recursive descent, write one function per precedence level, each calling the _next-tighter_ level for its operands. Parse OR at the top: an OR-expression is a sequence of AND-expressions joined by OR. An AND-expression is a sequence of NOT-expressions joined by AND. A NOT-expression is an optional NOT before a primary. A primary is a term, a quoted phrase, or a parenthesized OR-expression (which is how parens re-enter at the top and override everything). The call chain _is_ the precedence.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why can't you split on spaces — what does `a OR b AND c` mean, and who decided? **Check:** precedence explained, plus what parentheses do to it.
2. **Research.** The targets. _Objective:_ tokenize → parse → evaluate distinguished, each stage's input and output named.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Parser strategy, grammar written down, AST node design, error policy. **Check:** the grammar exists on paper with precedence explicit — before any code.
4. **Build — the tokenizer.** Words, operators, parens, quoted phrases. **Check:** a nasty string tokenizes exactly as hand-predicted.
5. **Build — the parser.** Text to tree per your grammar. **Check:** `a OR b AND c` and its parenthesized override produce visibly different trees (print them).
6. **Build — the evaluator.** Trees against document sets, `NOT` and phrases composing. **Check:** the hand-verified query table matches exactly.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Feed it unbalanced parens and dangling operators. **Check:** refused with useful messages, never a crash or a guess. _Ask:_ where would this same shape appear next (M11's trees, M25's rules)? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** parse a nested query and print/draw its AST; run the tricky-query suite green.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a boolean search parser — tokenize, parse to an AST, evaluate. The heart of it is precedence: AND binds tighter than OR, which you get for free if the grammar nests correctly, and parens re-enter at the top level to override. A naive split-on-spaces can't express that."
- **Design review:** a peer built it with string splitting and it fails on `a OR b AND c`. Show the failing case and make the tree-parsing case.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I wrote a little query parser — turning `(cat OR dog) AND NOT fish` into a tree you can evaluate. It's a tiny compiler, and precedence is the whole trick."
- **Reach-back (tree thread — closing it with M11 and M25):** BOM traversal, this AST, and the filesystem walk are the same skeleton. Write the one sentence that names what all three share and what each adds.

## M21 — Where Things Are · an interactive GIS map · (M)

> **Story.** As a generalist who keeps seeing maps in job posts, I want an interactive map over a real GeoJSON dataset that stays responsive and doesn't get lucky about projections, so that coordinate order and Mercator distortion are decisions I made, not accidents. **MVP.** Real data plotted correctly, pan/zoom, click-for-detail, responsive at non-trivial feature counts. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Features render in geographically correct positions (spot-checked).
> - Pan, zoom, and click-for-detail work smoothly; the map stays responsive as features grow.
> - Any distance/area figure accounts for the projection — or you can say why it's approximate.

**Anchors:** the data-visualization instincts from your React/TypeScript work; general data-handling from the data books.

### Briefing

Geographic data is everywhere — deliveries, sensors, incidents, users — and putting it on a map is a distinct skill with one big conceptual hurdle: **the Earth is round and your screen is flat.** A GIS (geographic information system) map has to take positions on a sphere (latitude/longitude) and _project_ them onto flat pixels, which is a lossy, choice-laden transformation. Build an interactive map that plots real geographic data, and you learn coordinate systems, projections, and the standard data format (GeoJSON) that the whole ecosystem speaks. The center of gravity is **coordinate systems and projection** — turning where-on-Earth into where-on-screen.

### The trap

**Coordinate confusion and projection distortion.** Longitude/latitude vs latitude/longitude ordering is inconsistent across formats and libraries, so points land in the ocean when you swap them. And the near-universal Web Mercator projection _distorts area_ badly toward the poles (Greenland looks continent-sized), so distances and areas measured naively in projected space are wrong. The tell: your points are in the wrong place, or your "distance" is off by a lot at high latitudes.

### The feature

> An interactive map rendering a real geographic dataset (points and/or regions from a GeoJSON source): it plots the data in the correct locations, supports pan and zoom, and responds to interaction (click a feature for detail). It should stay responsive at a non-trivial number of features, and correctly handle the coordinate-ordering and projection concerns rather than getting lucky.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Features render in geographically correct positions (spot-check known locations).
- Pan, zoom, and click-for-detail work smoothly.
- The map stays responsive as feature count grows (you've made a rendering-approach decision, not just mapped everything).
- A distance or area computation (if you include one) accounts for the projection, or you can explain why your number is approximate.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Mapping library choice.** Options: Leaflet (raster tiles, simple) · Mapbox/MapLibre GL (vector, GPU) · D3-geo (custom projections, full control) · deck.gl (large-data WebGL). _Axis:_ simplicity vs performance vs projection control vs data scale. _Forcing:_ Do you need a slippy tile map, custom projections, or to render a hundred thousand points? Each answer points at a different tool.
2. **Raster tiles vs vector rendering.** Options: pre-rendered image tiles · vector data drawn client-side. _Axis:_ simplicity vs styling flexibility and crispness at any zoom. _Forcing:_ Do you need to restyle features on the fly or crisp scaling — or is a static basemap enough?
3. **Handling many features.** Options: render every feature · cluster nearby points · canvas/WebGL instead of SVG/DOM markers. _Axis:_ simplicity vs staying smooth. _Forcing:_ At what feature count do individual DOM markers start to lag, and what's your escape (clustering, or a different render layer)? (Echoes M17's performance floor.)
4. **Projection awareness.** Options: ignore it (use the library default) · understand what Web Mercator does to distance/area. _Axis:_ convenience vs correctness. _Forcing:_ Are you _measuring_ anything on the map? If so, does projected-pixel distance mean real-world distance? What breaks near the poles?
5. **Coordinate order discipline.** Options: trust it and debug when wrong · verify the [lng, lat] vs [lat, lng] convention up front. _Axis:_ faster vs not landing in the ocean. _Forcing:_ What order does GeoJSON use, what order does your library expect, and where do you convert?

### Research targets

- _Latitude/longitude and coordinate systems — the basics._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain lat/lng and the [lng, lat] ordering gotcha, tab closed.
- _What GeoJSON is._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe its feature/geometry structure and coordinate order.
- _Map projections and why Web Mercator distorts._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain, in plain words, why you can't flatten a sphere without lying, and what Mercator trades away. _Seed:_ search "why all maps are wrong Mercator" — one good explainer.
- _(If measuring) great-circle / haversine distance._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say why straight-line pixel distance isn't real distance.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full geospatial analysis (buffering, spatial joins), routing/navigation, real-time tracking, custom tile servers, 3D terrain, heavy PostGIS work (note — park; it's the deep end). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Plot a dataset with known locations and spot-check them. **The number:** do features land where they actually are on Earth, and does the map stay responsive at your feature count? If you compute a distance, compare it to a known real value.

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ lat/lon or lon/lat — who uses which, and what does the mistake look like on screen? **Check:** "points in the ocean" traced to its cause; Mercator's area lie stated.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ GeoJSON's structure and coordinate order recited from memory.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Library, raster vs vector, many-features strategy, coordinate discipline. **Check:** written; the discipline is a rule ("convert at the boundary, once"), not a hope.
4. **Build — the base.** Map, pan, zoom, one hand-placed marker somewhere you know. **Check:** it's where it should be.
5. **Build — the data.** The real GeoJSON rendered. **Check:** spot-checks land correctly; click-for-detail works.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Scale to the full dataset. _Ask:_ where does it stutter first, and is your fix rendering-level or data-level? **Check:** responsive at full count, and you can say why.
7. **Measure & debrief.** If you compute distance or area: verify against a known value and account for the projection. _Ask:_ what would break at 10× the features? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the interactive map with correctly-placed features, pan/zoom/click; if included, a distance that matches reality.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an interactive GIS map from GeoJSON. The conceptual thing I'd flag is projection — you can't flatten a sphere without distortion, so Web Mercator lies about area toward the poles, and pixel-distance isn't real distance. Plus the coordinate-ordering gotcha that puts points in the ocean if you get [lng, lat] backwards."
- **Design review:** a peer renders 100k individual markers and it lags. Discuss clustering and WebGL layers as the fix (the M17 performance instinct).
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been playing with GIS mapping — the fun rabbit hole is that every flat map of the round Earth is 'wrong,' and you pick _which_ way it's wrong."
- **Reach-back (data thread):** this is the same data-visualization instinct as your charts, but the coordinate space is a sphere. What does the sphere force you to decide that a bar chart never does?

## M22 — The Update-Safe Customization · a WordPress feature in PHP · (S)

> **Story.** As a freelancer who meets WordPress in the wild, I want one genuine custom feature built the update-safe way, so that hooks, sanitize-in/escape-out, and nonces are reflexes and the next core update can't erase my work. **MVP.** A small plugin (or child-theme addition) doing something real through WordPress's APIs. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - No core files edited; a core update doesn't break or erase the feature.
> - All input sanitized in, all output escaped out.
> - Every state-changing action is nonce-protected and capability-checked.

**Anchors:** the security material from M4 and your Security+ track; the protective-vs-extractive-friction framework (relevant to how you scope client work).

### Briefing

A large share of the freelance web market runs on WordPress, and the paying work is rarely "install a theme" — it's _customizing_ WordPress in PHP without breaking it, and without opening a security hole. WordPress's whole extensibility rests on a **hook system**: actions (do something at a certain moment) and filters (modify a value as it passes through), which let your code run at the right time _without editing WordPress itself_. Building one real custom feature the update-safe, secure way is the difference between a freelancer who gets called back and one who gets a site defaced. The center of gravity is **the hook system, PHP, and doing it so a core update doesn't wipe your work or a script kiddie doesn't own the site.**

### The trap

**The insecure, update-unsafe customization.** Two failure modes, both common and both costly. Editing core or theme files directly means the next WordPress/theme update erases your work — or you refuse to update and rot into a vulnerable install. And WordPress is the most-attacked CMS on Earth, so unsanitized input, unescaped output, and unprotected form actions are how sites get SQL-injected, XSS'd, or CSRF'd. The tell: it works, it's live, and nobody sanitized anything.

### The feature

> One genuine custom feature built the right way — a small plugin (or a child-theme addition) that does something real: a custom form that stores and displays data, a custom post type with a admin screen, or a shortcode/block that renders dynamic content. It must use WordPress's hooks and data APIs (not raw file edits), sanitize all input, escape all output, protect any state-changing action (nonce), and check user capability where relevant — and survive a core update untouched.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The feature is packaged as a plugin or child-theme code — **no core files edited**.
- All user input is sanitized on the way in; all output is escaped on the way out.
- Any form/action that changes data is nonce-protected and capability-checked.
- Updating WordPress core does not break or erase the feature.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Plugin vs child theme vs must-use plugin.** Options: standalone plugin · child theme addition · mu-plugin. _Axis:_ portability and separation vs theme-coupling vs always-on. _Forcing:_ Is this feature about _presentation_ (child theme) or _functionality_ (plugin) — and which survives a theme switch?
2. **WordPress APIs vs raw PHP/SQL.** Options: `WP_Query`, hooks, the options/meta APIs · hand-rolled SQL and globals. _Axis:_ safe, update-proof, idiomatic vs fighting the framework and reintroducing injection risk. _Forcing:_ Why is `$wpdb->prepare` (or the higher APIs) safer than string-concatenated SQL? What does doing it "the WordPress way" protect?
3. **Data storage.** Options: custom post type · post meta · custom table · the options API. _Axis:_ leveraging WordPress's built-ins vs control. _Forcing:_ Does your data look like "content" (custom post type), "settings" (options), or something WordPress's models don't fit (custom table — and now you own the migration)?
4. **Security surface.** Options: trust input, echo directly · sanitize-escape-nonce-capability throughout. _Axis:_ speed vs not getting the client hacked. _Forcing:_ For each input, what's the sanitizer? For each output, what's the escaper? For each state change, where's the nonce and the capability check? (This is the M4 security mindset in a new house.)
5. **Classic vs block (Gutenberg).** Options: shortcode/classic PHP · a Gutenberg block. _Axis:_ simplicity and broad compatibility vs modern editor integration. _Forcing:_ Who edits this site, and do they need it inside the block editor?

### Research targets

- _WordPress hooks: actions vs filters._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain the difference (do-something vs modify-a-value) with one example each. _Seed:_ the WordPress Plugin Handbook, hooks section.
- _Sanitize-on-input, escape-on-output — the WordPress security model._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name the right function class for a couple of input and output types. _Seed:_ the handbook's "Securing Input/Output."
- _Nonces and capability checks._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain what a nonce protects (CSRF) and what a capability check protects (authorization) — the M4 concepts, WordPress-flavored.
- _Why child themes / plugins keep customizations update-safe._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain what editing core costs you at the next update.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full theme development from scratch, WooCommerce/e-commerce, multisite, performance/caching plugins, the REST API deep-dive, page builders. Keep it to one clean, secure feature. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run a basic security self-audit on your feature (inputs sanitized? outputs escaped? actions nonce'd and capability-checked?), then update WordPress core. **The number:** how many of the four security checks pass, and does the feature survive the update intact? (All four and yes is the bar.)

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what are the two deaths — the update that erases you, and the input you trusted — and where does each enter? **Check:** both told as one-sentence incidents.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ actions vs filters distinguished; sanitize-in/escape-out recited as a rule with its two moments.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Plugin vs child theme, storage choice, security surface. **Check:** written; every place user data enters or exits is listed.
4. **Build — the skeleton.** The plugin activates, hooks fire, nothing else. **Check:** activate/deactivate cleanly on your R13 install.
5. **Build — the feature.** The real thing, through WordPress APIs only. **Check:** it works; no core or parent-theme file has a diff.
6. **Build — the armor.** Sanitize every input, escape every output, nonce + capability-check every action. **Check:** a hostile paste (script tag) renders inert; a replayed form without its nonce is refused.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Update WordPress core (or reinstall it). **Check:** the feature survives untouched. _Ask:_ which WordPress API surprised you most, and what does it protect? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the feature working; walk your sanitize/escape/nonce points; update core and show it still works.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a custom WordPress feature as a plugin, using hooks rather than editing core so it's update-safe, and I sanitized input, escaped output, and nonce-protected the state-changing action. WordPress is the most-attacked CMS, so that discipline is the actual job, not an add-on."
- **Design review:** a client's previous developer edited theme files directly and echoed `$_POST` unescaped. Explain both the update-safety and the security problems, constructively.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been doing WordPress the right way — a custom plugin with proper sanitizing and nonces — because the freelance reality is that 'it works' and 'it's secure and update-safe' are very different bars."
- **Reach-back (security thread):** the nonce here and the token in M4 both defend against a class of attack. Which class does each defend, and where do they overlap?

## M23 — Write Once, Debug Twice · a minimal React Native app · (S)

> **Story.** As an engineer extending the contract to a fourth surface, I want a two-screen React Native app consuming my M1 API on both platforms, so that mobile's real differences — navigation, touch, virtualized lists, platform splits — stop being rumors. **MVP.** A list screen fetching incidents, a detail screen on tap, running on iOS and Android. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - One codebase renders acceptably on both platforms.
> - Tap-to-detail uses real mobile navigation; long lists use a virtualized list.
> - At least one platform difference is explicitly handled, and you can point to it.

**Anchors:** your React/TypeScript foundation (the function-first guide); the contract thread — this consumes your M1 `incidents` API.

### Briefing

Your React knowledge _mostly_ transfers to mobile — and the places it _doesn't_ are exactly what this module is for. React Native lets you write mobile apps for iOS and Android in React, but the runtime is not a browser: there's no DOM, no CSS as you know it, navigation works differently, and the two platforms diverge in ways you must handle. The goal here is deliberately narrow — build just enough to _feel_ those differences, not to ship an app. Point it at your existing `incidents` API so the backend is already done and you can focus entirely on the mobile-vs-web delta. The center of gravity is precisely that delta: **navigation, touch, layout-without-CSS, and two platforms from one codebase.**

### The trap

**Assuming web habits transfer** — and platform divergence. There's no `div`, no `className` cascade, no CSS Grid; layout is flexbox-only with density-independent units, styling is JavaScript objects, and scrolling a long list naively (mapping an array) tanks performance where the web would shrug. And "write once, run anywhere" is really "write once, _debug twice_" — a gesture, a safe-area inset, or a component can behave differently on iOS vs Android. The tell: you reached for a web pattern and it either doesn't exist or silently misbehaves on one platform.

### The feature

> A minimal two-screen mobile app running on _both_ iOS and Android: a list screen that fetches and displays incidents from your M1 API, and a detail screen you navigate to on tap. Keep features to the minimum that surfaces the mobile/web differences — navigation, touch handling, mobile layout, and at least one spot where you handle the two platforms differently.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- The same codebase runs on both an iOS and an Android simulator, rendering acceptably on each.
- Tapping a list item navigates to a detail screen (real mobile navigation, not a web route).
- The list scrolls smoothly (you've used a virtualized list, not a naive map, if the data is non-trivial).
- At least one platform difference is explicitly handled, and you can point to it.

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **Expo vs bare React Native.** Options: Expo (managed, fast start) · bare (full native control). _Axis:_ speed and simplicity vs native-module freedom. _Forcing:_ For a minimal learning app, do you need custom native code, or is the fastest path to _both_ simulators the goal? (Expo, almost certainly — but name why.)
2. **Navigation model.** Options: a stack navigator · tabs · both. _Axis:_ matching the app's shape. _Forcing:_ List-to-detail is a _push_, not a URL change — which navigator expresses "drill in and back out," and how is that different from web routing?
3. **Styling approach.** Options: `StyleSheet` objects · a styling library. _Axis:_ the platform primitive vs familiar ergonomics. _Forcing:_ There's no CSS file — where do styles live, and what layout tools do you _not_ have (grid) that you leaned on in M15?
4. **List rendering.** Options: map the array into components · a virtualized `FlatList`. _Axis:_ simplicity vs performance on real data. _Forcing:_ What happens to memory and scroll performance when you render five hundred rows the naive way? What does virtualization do about it? (Same instinct as M6/M17 — don't do unbounded work.)
5. **Handling platform differences.** Options: assume parity · detect and branch (Platform API / platform files). _Axis:_ simplicity vs correctness on both. _Forcing:_ Where do iOS and Android diverge in your app (safe areas, back behavior, a component's look), and how do you handle it cleanly rather than with scattered checks?

### Research targets

- _How React Native differs from React-for-web._ — **25 min.** Done when you can list the big ones (no DOM, native components, flexbox-only styling, navigation) tab closed. _Seed:_ the React Native "core components" intro.
- _Mobile navigation patterns (stack/tab)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain a stack push/pop vs a web route change.
- _Styling in React Native and the layout model._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what's different from CSS and what layout tools you have.
- _`FlatList` and list virtualization._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why it stays smooth where a mapped array doesn't.

### Out of scope (fence)

Native modules, push notifications, device APIs (camera/GPS), app-store deployment, offline sync, complex state management, animations. Minimal is the mandate — the point is to _feel the delta_, not ship. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run it on both an iOS and an Android simulator side by side. **The number:** does the same code render and behave acceptably on both, and does the list scroll smoothly? Note (out loud) every place the two platforms diverged and how you handled it.

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ which three web habits will betray you first here? **Check:** no div, flexbox-only, and the naive-list tax named with their replacements.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ stack vs tab navigation distinguished; virtualization explained in one sentence.
3. **Commit (DSA).** Expo vs bare, navigation model, styling, list strategy. **Check:** written; the platform-difference you'll handle explicitly is chosen.
4. **Build — the pipe.** The app fetches M1 incidents on both simulators. **Check:** real data renders on iOS and Android (LAN IP, not localhost).
5. **Build — the list.** Virtualized, styled, acceptable on both. **Check:** smooth scroll on a non-trivial dataset.
6. **Build — the navigation.** Tap → detail screen, mobile-native. **Check:** back gestures behave correctly on each platform.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Handle your chosen platform divergence explicitly. **Check:** you can point at the line and say what differs. _Ask:_ what did the contract (M1) give you for free here? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the app on both simulators; the tap-to-detail navigation; the smooth list; the platform-difference you handled.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a minimal React Native app on top of my existing incidents API, specifically to feel where web React stops transferring — flexbox-only styling with no CSS, real stack navigation instead of routes, virtualized lists, and handling an iOS/Android divergence. 'Write once' is really 'write once, debug twice.'"
- **Design review:** a peer rendered a long list by mapping the array and it's janky on device. Explain virtualization as the fix (the same don't-do-unbounded-work instinct from the web).
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been porting my React instincts to mobile with React Native — the interesting part is everything that _doesn't_ carry over: no DOM, no CSS grid, and two platforms to keep happy."
- **Reach-back (contract thread — closing it):** your `incidents` contract has now been served by Spring, re-served on Postgres, reshaped as GraphQL, guarded by auth, pushed over a WebSocket, and now consumed by a mobile client. Name the one property of that original contract that made every one of those clients easier to build.

## M24 — Data That Can Hurt · a health-tech feature done responsibly · (M)

> **Story.** As an engineer who may touch health tech, I want a small vital-signs feature done the responsible way — FHIR-compatible shape, validated units, controlled access, clean logs — so that I know where ordinary CRUD instincts become dangerous and where my build's boundary sits. **MVP.** A personal-metric tracker modeling Patient + Observation, validating plausibility on the way in. **Acceptance criteria.**
>
> - Records conform to a FHIR-compatible structure (or you can show the mapping).
> - Implausible or wrong-unit values are caught, not stored; sensitive fields never appear in logs.
> - Access is controlled, and you can name what would still be missing for production.

**Anchors:** the security/privacy mindset from M4; the honest-data discipline from M9 (you're not claiming clinical expertise); _Where the Data Lives_ on data modeling.

### Briefing

Health tech is a domain where getting the engineering _right_ is a safety issue, not just a quality one — and it has its own vocabulary of constraints: sensitive personal data with legal protections, interoperability standards so systems can safely exchange records, and a correctness bar where a unit error or a mislabeled field can harm a person. **FHIR** is the modern standard for representing and exchanging health data — a shared shape so a record from one system means the same thing in another. Building a small feature that handles health data _responsibly_ — standards-aware, privacy-aware, correctness-first — teaches what makes this domain different. The center of gravity is **treating health data as sensitive and standardized, not as ordinary rows.**

An honesty note (the M9 discipline again): this makes you fluent in the _engineering posture_ health tech demands. You'd say "I built a FHIR-shaped health-data feature with privacy and validation built in" — not "I have clinical or regulatory expertise."

### The trap

**Treating health data like any other data.** The ordinary-CRUD instinct — store it plainly, log freely, validate loosely, invent your own schema — is exactly wrong here. Sensitive fields logged in plaintext, no access control, no audit trail, home-grown formats no other system can read, and unchecked units (was that dose in mg or g?) are the failure modes, and in this domain they range from a compliance violation to a genuine harm. The tell: you handled a blood-pressure reading the same way you'd handle a to-do item.

### The feature

> A small feature that ingests, stores, and displays health data the responsible way: model the records in a **FHIR-compatible shape** (e.g. an Observation like a vital sign, or a simple Patient + Observation pair), validate units and plausible ranges on the way in, control access to the data, and keep sensitive fields out of plaintext logs. A personal-metric tracker (weight, blood pressure, glucose) is a fine, concrete vehicle.

Acceptance criteria (observable):

- Records conform to a FHIR-compatible structure (validates against the standard, or you can show the mapping).
- Units are explicit and validated; an implausible or wrong-unit value is caught, not stored blindly.
- Access to the data is controlled (ties to M4); sensitive fields do not appear in logs.
- You can explain what would need to change to be actually production/regulation-ready (showing you know the boundary of what you built).

### Decision map

Write your DSA before building.

1. **FHIR-compatible vs roll-your-own schema.** Options: model on FHIR resources · invent your own shape. _Axis:_ interoperability and rigor vs freedom. _Forcing:_ If another system had to read your data tomorrow, which choice makes that possible? What does a standard buy you beyond "looks professional"?
2. **Units and validation.** Options: store a bare number · store value + unit with range/plausibility checks. _Axis:_ simplicity vs safety. _Forcing:_ Is "120" a blood pressure, a weight, or a glucose reading — and in what unit? What catches a value that's physically impossible or off by a factor of ten?
3. **Privacy posture.** Options: store and log freely · minimize, access-control, keep sensitive data out of logs. _Axis:_ convenience vs not leaking health data. _Forcing:_ Which fields are sensitive, where do they currently appear (database, logs, error messages), and what's the minimum exposure you can operate with? (The M4 mindset, raised to a legal bar.)
4. **Auditability.** Options: none · record who accessed/changed what. _Axis:_ simplicity vs the accountability health data demands. _Forcing:_ If someone asked "who viewed this record and when," could your system answer? (Same append-only instinct as M13's shop-floor history.)
5. **Knowing your boundary.** Options: claim it's production-ready · document what's _missing_ for real compliance. _Axis:_ overclaiming vs credibility. _Forcing:_ What did you deliberately _not_ build (real auth-grade security, encryption at rest, full regulatory compliance), and can you name it honestly?

### Research targets

- _What is FHIR and what problem does it solve?_ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain "a shared shape for health data so systems interoperate," and name a resource type like Observation. _Seed:_ the HL7 FHIR overview (read the intro, park the depth).
- _Why health data has special handling requirements._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name sensitivity, interoperability, and correctness as the three pillars, without needing to be a compliance lawyer.
- _Units and safe validation of clinical-style values._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe validating value + unit + plausible range.
- _Privacy-by-design basics (minimization, access control, not logging sensitive data)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can list what _not_ to do with sensitive fields.

### Out of scope (fence)

Actual regulatory compliance (HIPAA/GDPR implementation), real encryption-at-rest engineering, clinical decision logic, EHR integration, medical accuracy claims. You're building the _posture_, and naming the boundary is part of the deliverable. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Validate a record against the FHIR shape and run your unit/range checks with good and bad values. **The number:** does a valid record conform and an invalid/implausible one get rejected — and can you grep your logs to confirm no sensitive field leaked? (Conforms, rejects, and clean logs is the bar.)

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ what makes health data different in kind, not just degree — and what does the ordinary-CRUD instinct get wrong first? **Check:** three concrete harms named (logged vitals, no access control, homemade schema drift).
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ FHIR's problem-solved stated; an Observation's core fields sketched from the spec.
3. **Commit (DSA).** FHIR-compatible vs roll-your-own, unit strategy, privacy posture, audit plan. **Check:** written; "what I am not building" is a section, not a footnote.
4. **Build — the shape.** Patient + Observation modeled, stored. **Check:** a record validates against the standard, or your mapping table says exactly where it diverges.
5. **Build — the gate.** Plausibility and unit validation on the way in. **Check:** a 400-kg weight and a mmHg-vs-kPa confusion are both caught, not stored.
6. **Build — the walls.** Access control (M4's machinery) and clean logs. **Check:** an unauthorized request is refused; grep the logs — no vitals anywhere.
7. **Measure & debrief.** The audit trail answers who-saw-what. _Ask:_ name three things still missing before this could touch real patients — knowing the boundary is the deliverable. Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** a conforming record; a wrong-unit value getting caught; logs with no sensitive data; and your spoken list of what's _not_ production-ready.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a health-metric feature modeled on FHIR so it's interoperable, with unit-and-range validation because a wrong-unit value is a safety issue, access control, and no sensitive data in logs. I'd be clear this is the responsible _engineering posture_ — I'm not claiming clinical or regulatory expertise, and I can tell you exactly what a real deployment would still need."
- **Design review:** a peer stored vitals as bare numbers in a custom schema with verbose logging. Raise interoperability, unit safety, and privacy — as a colleague, correctness-first.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been learning what makes health-tech engineering different — modeling data in FHIR and treating it as genuinely sensitive, because in this domain a unit bug isn't a typo, it's a hazard."
- **Reach-back (honest-verb + security threads):** this leans on M4's access control and M9's honest-data posture. Write the one sentence you'd say to a health-tech hiring manager that claims exactly what you built and nothing more.
