# PART A — Capture & Text

_Two small modules that are secretly the seed of every tools-for-thought project you'll ever build: a place to put words that persists them, and the browser APIs that let you grab words the user has already produced. The instruments thread, from zero._

---

## M26 — The Simplest Honest Loop · a notetaking feature · (S)

> **Story.** As a builder of tools-for-thought, I want a text field whose submissions persist and display newest-first, so that I have the irreducible seed every notes and annotation tool is built from — and I meet stored XSS where it actually lives. **MVP.** A textarea → submit → store the note with a server timestamp → render all notes newest-first, escaped. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - A submitted note survives a full page reload (it's in a database, not memory).
> - Notes render newest-first, and the ordering comes from the _query_, not a client-side reverse (so it survives pagination).
> - A note whose text contains HTML or a `<script>` tag renders as _literal text_ — it does not execute or alter the page.
> - An empty state (no notes yet) is shown distinctly.

**Anchors:** M1/M2 (you built the _contract_ version of a persisted resource; this is its plainest, most human form); _Where the Data Lives_; M4's security mindset applied to displayed input.

### Briefing

This is the smallest complete full-stack loop there is, and every app you admire is secretly made of it repeated: a place to type, a submission that gets _stored_ so it survives a refresh, and a display that reads it back in a sensible order. Building it deliberately makes the round trip visible — a textarea's text becomes an HTTP request becomes a database row becomes, on the next load, rendered output. The center of gravity is **the round trip and the ordering** — and, quietly, what happens when the thing you store is text a stranger wrote.

### The trap

Two, and they're why a "trivial" notes app earns a module. **Stored XSS:** you save whatever the user typed and render it straight back, so a note containing `<script>` (or an `onerror` image tag) _executes_ when displayed — for you and anyone else who loads it. And **ordering in the wrong place:** "newest first" feels like "reverse the array in JavaScript," which works with three notes and breaks the instant you paginate, because you're only reversing the _page_ you fetched.

### The build

A textarea, a submit, persistence with a timestamp, and a reverse-chronological display rendered _safely_. The note shape is a spec you may take as given: `{ id, body, created_at }`, list ordered by `created_at DESC`. Create-and-list only.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Where ordering happens.** Options: `ORDER BY created_at DESC` in the query · fetch-then-reverse in JS. _Axis:_ correctness at any scale vs momentary convenience. _Forcing:_ If you show ten per page, does reversing the fetched array give the newest ten, or the reverse of an arbitrary ten?
2. **Escape on output vs sanitize on input.** Options: store raw, escape when rendering · clean the text before storing. _Axis:_ preserving what the user wrote vs mangling it early. _Forcing:_ If you sanitize on input and later want the raw text elsewhere, is it gone? Why is escape-at-the-moment-of-output more robust? (This is the trap.)
3. **Timestamp source.** Options: server sets `created_at` · client sends its time. _Axis:_ trustworthy ordering vs client control. _Forcing:_ With wrong client clocks, whose time orders the list correctly? (Reaches back to M2's `timestamptz`.)
4. **Optimistic vs confirmed display.** Options: show the note immediately · wait for the server's success. _Axis:_ snappy feel vs never showing a note that failed to save. _Forcing:_ If the save fails after you've shown it, what does the user believe happened?

### Research targets

- _Stored XSS, and why "escape on output" is the fix._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why rendering user text unescaped runs their code, and where escaping belongs. _Seed:_ the OWASP XSS prevention cheat sheet (output-encoding section).
- _`ORDER BY` and why not to sort in application memory._ — **25 min.** Done when you can state why ordering belongs in the query, especially with a limit.
- _The `created_at` timestamp pattern._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say who should set it and why.

### Out of scope (fence)

Editing/deleting, rich text or markdown (which _reintroduces_ XSS harder — note, park), search, tags, authentication. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Submit a note whose text is `<b>hi</b>` or an image tag with an `onerror`. **The number:** does it render as visible literal text (safe) or does the formatting/script take effect (vulnerable)? Then add a note, reload, and confirm the newest sits at the top — proving the order came from the query.

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ where does stored XSS actually live in this feature, and why does client-side reversing break under pagination? **Check:** both traps explained before any code.
2. **Research.** The three targets. _Objective:_ escape-on-output justified against sanitize-on-input in one paragraph.
3. **Commit (DSA).** The four decisions, written. **Check:** each has a rejected alternative and a reason.
4. **Build — the loop.** Textarea → store with timestamp → render the list. **Check:** a note survives a full reload.
5. **Build — the order.** Newest-first from the query itself. **Check:** the ordering survives a `LIMIT` — the newest ten, not the reverse of an arbitrary ten.
6. **Walk into the trap.** Submit `<b>hi</b>` and an `onerror` image tag. **Check:** both render as literal text, inert.
7. **Measure & debrief.** The empty state shows distinctly; reload keeps order. _Ask:_ what did M1's contract discipline add that this plain loop lacks — and what does this loop teach that M1 hid? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** submit a script-containing note and show it rendered inert; reload and show newest-first surviving.

- **Interview (90s):** "It's a notes feature, but the two non-trivial parts are the ones I'd flag: I escape on output so a note containing a script tag can't run — that's stored XSS, and it lives in exactly this feature — and I order newest-first in the query, not with a client-side reverse, because reversing only flips the page you fetched once you paginate."
- **Design review:** a peer sanitizes on input and reverses client-side. Walk through "the raw text is gone" and "pagination breaks the reverse," constructively.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I built the simplest full-stack loop — type, store, show newest-first — and used it to actually understand stored XSS, which hides in every notes app."
- **Reach-back (instruments + data-lifecycle threads):** this is the humblest ancestor of dev-suite and every tool-for-thought you want. What's the same round trip you'd reuse, and what did M1's _contract_ discipline add on top of this plain version?

---

## M27 — Grab What They Highlighted · selected text & clipboard in JS · (S)

> **Story.** As a tools-for-thought builder, I want to capture the user's text selection and read/write the clipboard, so that I have the capture primitives under every highlighter and clip-to-notes feature — handled on the browser's security terms, not fighting them. **MVP.** Read the current selection (its text and position); copy text to and read text from the system clipboard via the async API, on a user gesture, handling a denied permission. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Highlighting text — even across element boundaries — yields the exact selected text.
> - A copy action lands text on the _real_ system clipboard (verify by pasting into another app).
> - A paste action reads the clipboard back into the page, on a user gesture.
> - Clipboard access without a user gesture (or when denied) fails _safely_ with a handled state, not an uncaught crash.

**Anchors:** M16 (text as a first-class concern); the "protective vs extractive friction" framework (the clipboard's permission gate is _protective_ friction — it buys the user a security property); your tools-for-thought instincts.

### Briefing

Two browser capabilities turn a passive page into something you can _capture from_, and they're the raw material under every annotation tool. The **Selection API** tells you what the user highlighted — the text, and where it is, as a `Range` object. The **Clipboard API** reads from and writes to the system clipboard. The center of gravity is **capturing a selection as a structured Range, and the deliberately-gated clipboard** — because the browser will _not_ let you read the clipboard silently, and understanding _why_ is half the module.

### The trap

The **clipboard permission and security model**, which trips people who expect to just call `read()`. The clipboard is a cross-application secret (your passwords pass through it), so browsers gate access behind a user gesture and a permission, and arbitrary reads are _supposed_ to fail — code that assumes silent access breaks correctly. Add the legacy confusion: the old `document.execCommand('copy')` is deprecated in favor of the async `navigator.clipboard`, so tutorials disagree. And Selection `Range`s are fiddly — a selection can span multiple DOM nodes, so "the selected text" isn't always a simple string grab.

### The build

A capture surface: (a) on selection, read the selection's text and, ideally, position, and do something visible with it; (b) a copy action writing text to the clipboard via the async API, and a paste action reading it — both tied to a user gesture, both handling a denied permission. No backend.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Async Clipboard API vs legacy `execCommand`.** Options: `navigator.clipboard` · `document.execCommand`. _Axis:_ the modern, secure, supported path vs older-browser reach. _Forcing:_ Which are vendors moving toward, and what does the async one require that the old one didn't (a gesture, a permission)?
2. **Where clipboard access is triggered.** Options: on an explicit user action · on load or a timer. _Axis:_ works-and-is-trusted vs blocked-by-design. _Forcing:_ Why does the browser _require_ a user gesture for reads especially? What protection does that gate provide? (Name the protective friction.)
3. **Selection API vs input `selectionStart/End`.** Options: `window.getSelection()` + `Range` · a form field's selection indices. _Axis:_ arbitrary-page selection vs the simple form-field case. _Forcing:_ Is the text in a plain `<textarea>` (simple indices) or anywhere on the page (Ranges across nodes)?
4. **Plain vs rich clipboard content.** Options: plain text · `ClipboardItem` with MIME types. _Axis:_ simplicity vs preserving formatting. _Forcing:_ Do you need formatting, or is plain text the honest scope?
5. **Permission-denied behavior.** Options: assume success · detect rejection and inform/fall back. _Axis:_ less code vs a usable feature. _Forcing:_ When `read()` rejects, does the UI break silently or tell the user?

### Research targets

- _The Selection and Range API._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain how a selection spanning two elements is represented, and how you extract its text. _Seed:_ MDN `Selection`/`Range`.
- _The async Clipboard API and its permission model._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state what a read requires (gesture, permission, secure context) and how it rejects. _Seed:_ MDN Clipboard API.
- _Why clipboard access is restricted — the reasoning._ — **25 min.** Done when you can articulate the cross-application-secret argument (your protective-friction framing).
- _`execCommand` deprecation — what replaced it and why._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say why the sync API gave way to the async one.

### Out of scope (fence)

Copying images/rich media, drag-and-drop APIs (note, park), full `contenteditable` rich editing, Safari/Firefox permission quirks (note, park), building the whole annotation tool. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Copy via your button, then paste into a _different_ application. **The number:** did the text arrive? Then trigger a clipboard read with no user gesture and confirm it _fails safely_ (the protective gate working, your handling catching it). Two behaviors: works-on-gesture, fails-clean-off-gesture — do both hold?

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>getSelection gives me weird/empty results when the selection crosses elements</summary>

**Orient:** a selection isn't a string — it's one or more `Range`s over DOM nodes, and "the text" is something you extract _from_ the range, not a property handed to you in every case.

**Approach:** get the `Selection` from `window.getSelection()`, read its string form for the simple case, or pull the `Range` (`getRangeAt(0)`) when you need position or when the simple string is empty. A range spanning elements has a start container/offset and an end container/offset in _different_ nodes — the text between them crosses node boundaries, which is why a naive `.textContent` of one node misses it. Extract from the range itself.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why is the clipboard gated — what secret passes through it — and what does the browser demand before it opens? **Check:** the threat and the two conditions (gesture, permission) stated.
2. **Research.** The four targets. _Objective:_ Selection/Range API shape sketched; the async Clipboard API's permission flow narrated.
3. **Commit (DSA).** The five decisions, written — including exactly what happens on permission-denied. **Check:** the denied path is designed, not hoped away.
4. **Build — the selection.** Read the selection's text and position on select; do something visible with it. **Check:** works across multi-node selections, not just single text nodes.
5. **Build — the write.** Copy to clipboard on a user gesture. **Check:** paste into another app proves it landed.
6. **Build — the read.** Paste-from-clipboard, gesture-gated. **Check:** it reads back what an external app copied.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Deny the permission and use the feature anyway. **Check:** your designed fallback appears — no console error as UX. _Ask:_ why did the browser make you work this hard, and are you glad? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** highlight cross-element text and show it captured; copy-then-paste into another app; a gesture-less read failing cleanly.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built the capture primitives for an annotation tool — reading the selection as a Range, and read/write to the clipboard with the async API. The interesting part is that clipboard access is deliberately gated behind a user gesture and a permission, because the clipboard is a cross-app secret — so 'it fails silently off-gesture' is the API working as designed, and I handle that rather than fighting it."
- **Design review:** a peer used deprecated `execCommand` and tries to read the clipboard on load. Explain the modern async API and why the gesture requirement is protective, not an obstacle.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been playing with the Selection and Clipboard APIs — the foundation of any highlight-to-notes tool — and the neat thing is how the clipboard's permission gate is a feature, not a bug."
- **Reach-back (instruments thread):** M26 stored words the user _typed_; this grabs words they _selected or copied_. Combine them — what's the smallest tool-for-thought those two modules already let you build?

---

# PART B — Responsive Surfaces

_UI as a function of a changing input — scroll position, the time of day, playback time. Three surfaces that react to something continuous, and the performance and time-math traps that come with each. The time thread, at browser altitude._

---

## M28 — Full-Bleed Gallery · a scroll-driven image gallery with overlays · (M)

> **Story.** As a design-engineer building a portfolio-grade site, I want a full-viewport image gallery driven by scroll, with text overlays and award-tier polish, so that I can present my own images with the craft that wins Awwwards — and learn scroll-driven UI without the jank. **MVP.** Full-viewport (100vh) images _I supply_, advanced smoothly by scroll, each with a legible overlay, performant. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Each image fills the viewport full-bleed, with no distortion, across images of varying dimensions.
> - Scrolling advances through the gallery smoothly, with the intended transition, at a steady frame rate.
> - Overlays (titles/captions) stay legible over _any_ image — light or dark.
> - Images appear without janky pop-in as you scroll; the page never stutters.

**Anchors:** _Pixels With Intent_ (rendering pipeline and compositing); reach-backs to M15 (layout), M16 (avoiding reflow), M17 (60fps, award-tier). You'll drop in your own images (photography or a curated set), which also makes this portfolio-real.

### Briefing

Full-viewport ("full-bleed") imagery advanced by scroll is the signature of award-winning editorial and portfolio sites. Underneath the polish is one idea — **map scroll position to visual state** — done on the browser's terms: on the compositor, not by thrashing layout. The overlays add a second problem, legibility of text over arbitrary images. The center of gravity is **scroll-as-input rendered cheaply, plus contrast over images you don't control.**

### The trap

**Scroll jank, and the wrong primitive.** Scroll events fire at very high frequency; doing layout or heavy work in the scroll handler — or animating layout properties like `top`/`height` — causes stutter (the same performance floor as M17, the same throttle lesson as M5). Its partners: **image pop-in** (a full-viewport image that isn't decoded yet flashes as you reach it) and **overlay illegibility** (white text vanishing over a light photo). The tell: your fan spins up and the scroll feels sticky.

### The build

A full-viewport gallery over a user-supplied, ordered set of images, scroll driving progression with a chosen transition, an overlay per image, and a loading strategy that avoids pop-in. No backend. Data shape (a spec): an ordered list of `{ image, overlayText }`.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building. Design the intended effect / reference board first._

1. **Scroll primitive.** Options: a throttled/`rAF` scroll handler · `IntersectionObserver` · CSS scroll-driven animations (scroll-timeline) · a library (GSAP ScrollTrigger, Lenis). _Axis:_ control vs performance vs modernity vs batteries-included. _Forcing:_ Which runs off the main thread and avoids work per scroll tick? Do you need _continuous_ progress (parallax) or _discrete_ (snap between images)? (`IntersectionObserver` fires on threshold crossings — cheap; a scroll event fires constantly — needs `rAF`.)
2. **Transition style.** Options: hard snap (`scroll-snap`) · crossfade · pin-and-parallax · horizontal-on-vertical-scroll. _Axis:_ simplicity vs the award-tier feel. _Forcing:_ Which properties animate — `opacity`/`transform` (compositor-safe) or `top`/`width`/`height` (layout, janky)? (This is the trap.)
3. **Full-bleed image fit.** Options: `object-fit: cover` · `contain` · art-directed crops per breakpoint. _Axis:_ fill-the-viewport vs no-cropping vs editorial control. _Forcing:_ Your images vary in aspect ratio — `cover` crops, `contain` letterboxes; which serves a full-bleed gallery, and how do you avoid distortion?
4. **Overlay legibility over arbitrary images.** Options: fixed text color · a gradient scrim/backdrop · dynamic contrast (sample the image's luminance). _Axis:_ simplicity vs guaranteed readability. _Forcing:_ What keeps white text readable over a _light_ image — a scrim, a backdrop-filter, or measuring luminance and flipping the color? (Reaches back to M15/M16.)
5. **Image loading strategy.** Options: load all up front · lazy-load with preload-ahead · responsive `srcset` + a blur-up placeholder. _Axis:_ simplicity vs no-pop-in smoothness at award quality. _Forcing:_ How do you avoid a flash when the next full-viewport image isn't decoded yet? What does preloading the next image (or a low-res placeholder + `img.decode()`) buy you?
6. **Reduced motion.** Options: assume motion · honor `prefers-reduced-motion`. _Axis:_ simplicity vs accessibility (award sites still need it). _Forcing:_ What does the gallery _become_ for a reduced-motion user — a plain vertical scroll of images? (Same instinct as M17.)

### Research targets

- _Scroll-driven animation: scroll event vs `IntersectionObserver` vs CSS scroll-timeline._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can say which avoids main-thread work per tick and when each fits. _Seed:_ MDN scroll-driven animations; the `IntersectionObserver` page.
- _Compositor-safe animation (`transform`/`opacity` vs layout properties)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name which properties are cheap and why (reaches M17). _Seed:_ web.dev "Animations and performance."
- _`object-fit` and full-bleed image handling._ — **25 min.** Done when you can fill a viewport with a varying-aspect image without distortion.
- _Avoiding image pop-in: preloading, `img.decode()`, `srcset`, blur-up._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe eliminating the flash of an undecoded next image.
- _Overlay contrast over images (scrim/gradient/luminance)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can keep text legible over a light and a dark image.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full page-transition frameworks, video backgrounds, WebGL image transitions/shaders (note — park; that's the deep award end and overlaps M17), infinite scroll, a CMS for the images. → parking lot. **And: study the award-site effects you admire, but save source-diving until after your DSA** (post-commit), same discipline as M17.

### Measure ritual

Open the Performance panel and scroll hard through the gallery. **The number:** does it hold ~60fps (≤16.7ms/frame) with no layout-thrash spikes, and do images appear without pop-in? Then eyeball the worst-contrast overlay — is the text still legible over its image?

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My scroll animation stutters</summary>

**Orient:** something is running on the main thread every scroll tick, or you're animating a layout property.

**Approach:** move per-scroll work off the hot path — prefer `IntersectionObserver` (fires only on threshold crossings) or CSS scroll-timeline over a JS scroll handler; and animate only `transform`/`opacity` (compositor), never `top`/`left`/`width`/`height` (layout). If you must use a scroll handler, coalesce it into a single `requestAnimationFrame`. In the Performance panel, purple "Layout" bars appearing during scroll _are_ the jank — chase them.

</details>

### The slow walk

_Run the module in this order, one step at a time. A step's **Check** must pass — out loud or on screen — before the next step begins. Timeboxes from the research targets apply; anything shiny and off-path goes to the parking lot._

1. **Orient.** _Ask:_ why do scroll handlers jank — what work is compositor-safe and what isn't? **Check:** `transform`/`opacity` vs layout properties sorted correctly, aloud.
2. **Research.** The five targets. _Objective:_ scroll event vs IntersectionObserver vs CSS scroll-timeline compared with a use case each.
3. **Commit (DSA).** The six decisions — scroll primitive first, reduced-motion not last. **Check:** written before any scrolling code.
4. **Build — the bones.** Full-viewport images in order, correctly fitted (`object-fit`), no interaction yet. **Check:** no pop-in on a hard reload with a cold cache.
5. **Build — the drive.** Scroll advancing the gallery via your chosen primitive with your chosen transition. **Check:** DevTools performance recording during a fast scroll shows no long frames.
6. **Build — the voice.** Overlays legible over your images. **Check:** legible over the worst image (brightest, busiest), not the best.
7. **Walk into the trap, then debrief.** Set `prefers-reduced-motion` and revisit. **Check:** a dignified non-animated experience. _Ask:_ which of M5's throttle lessons and M17's frame-budget lessons reappeared here? Rubber duck.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** scroll the full gallery at a held frame rate (show the meter); an overlay legible over a light _and_ a dark image; no pop-in.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a full-bleed scroll-driven image gallery at award-site polish. The decision I'd defend is using `IntersectionObserver`/CSS scroll-timeline over a JS scroll handler and animating only compositor-safe properties — scroll jank comes from main-thread work per tick and animating layout. I handled overlay contrast over arbitrary images with a gradient scrim, and preloaded-and-decoded the next image to kill pop-in."
- **Design review:** a peer animates in a scroll handler mutating `top`/`height` and it janks. Diagnose main-thread work plus layout thrash; propose `IntersectionObserver` and transforms.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I'm building a full-viewport scroll gallery for my portfolio — the craft is all in holding 60fps and keeping captions readable over any image."
- **Reach-back (time + floor threads):** scroll is another continuous input (position instead of the clock), and this sits on the same compositor floor as M17 and the reflow lesson of M16. State the one performance discipline shared across M16, M17, and this.

---

## M29 — Open Now, Then Sorted · time-of-day conditional ranking · (M)

> **Story.** As a front-end engineer, I want a table that's sorted by one criterion but floats "currently open" items to the top based on each item's own hours, so that I can build the "Open now" ranking real apps (maps, delivery) use — and handle the moving-target and midnight-crossing pitfalls. **MVP.** A table of items, each with their own weekly open-hours; items open _right now_ rise above an otherwise-sorted list, and the ranking recomputes as time passes. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Items open at the current time appear above closed ones, while the existing sort (e.g. rating) orders _within_ each group.
> - An item whose hours cross midnight (open 22:00–02:00) is classified correctly.
> - "Open now" status updates as the clock advances — it is not frozen at page load.
> - Timezone/DST is handled sensibly (you decide how — but decide).

**Anchors:** M12 (time logic, walking backward through time), M14 (time windows), _Where the Data Lives_. The time thread, ranked by a moving "now."

### Briefing

This is multi-criteria sorting where the _primary_ key is **computed and time-dependent**. "Open now" is a moving target: whether an item qualifies changes as time passes and depends on each item's own schedule. The center of gravity is **layering a computed, time-dependent primary sort on top of a stable secondary sort** — and surviving the time-math pitfalls that make grown engineers wince.

### The trap

**The moving target, the midnight crossing, and the unstable sort.** "Open now" computed once at load goes stale as the user sits there. Hours that wrap past midnight (22:00–02:00) break a naive `open <= now <= close` check — 01:00 tests as closed. And floating open items up with an unstable sort can scramble the rating order you already established within each group. The tell: it looks right at 3pm on a Tuesday and wrong at 1am.

### The build

A table sorted by a secondary criterion (say rating, descending), a per-item "open now" boolean derived from weekly hours, open items grouped above closed, recomputed over time. Item shape (a spec): `{ id, name, rating, hours: [{ day, open, close }, …] }`. "Open now" is _derived_, never stored.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Where "open now" is computed.** Options: client · server. _Axis:_ recompute-as-time-passes on the client vs consistent server truth. _Forcing:_ Computed server-side at request time, does it go stale while the user reads? Who owns "now"? (Reaches M26's timestamp question.)
2. **Representing hours that cross midnight.** Options: same-day `open`/`close` · allow `close < open` to mean "next day" · store intervals as minutes-from-week-start. _Axis:_ simple vs correct across midnight. _Forcing:_ How do you represent 22:00–02:00 so "is 01:00 open?" is true? (This is the trap.)
3. **Stable vs unstable sort for the grouping.** Options: rely on a stable sort · a composite key. _Axis:_ preserve the secondary order vs scramble it. _Forcing:_ After floating open items up, is the rating order within each block preserved? Is your sort stable, or do you need a composite comparator?
4. **Composite key vs two-pass partition.** Options: one comparator (open-first, then rating) · partition-then-concatenate. _Axis:_ single expression vs explicit blocks. _Forcing:_ Which expresses "open above closed, each block by rating" more clearly _and_ stably?
5. **Timezone/DST.** Options: local device time · the item's timezone · a fixed zone. _Axis:_ simplicity vs correctness for items elsewhere. _Forcing:_ Is a place's "open now" judged in the _user's_ zone or the _item's_? What breaks on a DST boundary? (Reaches M2's `timestamptz`.)
6. **Recompute cadence.** Options: on an interval (tick) · on interaction · exactly at the next open/close boundary. _Axis:_ freshness vs work. _Forcing:_ How often must "open now" refresh to never be wrong — every minute, or precisely at the next boundary?

### Research targets

- _Multi-key / composite sorting and sort stability._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain a comparator that sorts by open-status then rating without scrambling ties. _Seed:_ MDN `Array.prototype.sort` (stability).
- _Representing and testing time intervals that cross midnight._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can correctly answer "is now within this interval?" for a 22:00–02:00 window.
- _Timezone and DST pitfalls._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name why local-time math breaks across DST and what a zone-aware approach fixes.
- _Keeping a time-derived value fresh (interval vs next-boundary scheduling)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe refreshing exactly at the next boundary.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full calendar/holiday handling, per-item timezones at scale (note, park), natural-language hours parsing ("open till late"), a real maps backend. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Mock "now" to a time where some items straddle open/closed and one crosses midnight. **The number:** are exactly the open items on top, each block still rating-ordered, and the midnight-crosser classified right? Then advance the clock past a boundary and confirm the list re-ranks.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My midnight-crossing hours misclassify</summary>

**Orient:** a naive `open <= now <= close` assumes `close > open`, which fails when the interval wraps past midnight.

**Approach:** detect the wrap (`close < open` means it spans midnight) and, for that case, treat "open" as `now >= open` **OR** `now <= close`; or normalize everything to minutes-from-week-start as intervals that can exceed a day, so the wrap disappears entirely. Test 01:00 against a 22:00–02:00 window explicitly — that single case catches the bug.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** a mocked "now" showing correct grouping, a midnight-crosser correct, and a boundary re-rank.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an 'open now' ranking — items open at the current time float above an otherwise rating-sorted table. The subtle parts: hours crossing midnight break naive interval checks, a stable/composite sort so floating open items up doesn't scramble the rating order, and recomputing as time passes because 'open now' is a moving target, not a load-time fact."
- **Design review:** a peer computes open-now once at load with `open <= now <= close`. Name the staleness and midnight bugs; propose the fixes.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been building the 'Open now' sort that maps and delivery apps use — the gotcha is that 'now' keeps moving and some places are open past midnight."
- **Reach-back (time thread):** M12 walked backward through time, M14 windowed a stream; this ranks by a moving "now." What's shared about treating time as a first-class input, and what's unique here?

---

## M30 — Transport Controls · a JS audio player · (S)

> **Story.** As a front-end engineer, I want an audio player with standard transport controls, so that I can handle real media state — buffering, seeking, autoplay policy — and sync a progress UI without drift. **MVP.** Play/pause, seek (scrubber), volume, current-time/duration display, over a provided audio file. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Play/pause works and the button reflects _actual_ playback state.
> - The scrubber seeks, and tracks playback as it advances.
> - Volume works.
> - The progress bar advances smoothly (not choppy) and stays locked to real playback time; `play()` handles an autoplay-policy rejection gracefully; seeking while still loading behaves sanely.

**Anchors:** M5 (async and events); M17 (`requestAnimationFrame` for smooth UI); _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (events).

### Briefing

An `<audio>` element (or the Web Audio API) exposes playback and a stream of state events; building transport controls means both _driving_ that state and _reflecting_ it. The center of gravity is **media state — loading, buffering, playing, ended — and syncing the UI to real playback time without drift.**

### The trap

**Async loading plus media state, and progress-bar drift.** `play()` returns a promise that can _reject_ (autoplay policies block sound without a user gesture). Audio has buffering states, so seeking before it's loaded is fiddly. And the `timeupdate` event fires coarsely (~4×/sec), so a progress bar driven by it looks choppy — smooth progress wants `requestAnimationFrame`, but the _source of truth_ must stay the element's `currentTime`, or your animated bar drifts out of sync. The tell: a stuttery scrubber that slowly disagrees with the audio.

### The build

Transport UI over a provided audio file — play/pause, seek, volume, time display, smooth progress. Consumes an audio source URL; reflects the element's state. No backend.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **`<audio>` element vs Web Audio API.** Options: the element · Web Audio. _Axis:_ simple standard controls vs full processing/visualization power. _Forcing:_ Do you need effects or a waveform (Web Audio) or just transport (the element)? (M12-style: right tool for the job.)
2. **Progress updates.** Options: `timeupdate` events · `requestAnimationFrame` reading `currentTime`. _Axis:_ simple/coarse vs smooth. _Forcing:_ `timeupdate` fires ~4×/sec — smooth enough for a scrubber? If you `rAF`, what's the source of truth so you don't drift? (This is the trap.)
3. **Handling the `play()` promise.** Options: ignore it · `await`/`catch` the rejection. _Axis:_ less code vs handling autoplay blocks. _Forcing:_ On some browsers `play()` rejects without a gesture — what does the UI show then?
4. **Seeking during load.** Options: assume loaded · handle the not-yet-seekable state. _Axis:_ simple vs robust. _Forcing:_ The user drags the scrubber before enough has buffered — what happens, and what _should_?
5. **State reflection.** Options: track your own `isPlaying` flag · derive from the element's `play`/`pause`/`ended` events. _Axis:_ simple vs never-out-of-sync. _Forcing:_ If playback ends or the OS pauses it, does your button reflect it?

### Research targets

- _The `HTMLMediaElement` API: `currentTime`, `duration`, `buffered`, `play()`/`pause()`, events._ — **25 min.** _Seed:_ MDN `HTMLAudioElement`.
- _Autoplay policies and the `play()` promise rejection._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why sound needs a gesture and how to handle the rejection.
- _Smooth progress: `timeupdate` vs `rAF`, with `currentTime` as source of truth._ — **25 min.** (Reaches M17's `rAF`.)
- _Buffering and `seekable`/`buffered` ranges._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what those tell you.

### Out of scope (fence)

Playlists, Web Audio visualizations/effects (note, park; overlaps M17/M37), streaming/HLS, custom codecs, the Media Session API. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Play the file and watch the scrubber. **The number:** does the progress bar advance smoothly and stay locked to actual `currentTime` (no drift over a few minutes), and does `play()` fail gracefully when blocked? Scrub mid-load and confirm sane behavior.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** play/pause/seek/volume; smooth in-sync progress; a graceful autoplay-block.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built an audio player with standard transport. Two real bits: `play()` returns a promise that rejects under autoplay policies, so I handle that; and a progress bar driven by `timeupdate` is choppy because it fires ~4×/sec, so I animate with `requestAnimationFrame` but keep the element's `currentTime` as the source of truth to avoid drift."
- **Design review:** a peer tracks an `isPlaying` boolean and drives progress off `timeupdate`; it desyncs and stutters. Propose deriving state from media events and `rAF`-with-`currentTime`.
- **Meetup (30s):** "Quick audio player with real controls — the surprise was how much state (buffering, autoplay blocks) hides behind a play button."
- **Reach-back (time thread):** playback time is another continuous input, like scroll (M28) and streams (M5). What smoothing technique do M28, M30, and M17 all share?

---

# PART C — Beyond the Browser

_Two modules of code that leaves the sandbox: a desktop app that must reach the OS across a process boundary, and firmware on a chip with kilobytes of RAM and no operating system. The floor thread and the sovereignty thread, meeting at the metal._

---

## M31 — The Window Is a Process Away · Electron window control · (S)

> **Story.** As a developer building a desktop app, I want to control window sizing, transparency, and always-on-top from Electron, so that I can build native-feeling desktop windows — and understand the main/renderer process split and its security model. **MVP.** An Electron app whose window has a set default size, resizes within limits, and can toggle transparency and always-on-top. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - The window opens at a defined default size and respects min/max resize bounds.
> - A control toggles always-on-top (the window stays above other windows).
> - A control toggles transparency (a see-through window).
> - Window controls invoked from the UI actually take effect _via the correct process boundary_.
> - The renderer does **not** have Node integration enabled (secure default).

**Anchors:** the floor thread (Electron is Chromium + Node bundled — a convenience with a receipt); the security material from M4 and the networking primer.

### Briefing

Electron builds desktop apps with web tech by bundling Chromium (the UI) and Node (system access). Crucially, it runs two kinds of process: the **main** process (one, owns windows and the OS) and **renderer** processes (your web UI, one per window, sandboxed). Window control lives in main; your UI lives in renderer; they talk over **IPC** (inter-process communication). The center of gravity is **the main/renderer split, and controlling the OS window from the right side of that boundary.**

### The trap

**The main/renderer boundary, and the security model.** Window APIs (`BrowserWindow`) live in the _main_ process — you can't call them from the renderer directly, so a button that calls `window.resize` silently does nothing. The dangerous "fix" is enabling `nodeIntegration` in the renderer, which hands full system access to any web content (or any XSS) — a severe hole. The safe pattern is `contextIsolation` plus a **preload bridge** exposing only the specific commands you intend.

### The build

An Electron window with a default size, min/max bounds, and toggles for transparency and always-on-top, driven from the UI through IPC. Config is a spec you may take as given — `BrowserWindow` options `{ width, height, minWidth, minHeight, transparent, alwaysOnTop, frame }` — and the renderer sends _intents_ ("set always-on-top true") that main executes.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Where window control runs.** Options: main · renderer. _Axis:_ correct architecture vs the tempting shortcut. _Forcing:_ `BrowserWindow` lives in main — how does a button in your UI trigger it, and what breaks if you reach for it directly?
2. **The renderer's Node access.** Options: `nodeIntegration` on · `contextIsolation` + a preload `contextBridge`. _Axis:_ convenience vs security. _Forcing:_ With `nodeIntegration`, what can injected web content do to the user's machine? Why is exposing _only_ specific commands via preload safer? (This is the trap.)
3. **Transparency approach.** Options: a `transparent` frameless `BrowserWindow` · a CSS-only translucent look. _Axis:_ real OS-level transparency vs simplicity. _Forcing:_ Do you need the window actually see-through (compositing with the desktop) or just a translucent panel? Transparent windows have platform quirks — what do you give up?
4. **Frame vs frameless.** Options: native chrome · frameless custom UI. _Axis:_ OS title bar and resize handles vs a custom look. _Forcing:_ If frameless, you lose the OS resize handles and drag bar — do you re-implement them, and is that in scope?
5. **Default + constraints.** Options: hardcoded size · remember-last · fit-to-content. _Axis:_ simple vs polished. _Forcing:_ What size on first launch, and do min/max bounds prevent an unusable window?

### Research targets

- _Electron's process model: main vs renderer, and IPC._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain which process owns windows and how the two communicate. _Seed:_ Electron's process-model docs.
- _`contextIsolation`, preload, and the `contextBridge`._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why exposing only specific APIs via preload beats `nodeIntegration`.
- _`BrowserWindow` options: `transparent`, `alwaysOnTop`, `frame`, min/max._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name the option behind each feature.
- _Transparency/frameless platform quirks._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name one gotcha (e.g. missing resize handles).

### Out of scope (fence)

Auto-updates, packaging and code-signing, native modules, tray/menu apps, multi-window management, the Chromium bundle-size debate (note — park; it's the floor-thread receipt). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Open the app. **The number:** does the window honor its default size and min/max, and do the always-on-top and transparency toggles actually take effect (stays above others / is see-through)? And confirm `nodeIntegration` is off / `contextIsolation` on in the config.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My resize / always-on-top call from the UI does nothing</summary>

**Orient:** `BrowserWindow` lives in the main process; renderer JavaScript can't touch it directly.

**Approach:** the renderer must _ask_ main to do it — send a message over IPC (`ipcRenderer` → `ipcMain`), and main calls the `BrowserWindow` method. Expose that send through a preload `contextBridge` so the renderer gets a narrow, safe API (`window.api.setAlwaysOnTop(true)`) rather than raw `ipcRenderer`. If nothing happens, confirm your channel names match and the handler is registered in main.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the window at its default size with min/max enforced; toggling always-on-top (verified against another window) and transparency.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built Electron window controls — default size, min/max, always-on-top, transparency. The architectural point is the main/renderer split: window APIs live in the main process, so the UI sends intents over IPC, and I expose them through a preload `contextBridge` rather than enabling `nodeIntegration`, which would hand system access to any injected content."
- **Design review:** a peer enabled `nodeIntegration` to call window APIs from the renderer. Explain the security hole and the `contextBridge` fix.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been learning Electron's guts — the a-ha is that your web UI can't touch the OS window directly; it's in a different process and has to ask nicely over IPC."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** Electron bundles a whole browser and Node runtime — web-tech-on-the-desktop, paid for in bundle size and a two-process security model. Name the receipt here, and contrast it with M32's kilobytes.

---

## M32 — Kilobytes and a Screen · ESP32 microSD + OLED firmware · (L)

> **Story.** As a builder who wants to compute on hardware I fully own, I want firmware for an ESP32 that reads a microSD filesystem and drives an animated OLED, so that I can work at the embedded floor — kilobytes of RAM, no OS, direct hardware — and feel the constraints the whole stack hides. **MVP.** ESP32 firmware that mounts a microSD card, reads files from it, drives a smooth OLED animation, and offers a small browse/select interface (on-screen or over serial). **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - The microSD mounts and files are listed/read reliably (across resets).
> - The OLED shows a smooth animation _without blocking_ the rest of the firmware.
> - A simple interface browses SD contents (on-screen or over serial).
> - The firmware runs continuously without exhausting memory or hanging.

**Anchors:** _The Machine Under Everything_ — the floor _below_ its floor: memory-mapped I/O, direct hardware; M18 (manual memory, but with far less headroom) and M19 (the loop that never stops); the sovereignty thread.

### Briefing

An ESP32 is a microcontroller — a whole computer on a chip with _kilobytes_ of RAM (not gigabytes), no operating system, running one program directly on the metal. Firmware talks to peripherals over buses: a microSD card (a FAT filesystem over SPI) and an OLED display (pixels over I2C or SPI). Doing both — a filesystem interface _and_ a screen animation — forces you to live inside real constraints. The center of gravity is **the embedded resource model (tiny RAM, no OS, direct hardware) and a non-blocking main loop juggling SD I/O and screen refresh.**

### The trap

**The resource constraints, and the blocking operation.** An ESP32 has tens of KB of usable RAM — allocate carelessly (big buffers, `String` churn, fragmentation) and it crashes; it's M18's memory discipline with a fraction of the headroom. And blocking: an SD read or a long draw call _stalls_ the loop, so the animation stutters or freezes — the same blocking-loop trap as M19, now with hardware timing. SD-over-SPI adds its own gotchas: mount failures, a shared bus, buffering.

### The build

Firmware mounting a microSD (FAT over SPI), reading files, and driving an OLED animation, with a browse/select interface. The pin configuration (SPI pins for the SD, I2C/SPI for the OLED) and the file-listing interface are specs you define; the firmware is yours.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Firmware framework.** Options: Arduino/PlatformIO · ESP-IDF (closer to the metal) · MicroPython. _Axis:_ fast start and libraries vs full control and performance vs ease at a RAM cost. _Forcing:_ How bare do you want the metal? MicroPython is easiest but eats RAM you barely have; ESP-IDF is closest to the hardware. Pick a point and defend it.
2. **Blocking vs non-blocking loop.** Options: SD reads and draws inline · cooperative scheduling (a state machine / `millis()`-based timing) · FreeRTOS tasks (the ESP32 has an RTOS). _Axis:_ simple vs a smooth animation that doesn't freeze on I/O. _Forcing:_ A slow SD read stalls the loop — how does the animation keep running? (The trap; M19's game loop, in firmware.)
3. **Memory strategy.** Options: dynamic allocation / `String` · fixed static buffers. _Axis:_ convenience vs no fragmentation/crash on tiny RAM. _Forcing:_ With tens of KB of RAM, what does repeated `String` concatenation or a big `malloc` do? Why do embedded devs prefer fixed buffers? (Reaches M18 hard.)
4. **Animation rendering.** Options: full-frame redraw · partial updates; a framebuffer in RAM · draw-direct. _Axis:_ simple vs fits-in-RAM and flicker-free. _Forcing:_ Can you afford a full OLED framebuffer in RAM? How do you refresh smoothly within the display's timing?
5. **SD/SPI handling.** Options: SD sharing the SPI bus with the display · separate; buffered reads. _Axis:_ pin economy vs contention. _Forcing:_ If SD and OLED share the bus, how do you keep them from stepping on each other? What happens on a mount failure — retry, or hang?
6. **UI location.** Options: an on-screen menu · a serial-console interface. _Axis:_ self-contained vs simpler to build. _Forcing:_ Does browsing happen on the OLED (needs input + rendering) or over serial (easier, less embedded-feeling)?

### Research targets

- _The embedded resource model: RAM/flash sizes, no OS, the main loop._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state roughly how much RAM you have and why that changes everything. _Seed:_ the ESP32 datasheet overview.
- _Non-blocking firmware: `millis()`-based timing / state machines / FreeRTOS tasks._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain keeping an animation running while doing I/O. (Reaches M19.)
- _SPI/I2C buses: driving an OLED (SSD1306/SH1106) and SD over SPI._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name the bus each peripheral uses and the mount step.
- _Memory discipline on microcontrollers (fragmentation, static buffers, avoiding `String`)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why fixed buffers beat dynamic allocation here. (Reaches M18.)

### Out of scope (fence)

A custom PCB, deep sleep / power optimization, WiFi/networking (Reticulum-adjacent — that's M33; park), a full GUI framework, writing your own display driver, embedded Rust (note — park; ties to your Rust track). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run it for a while. **The number:** does the animation stay smooth (no freeze) _while_ SD reads happen, and does free heap stay stable over time (no leak/fragmentation death)? Watch the free-memory reporting; confirm the SD mounts reliably across resets.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My animation freezes whenever I read the SD card</summary>

**Orient:** the SD read is blocking — it halts the loop until it finishes, so nothing else (including the animation) advances meanwhile.

**Approach:** you can't let the loop wait. Either break the work into small chunks the loop revisits (a state machine reading a bit per iteration), drive the animation on a `millis()`-based timer independent of the read, or put the SD work and the animation on separate FreeRTOS tasks so the scheduler interleaves them. This is M19's blocking-input trap in firmware — the loop must never wait on one slow thing.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** SD mounting and file listing; a smooth OLED animation that keeps running during an SD read; browse/select working; stable free heap over time.

- **Interview (90s):** "I wrote ESP32 firmware that mounts a microSD filesystem and drives an animated OLED. The two hard constraints are memory — tens of KB, so fixed buffers, not `String` churn, or it fragments and crashes — and blocking: an SD read stalls the loop and freezes the animation, so I kept the loop non-blocking with timer-based updates. It's the C-memory and game-loop lessons on bare metal."
- **Design review:** a peer reads a whole SD file into a `String` and draws a full framebuffer, then wonders why it crashes. Diagnose RAM exhaustion/fragmentation; propose fixed buffers and chunked reads.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been writing ESP32 firmware — SD card plus an animated OLED — and the humbling part is doing it in kilobytes of RAM with no OS to catch you."
- **Reach-back (floor + sovereignty threads):** this is the deepest floor in either volume — below C, at memory-mapped hardware — and it runs on a chip you fully own. Name the convenience every higher layer sells you that's simply _absent_ here, against both M18 and M31.

---

# PART D — Networks, Seen and Unseen

_Two modules about the network from opposite ends: build one that needs no infrastructure at all, then learn to see exactly what's talking to your own machine. The sovereignty thread, with a defensive-security turn._

---

## M33 — A Network That Needs No One · a Reticulum feature · (M)

> **Story.** As a builder who values independent, resilient technology, I want to make two nodes talk over Reticulum, so that I understand identity-based, infrastructure-free networking — a fundamentally different model from the internet I grew up on. **MVP.** Two Reticulum nodes that discover each other and exchange an encrypted message, addressed by cryptographic identity, with no central server and no assigned addresses. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Node A can address node B by its identity and send it a message that B receives.
> - The exchange uses no shared server, no IP address you assigned, and no DNS.
> - It still works after restarting a node (the path is re-discovered).
> - You can explain what a "destination" is in Reticulum and why it isn't an IP address.

**Anchors:** the networking primer in your project (for the TCP/IP contrast); the sovereignty thread; your permacomputing instincts.

### Briefing

Let's start from the ground. A _network stack_ is the layers of software that move messages between computers. The internet's stack makes big assumptions: your device is handed an **IP address** by someone (your router, your ISP), names like `example.com` are translated to addresses by **DNS** servers, and your packets travel through infrastructure owned by companies. That works beautifully — until the infrastructure is down, censored, or absent.

**Reticulum** throws those assumptions out. It's a network stack where every participant is identified by a **cryptographic identity** — a public/private key pair — instead of an assigned address; where messages are **encrypted end-to-end by default**; and which runs over _any_ medium that can carry bytes: a LoRa radio, a serial wire, a TCP link over the existing internet. No central authority, no DNS, no addresses handed down from above. It's built for resilience — networks that keep working when the usual infrastructure doesn't. The center of gravity is the **mental shift** from _address-based, infrastructure-dependent_ networking to _identity-based, infrastructure-independent_ networking.

### The trap

**Applying your TCP/IP mental models.** There is no "IP address to connect to," no central server, no endpoint guaranteed to be online. If you reach for the familiar client/server-with-an-address frame, nothing fits: Reticulum is peer-to-peer, addressed by keys, and delay-tolerant, so "connect to this address" is simply the wrong picture. And a consequence people miss: the key _is_ the identity — lose the private key and you lose the identity; there is no account recovery, because there's no account and no authority to recover it from.

### The build

A small Reticulum feature: two nodes that find each other and exchange messages over RNS (the Reticulum Network Stack), using its own concepts. Run it locally over a TCP interface for testing (or over LoRa if you have the hardware). The building blocks — **Identity** (your keypair), **Destination** (an address derived from an identity), **announce** (how a node advertises a destination so others can find a path to it), and **Link/Packet** (an established encrypted channel / a single encrypted message) — are the RNS API; use it, don't reinvent the crypto.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Transport interface.** Options: TCP (for local testing) · LoRa radio · serial. _Axis:_ easy to develop vs the real off-grid use. _Forcing:_ Reticulum runs over anything — which medium lets you _build and test_ now, and which is the point later?
2. **Packet vs Link vs Resource.** Options: a single `Packet` · an established `Link` (an encrypted channel) · a `Resource` (for larger transfers). _Axis:_ one-shot simplicity vs a session vs bulk data. _Forcing:_ For a small message, do you need a persistent channel? For a big file, what does a `Resource` handle that a `Packet` doesn't?
3. **How nodes discover each other.** Options: announces (a node advertises; others learn a path) · a known destination hash. _Axis:_ dynamic discovery vs a pre-shared address. _Forcing:_ How does B learn where A is with no DNS and no central registry?
4. **Identity persistence.** Options: store the keypair · ephemeral per run. _Axis:_ a stable identity across restarts vs a fresh one each time. _Forcing:_ If the identity _is_ the key, what happens to your address when you regenerate it?
5. **Trust.** Options: trust any destination · verify it's the identity you expect. _Axis:_ simplicity vs knowing who you're really talking to. _Forcing:_ How do you know a destination belongs to the party you think, with no central authority vouching for it?

### Research targets

- _What Reticulum is, and how it differs from TCP/IP._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain identity-addressing vs IP-addressing and infrastructure-independence, tab closed. _Seed:_ the Reticulum manual — "What is Reticulum" (Mark Qvist / unsigned.io).
- _Destinations, identities, and announces._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain how a node is addressed by a key-derived hash and how others find a path to it.
- _Links vs Packets vs Resources._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say which to use for a small message vs a large transfer.
- _The delay-tolerant, no-infrastructure model._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why there's no central server and what that buys.

### Out of scope (fence)

Mesh-routing internals, LoRa hardware bring-up (unless you have it — note, park), the NomadNet/Sideband app internals, building your own cryptography (never — RNS handles it), running a public transport node. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Two nodes locally over the TCP interface. **The number:** can node A address node B by its identity and exchange an encrypted message with no shared server and no assigned addresses — and does it still work after you restart one node (path re-discovery)?

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** two nodes exchanging an encrypted message by identity; a restart-and-recover.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a small Reticulum feature — two nodes exchanging encrypted messages addressed by cryptographic identity, with no server, no IP addresses, no DNS. The mental shift I'd share is from address-based networking, where you connect to an assigned IP, to identity-based networking, where a destination _is_ a key and the network is infrastructure-independent and delay-tolerant."
- **Design review:** a peer models it as client/server with an address to connect to. Explain why that's the TCP/IP frame, and how identity-addressing and announces replace it.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been playing with Reticulum — a networking stack that needs no internet, no IP addresses, just cryptographic identities over any medium. It's what resilient, independent networking looks like."
- **Reach-back (sovereignty thread):** M32 was hardware you own; this is a network no one controls; M34 is knowing who's on your network. Name the shared conviction — computing you don't have to trust an outside authority for.

---

## M34 — Who's Talking to Your Machine · connection detection & covert channels · (M)

> **Story.** As someone on a security track, I want a defensive monitor that shows what's connected to my own machine and understands how connections hide, so that I can reason about the real 2026 threat landscape — not just the connections that are easy to see. **MVP.** A tool that, on _my own_ device, enumerates open connections, baselines what's normal, and flags unexpected outbound connections with their owning process and remote endpoint. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - It lists the machine's current connections, each mapped to the process that owns it.
> - It establishes a baseline and flags a _new_ unexpected connection without drowning you in false positives on normal traffic.
> - You can name several categories of covert channel and say, honestly, which ones a connection-listing monitor can and cannot catch.

**Anchors:** the networking primer; the security material from M4; your Security+ track. _This is strictly defensive — watching your own machine — and the honest-verb discipline applies: you'll be fluent in the posture and concepts, not a threat-hunting expert._

### Briefing

Start from the basics. When two programs talk over a network, the operating system tracks each live link as a **socket** — one endpoint of a connection, identified by an address and a **port** (a number that distinguishes one connection or service from another on the same machine). An **established connection** is a live link between your machine and another. Your OS knows every open socket, and tools like `netstat`, `ss`, and `lsof` list them.

Here's the honest problem, and the reason this is a real module: **not every connection is easy to see.** Malicious software and _covert channels_ hide — smuggling data out over DNS queries, piggybacking on protocols you already allow, or (with deep compromise) hiding below the view of the standard tools. So detection isn't "run `netstat` and you're done." It's knowing your normal **baseline** and spotting **anomalies**, plus understanding the _categories_ of covert channel well enough to know what you might be missing. The center of gravity is **what a connection is at the OS level, enumerating your own machine's connections, and grasping the threat landscape** so your monitor flags the unexpected without crying wolf.

### The trap

**Believing a socket listing is the whole picture** — and its opposite, drowning in noise. The "constantly advancing threat vector" is real: DNS tunneling, protocol piggybacking, timing/storage channels, and rootkit-hidden connections all mean a connection list is _necessary but not sufficient_. The trap is a false sense of completeness. The mirror trap: every browser tab and OS service opens outbound connections, so "an external connection" is almost always benign — flag them all and your tool is useless. Telling signal from noise is the actual skill.

### The build

A defensive monitor for _your own_ machine: enumerate current connections (via the OS tools' output or an API), baseline what's normal, and flag new/unexpected outbound connections with the process and remote endpoint. Purely observational, on your own device. A connection record is a spec: `{ local_addr:port, remote_addr:port, state, pid, process }`. **No exploitation, no building of hidden channels — detection and understanding only.**

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Data source.** Options: parse `netstat`/`ss`/`lsof` output · read the OS directly (`/proc` on Linux) · a library (`psutil`). _Axis:_ quick vs robust vs portable. _Forcing:_ Which gives you the connection _and_ its owning process reliably, without brittle text parsing?
2. **Baseline strategy.** Options: an allowlist of known-good · anomaly-from-history. _Axis:_ explicit vs adaptive. _Forcing:_ How does the tool learn "normal," and how does it treat a connection it's never seen?
3. **What "suspicious" means.** Options: a new remote · an unusual port · an unexpected process · a match against a known-bad list. _Axis:_ sensitivity vs noise. _Forcing:_ Which signals are worth an alert, and which just create fatigue?
4. **Polling vs event-driven.** Options: periodic snapshots · real-time (audit framework / eBPF). _Axis:_ simple vs immediate. _Forcing:_ Do you need to catch a short-lived connection the instant it opens, or is a snapshot every few seconds enough? (eBPF is the deep path — fence it.)
5. **Covert-channel awareness scope.** Options: detect what a socket listing can · explicitly document what it can't. _Axis:_ overclaiming vs honesty. _Forcing:_ Which categories (DNS tunneling, timing channels, rootkit-hidden) are simply _outside_ what your monitor sees — and do you say so?

### Research targets

- _What a socket, port, and connection are at the OS level._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain a TCP connection's endpoints and states and how the OS tracks them. _Seed:_ a "TCP connection states" primer; your networking primer.
- _Enumerating connections: `netstat`/`ss`/`lsof`, `/proc`, or `psutil`._ — **25 min.** Done when you can list your established connections and map each to a process.
- _Categories of covert channel in 2026 (DNS tunneling, protocol piggybacking, timing/storage, rootkit-hidden)._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name several and say, honestly, which a socket-listing monitor can and can't catch. _Seed:_ search "covert channel taxonomy" and "DNS tunneling detection" — read defensive sources.
- _Baselining and anomaly detection for connections._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe separating normal from anomalous without a flood of false positives.

### Out of scope (fence)

Writing _any_ covert channel or exploit (never — detection only), deep packet inspection at scale, kernel-level eBPF programming (note — park; the advanced real-time path), full IDS internals (Suricata/Zeek — note, park), attribution and incident response. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run your monitor on your own machine, let it baseline, then open a new app that makes an outbound connection. **The number:** does it flag the _new_ connection with its process and remote endpoint, _without_ flooding you on normal traffic? And can you name one covert-channel category your monitor would _miss_ (honest limits)?

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the baseline, then a newly-opened connection correctly flagged with its process; state one category you'd miss.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a defensive connection monitor for my own machine — it enumerates open sockets, baselines normal traffic, and flags unexpected outbound connections with the owning process. The honest framing is that a socket listing is necessary but not sufficient: covert channels like DNS tunneling hide from it, so detection is about baselines and anomalies and knowing what you _can't_ see — not a false sense of completeness. This is defensive posture and concepts, not DFIR expertise I'm claiming."
- **Design review:** a peer alerts on _every_ external connection and drowns in noise, or trusts `netstat` as complete. Discuss baselining to cut false positives, and the covert-channel categories that evade a socket listing.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been building a defensive tool that watches what's talking to my own machine — the interesting part is how much hides from a simple connection list, so it's really about knowing your baseline."
- **Reach-back (security + sovereignty threads):** M4 guarded identity, M39 hardens the app surface; this watches the machine itself. And M33 built a network no one controls, while this asks who's on _yours_. Name the shared instinct about not trusting the network blindly.
