# PART E — Foundations & Floors

_The rigor under the algorithms, and the machine under the Observable. M35 is a study module — its "build" is a portfolio of proofs — and M36 removes the magic from M5's RxJS by rebuilding it from raw bytes up. The floor thread, at its foundation._

---

## M35 — Why It's True · functions & proofs, a slow walk · (M)

> **Story.** As a self-taught engineer who learns by deriving, I want to write real proofs from zero, so that I can reason rigorously about correctness and termination — the difference between "it passed my tests" and "it's true for all inputs." **MVP.** A small portfolio of proofs, each using a named technique: one direct, one by contrapositive, one by contradiction, and one by induction — ideally one tied to code you've written. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Each proof states the claim, names the technique, and moves in gap-free steps.
> - The induction proof has a correct base case and an inductive step that doesn't assume what it's proving.
> - You can state, out loud, why a proof establishes something for _all_ cases, not just the ones you checked.

**Anchors:** `functions_and_proofs_slow_walk.md` (the primary text for this module); your discrete-math docs (`Chapter_3_extended`, the Discrete Math guides). This is a _study_ module — no code required, though tying a proof to code you wrote is ideal.

### Briefing

Let's begin with a plain question: what _is_ a proof? In everyday life, a few examples convince us. In mathematics and computer science, examples are never enough — you show a claim holds for _every_ case by argument, not by checking. A **proof** is a chain of logical steps from things already known to be true to the claim, with no gaps.

Why should a programmer care? Because the guarantees you lean on daily are proofs in disguise: _this loop terminates_, _this function is correct for all inputs_, _this algorithm is O(n log n)_. The ability to argue rigorously is what separates "it worked on my tests" from "it cannot be wrong." And **functions**, in the rigorous sense (not just code): a function maps each input to exactly one output, and properties like **injective** (no two inputs share an output), **surjective** (every possible output is produced), and **bijective** (both) sit underneath hashing, encoding, and data-structure correctness. The center of gravity is **mathematical maturity** — reading and writing proofs, and the core techniques: **direct**, **contrapositive**, **contradiction**, and **induction** — with induction the one that powers all reasoning about loops and recursion.

### The trap

**The "proof by example" reflex, and the hand-wave.** Checking a handful of cases _feels_ like proof but isn't — a claim can hold for the first thousand inputs and fail at the thousand-and-first (there are famous examples). And the hidden gap: a proof containing an unjustified "clearly" or "obviously" is not a proof. The discipline is leaving _no_ gaps — every step earns its place.

### The build

A portfolio of proofs, written from zero, each with a named technique: one **direct** proof, one by **contrapositive**, one by **contradiction**, and one by **induction** (ideally about something you've coded — a sum formula, or the correctness/termination of a loop). Plus a rigorous statement of one function property (e.g. why a hash collision is exactly a _non-injective_ mapping). Each proof states the claim, the technique, and gap-free steps.

### Decision map

▸ _This module's "decisions" are which technique fits — write your reasoning before proving._

1. **Direct vs contrapositive vs contradiction.** _Axis:_ which makes the argument tractable. _Forcing:_ For "if n² is even then n is even," is a direct proof or the contrapositive ("if n is odd then n² is odd") easier — and why?
2. **When induction is the tool.** _Forcing:_ Which claims are "for all natural numbers n" or about a recursive/looping structure — and why does induction fit exactly those?
3. **Weak vs strong induction.** _Forcing:_ When do you need to assume _all_ smaller cases hold, not just the immediately previous one?
4. **Base case and inductive step.** _Forcing:_ Where does a proof break if the base case is wrong, or if the step quietly assumes the very thing it's proving?
5. **Rigor vs hand-wave.** _Forcing:_ Which of your steps is a "clearly" hiding a gap? Can you justify it explicitly?

### Research targets

- _What a proof is, and why examples don't suffice._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain "true for all cases" vs "checked some," tab closed. _Seed:_ `functions_and_proofs_slow_walk.md`.
- _The techniques: direct, contrapositive, contradiction._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state when each applies and prove one small claim with each.
- _Induction (weak and strong), and its link to recursion/loops._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can prove a sum formula and explain why induction mirrors a loop invariant. _Seed:_ your discrete-math docs.
- _Functions rigorously: injective / surjective / bijective._ — **25 min.** Done when you can define each and connect one to hashing or encoding.

### Out of scope (fence)

Heavy formal logic and set theory beyond the basics, proof assistants (Coq/Lean — fascinating, note, park), real analysis, the full discrete-math course. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

The real test is reproduction. **The number:** can you, on a blank page with no notes, state and prove one claim _by induction_ from scratch — base case, inductive hypothesis, inductive step, no gaps — and explain aloud why it holds for _all_ n? One clean induction proof reproduced cold is the bar.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>My induction feels circular — I'm using what I'm trying to prove</summary>

**Orient:** the inductive step doesn't assume the claim _in general_; it assumes it for the _previous_ case (the inductive hypothesis) and uses that to establish the _next_ one — that's borrowing, not circularity.

**Approach:** be explicit about three things. The **base case**: prove the claim for the smallest n outright (no assumption). The **inductive hypothesis**: assume the claim holds for n = k (one specific case). The **inductive step**: using only that assumption, prove it for n = k+1. The whole thing works like dominoes — the base tips the first, the step guarantees each tips the next, so _all_ fall. If your step is assuming the k+1 case itself, that's the circularity; you may only assume k.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** reproduce one induction proof cold, narrating each part.

- **Interview (90s):** "I did a slow walk through proofs — direct, contrapositive, contradiction, induction. The one that pays off for engineering is induction, because it's the same shape as a loop invariant: prove a base case, assume it holds for the previous step, show it holds for the next. I proved [a sum formula / a loop's correctness] that way, and I can reproduce it on a whiteboard."
- **Design review:** a peer says an algorithm is correct because it passes tests. Explain the difference between testing and proof, and sketch how you'd _prove_ a small loop invariant.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been strengthening my proof muscles — the click was realizing induction is just a loop invariant in math clothes."
- **Reach-back (foundations):** M20's parser and M11's BOM are recursion, and induction is how you prove recursion correct. Take one and state the base case and inductive step you'd prove.

---

## M36 — The Observable, From the Floor Up · C streams to a C++ Observable · (L)

> **Story.** As someone who learns by building the machine under the magic, I want to rebuild an RxJS Observable from raw byte streams, so that I finally understand what an Observable _is_ — not a library feature, but a protocol. **MVP.** A byte stream processed incrementally in C, then a minimal Observable in C++ with `subscribe`, one or two operators (`map`, `filter`), and clean teardown. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - The C stage processes bytes _as they arrive_, without loading everything into memory.
> - The C++ Observable lets a consumer subscribe with on-next/on-error/on-complete callbacks and get back a way to unsubscribe.
> - Values flow through a `map`→`filter` chain transformed correctly; `unsubscribe` stops further callbacks.
> - The C portions are valgrind-clean — no leaks in the callback chain.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (streams, the event loop — M5 was written on top of this); M5 (RxJS — this is its floor); M18 (manual memory and ownership).

### Briefing

Start from the simplest idea. Data doesn't always arrive all at once — sometimes it comes a piece at a time, over time: bytes off a network, lines from a file, readings from a sensor. A **stream** is exactly that: a sequence of values arriving over time rather than a batch you already hold. In C, the rawest stream is reading bytes as they come.

Now the leap. Back in M5 you used an RxJS **Observable** — an object you could `filter`, `map`, and cancel — and it felt like magic. This module removes the magic by rebuilding it from the floor. Here is what an Observable _actually is_, stated plainly: **not a data structure, but a protocol.** A producer _pushes_ values to a consumer's callbacks — `onNext` for each value, `onError` if something breaks, `onComplete` when the stream ends — and hands back a way to _unsubscribe_ (a teardown). That push relationship, and its lifecycle, is the entire idea RxJS wraps in convenience. Building it up — raw byte streams in C, then the Observable protocol in C++ — teaches streaming and reactivity from the metal. The center of gravity is **the Observable-as-push-protocol and the machinery RxJS hides**: callbacks, subscription lifecycle, teardown, and backpressure.

### The trap

**Thinking an Observable is a container of data.** It is _not_ a list you pull values out of; it's a _push_ relationship where the producer calls _you_. Get that backwards and nothing about teardown or operators makes sense. The lifecycle traps follow: who owns the callbacks, when teardown runs, what happens if the consumer can't keep up (**backpressure**), and — in C and C++ specifically — the memory ownership of the whole callback chain, which is a fertile field for the M18 bugs (leaks, use-after-free) all over again.

### The build

Staged. **In C:** read a byte stream and process it incrementally (never load it all). **In C++:** build a minimal Observable — a `subscribe(onNext, onError, onComplete)` that returns an unsubscribe handle, a producer that pushes values, and one or two operators (`map`, `filter`) that wrap a source Observable into a new one — then demonstrate subscription and teardown. The protocol is your spec: `subscribe(observer) -> Subscription`, where `observer = { onNext, onError, onComplete }` and `Subscription.unsubscribe()`. You are rebuilding RxJS's core, not using it.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Representing callbacks in C vs C++.** Options (C): a function pointer + a `void*` context. Options (C++): `std::function`, or templates / function objects. _Axis:_ raw mechanism vs ergonomic, type-safe closures. _Forcing:_ How does a callback "remember" its state — the `void*` + function-pointer pattern in C, versus a capturing `std::function` in C++? What is each costing you?
2. **Subscription and teardown ownership.** Options: the caller owns and calls `unsubscribe` · RAII (the subscription cleans up in its destructor). _Axis:_ explicit vs automatic. _Forcing:_ When exactly does teardown run, and who guarantees the callbacks aren't invoked _after_ unsubscribe? (Reaches M18's ownership discipline.)
3. **Push vs pull (hot vs cold).** _Forcing:_ Does your Observable start producing when subscribed (cold) or push regardless of listeners (hot)? What changes for teardown?
4. **Operators as source-wrapping.** _Forcing:_ How does `filter` subscribe to _its_ source and conditionally forward `onNext`? How does an `unsubscribe` at the bottom propagate back up the chain?
5. **Backpressure.** _Forcing:_ If the producer pushes faster than the consumer handles, what happens — drop, buffer, or block? Why is this the problem streaming systems obsess over? (Fence the full solution.)
6. **Error propagation.** _Forcing:_ When the source errors, how does `onError` travel down the operator chain, and does it tear everything down?

### Research targets

- _What a stream is; reading bytes incrementally in C._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can process a byte stream without loading it whole, and say why that matters (reaches M40's laziness).
- _What an Observable actually is: the push protocol._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state, tab closed, that an Observable is a _protocol not a container_, and name its three callbacks plus teardown. _Seed:_ the ReactiveX "Observable contract" docs (read the _contract_, not RxJS source).
- _Callbacks in C (function pointer + context) and C++ (`std::function`, RAII)._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain how a callback carries state in each language.
- _Subscription lifecycle, teardown, and backpressure._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain teardown timing and what backpressure is.

### Out of scope (fence)

A full Rx operator library, schedulers/threading, hot/cold multicasting beyond the basics, a production allocator for the chain (note — park), C++ coroutines (fascinating — park). Keep it to a minimal Observable with one or two operators. → parking lot. **Sealed answer key:** RxJS source and full Rx implementations are post-commit reading — build your minimal version first.

### Measure ritual

Subscribe to your Observable through a `map`→`filter` chain, push values, then `unsubscribe`. **The number:** do the right values arrive transformed, does `unsubscribe` stop further `onNext`, and — running the C parts under **valgrind** — are there zero leaks in the callback chain? Teardown correctness plus valgrind-clean is the bar.

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>I don't see how an operator like filter "wraps" a source</summary>

**Orient:** an operator is itself an Observable whose `subscribe` _subscribes to the upstream_ and passes along a modified stream of callbacks — it sits in the middle of the push chain.

**Approach:** `filter(predicate)(source)` returns a new Observable. When someone subscribes to _it_ with their `onNext`, it subscribes to `source` with its _own_ `onNext` that says "if `predicate(value)`, call the downstream `onNext`; otherwise drop it." `onError`/`onComplete` pass straight through. Unsubscribing from the filter unsubscribes from the source. Every operator is this shape — subscribe upstream, transform the callbacks, forward teardown. Once you see one, `map` (transform the value instead of dropping it) is the same skeleton.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** values flowing through `map`→`filter`; `unsubscribe` halting them; a valgrind-clean run.

- **Interview (90s):** "I rebuilt an RxJS Observable from the floor up — raw byte streams in C, then the Observable protocol in C++. The insight is that an Observable isn't a data structure, it's a push protocol: a producer calling your onNext/onError/onComplete and handing back a teardown. I built map and filter as source-wrapping operators and made subscription teardown clean and leak-free."
- **Design review:** a peer thinks of an Observable as a list you pull from. Correct the pull-vs-push model and explain teardown and backpressure as the real hard parts.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I removed the magic from RxJS by rebuilding an Observable in C++ — turns out it's just a producer pushing to callbacks with a way to unsubscribe."
- **Reach-back (floor + time threads):** M5 handed you Observables; now you've built one. What was RxJS doing for you that you now see explicitly — and how does this connect to M40's lazy iteration and M43's reactivity-from-scratch?

---

# Capstone

_One module that is your own portfolio thesis made concrete: instruments that make the invisible workings of programs legible. It closes the instruments thread and rhymes with dev-suite and Steppe._

---

## M37 — Make It Legible · interactive diagrams & custom graphs · (L)

> **Story.** As a creative technologist whose thesis is "instruments that make the invisible workings of programs legible," I want a toolkit for interactive diagrams — graphs and algorithms I draw or dream up, rendered as objects you can poke — so that I have the recurring instrument behind my portfolio and my "used code" site. **MVP.** A small toolkit that renders a graph/algorithm/data-structure as interactive objects — hover to inspect, click to select, drag to rearrange — built so a diagram is a reusable object you can drop onto a page. 3D optional. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - A non-trivial graph renders and responds correctly to hover, click, and drag (the right shape reacts).
> - It stays smooth (~60fps) as the graph grows.
> - The same diagram object can be dropped onto a second page unchanged (it's composable).
> - You can explain the SVG-vs-canvas hit-testing tradeoff and which you chose and why.

**Anchors:** M17 (the render loop, 3D, `requestAnimationFrame`); M15/M16 (rendering and layout); M6 and M43 (where interaction state lives); your Steppe and dev-suite work, which this generalizes.

### Briefing

Start from your own thesis. A _static_ diagram explains once; an _interactive_ one lets you poke it, and poking is how understanding sticks. What makes a diagram "interactive"? The shapes on screen respond to the mouse (hover, click), can be moved or transformed, and are _reusable objects_ rather than a one-off picture.

This capstone builds a small toolkit for exactly that: you draw or specify a visual — a graph, an algorithm's changing state, a data structure — and it renders as manipulable objects you can hover, click, transmute, and maybe view in 3D. The center of gravity is **the diagram as a first-class interactive object, and the central rendering tradeoff.** That tradeoff is worth stating plainly, because it decides everything else: with **SVG**, each shape is a real DOM element, so the browser hands you mouse events and _hit-testing_ (knowing which shape the cursor is over) for free — but thousands of elements get slow. With **canvas** (or **WebGL** for 3D), you draw pixels, so it's fast at scale — but "what did I just click?" becomes _your_ problem, and you must map mouse coordinates back to shapes yourself.

### The trap

**Hit-testing on custom-drawn graphics, and the composability wall.** With SVG you get per-shape click/hover free, but it won't scale to a huge graph; with canvas you scale, but figuring out which shape the mouse is over is now code you write. And composability: a diagram that's a tangle of one-off drawing code can't be reused or "transmuted" — making each diagram a self-contained object with its own state and a clean interface is the hard _design_ part, and it's exactly the line between a throwaway demo and a real instrument.

### The build

A small diagramming toolkit: render a graph/algorithm/structure as interactive objects (hover to inspect, click to select, drag to rearrange, optionally animate an algorithm's steps), built so a diagram is a reusable object you drop onto a page — optionally in 3D. It consumes a graph/structure description you define and produces interactive visuals. This is the instrument behind your "used code" portfolio.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building. Sketch the target diagrams first._

1. **SVG vs canvas vs WebGL/3D.** _Axis:_ free events and easy-at-small-scale vs performance and manual hit-testing vs 3D. _Forcing:_ How many nodes before SVG's per-element DOM cost hurts? Do you need 3D — and if so, how does hit-testing work there (raycasting)? (Reaches M17.)
2. **Hit-testing strategy (if canvas/WebGL).** Options: track each shape's geometry and test the mouse coords · an off-screen "color-picking" buffer · a spatial index (quadtree). _Axis:_ simple vs fast at scale. _Forcing:_ How do you know which of a thousand drawn shapes the mouse is over, cheaply?
3. **Diagram as an object — the interface.** Options: a config-driven component · an imperative API · a small DSL. _Axis:_ reuse and "transmutability" vs simplicity. _Forcing:_ What must a diagram expose so it can be re-used, re-themed, and transformed rather than re-coded?
4. **Graph layout.** Options: manual positions · a force-directed layout · a hierarchical layout. _Axis:_ control vs automatic. _Forcing:_ For an arbitrary graph you dream up, who positions the nodes — you, or an algorithm?
5. **Animating algorithm steps.** Options: none · step-through state (this is Steppe's territory). _Axis:_ static vs teaching. _Forcing:_ To show an algorithm _working_, how do you represent and advance its steps as visual state? (`rAF` from M17; a single source of truth like M30.)
6. **State + interaction model.** _Forcing:_ Where does the diagram's selection/hover/position state live, and how does it stay in sync as you interact? (Reaches M43's reactivity, M6's state.)

### Research targets

- _SVG vs canvas vs WebGL for interactive graphics — events, scale, hit-testing._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state the hit-testing tradeoff and the scale where each wins. _Seed:_ an "SVG vs canvas" comparison; MDN canvas.
- _Hit-testing on canvas (geometry test vs color-picking buffer vs spatial index)._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can describe mapping a click to a shape without the DOM.
- _Graph layout algorithms (force-directed, hierarchical)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name how nodes get positioned automatically.
- _Making a visual a reusable, composable object._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe a diagram's interface for reuse and transmutation.
- _(If 3D) raycasting for 3D hit-testing._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain picking the object under the cursor in 3D. (Reaches M17.)

### Out of scope (fence)

A full graph-editor product, a diagramming DSL _with a parser_ (note — that's M20-flavored; park), physics beyond a simple force layout, export/serialization formats, a huge component library. Keep it a focused toolkit. → parking lot. Study visualizations you admire (d3 examples, your own Steppe lineage), but draft your own approach first (post-commit).

### Measure ritual

Render a non-trivial graph and interact — hover, click, drag, and (if built) step an algorithm. **The number:** do interactions correctly hit the intended shape (hit-testing right) and stay smooth (~60fps) as the graph grows? And can you drop the same diagram object onto a second page unchanged (composability)?

### Hints ladder

<details><summary>On canvas, I can't tell which shape the mouse clicked</summary>

**Orient:** unlike SVG, canvas has no elements — after you draw, the pixels don't remember what they are, so you have to keep the shapes' geometry yourself and test the click against it.

**Approach:** keep your shapes as data (positions and sizes), and on a click, test the mouse coordinates against each shape's bounds (point-in-circle, point-in-rect) — nearest/topmost wins. That's fine for hundreds of shapes; for thousands, testing every one per mouse-move is too slow, so reach for a spatial index (a quadtree that narrows candidates by region) or the color-picking trick (draw every shape in a unique flat color to an off-screen canvas, then read the pixel under the cursor to get its id). In 3D the same idea becomes raycasting — cast a ray from the camera through the cursor and see what it hits.

</details>

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** a non-trivial graph you can hover/click/drag (and step, if built); the same object reused on a second page.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a small interactive-diagram toolkit — graphs and algorithm states you can hover, click, drag, and step through, each a reusable object. The core decision is SVG versus canvas: SVG gives you events and hit-testing free but doesn't scale; canvas scales but you implement hit-testing — mapping a click back to a shape — yourself, which I did with [geometry tests / a quadtree]. It's the instrument behind my portfolio: making the invisible workings of programs legible."
- **Design review:** a peer's canvas diagram can't tell what was clicked, or their SVG one crawls at 5,000 nodes. Discuss the hit-testing and scale tradeoff and the fix for their case.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I'm building a toolkit that turns algorithms and graphs into things you can actually poke — hover, click, drag, 3D. Interactive diagrams are how I make code legible."
- **Reach-back (instruments thread — the capstone):** M26 captured notes, M27 captured selections, M44 designs the surface, dev-suite _observes_ a running system, Steppe _enacts_ an algorithm — and this makes any of it visual and manipulable. State your portfolio thesis in one sentence, and name which instrument this one is.

# PART F — Employability Bridges

_Five modules resurrected from the v2 lineup — the job-market breadth that got cut when we swapped in your twenty-five. A fourth API ecosystem, appsec from both sides, Pythonic laziness, safe shell, and a BI dashboard. Two of these (M38, M42) deliberately cut against your open-source grain; they're checkboxes, named as such._

---

## M38 — The Mirror Endpoint · C#/.NET · (S)

> **Story.** As someone who wants breadth across enterprise stacks, I want to serve my incidents contract in ASP.NET Core, so that I can honor one contract in a fourth ecosystem and speak C#/.NET credibly — a deliberate exception to my open-source grain, given my finance background. **MVP.** An ASP.NET Core minimal API serving M1's incidents contract, backed by an in-memory or EF Core store. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - M1's curl suite passes _verbatim_ against this server (same contract, different implementation).
> - A LINQ pipeline demonstrates deferred execution — you can show it running the number of times you expect, not twice.
> - Dependency-injection lifetimes are chosen deliberately (no captive dependency).
> - Nullable reference types are on.

**Anchors:** M1 (the written contract is the spec; its curl suite is the test); the contract thread. _A named MS exception._

### Briefing

.NET is Microsoft's cross-platform runtime and C# its main language; **ASP.NET Core** is its web framework, and it's everywhere in enterprise and finance — which, given your JPMC background, makes it a natural credibility add. Serving your _same_ incidents contract here — a fourth time, after Spring, FastAPI, and GraphQL — proves the contract was real and gives you a C# reference implementation. Define as you go: a **minimal API** (declaring endpoints without controller boilerplate), **dependency injection** (the framework creates and supplies your objects, each with a chosen lifetime), and **LINQ** (a query syntax over collections and databases, with a famous gotcha). The center of gravity is **the contract in a new ecosystem, and .NET's two classic traps.**

### The trap

**LINQ deferred execution, and DI lifetimes.** A LINQ query doesn't run when you write it — it runs when you _enumerate_ it, so enumerating twice runs it twice (a hidden double database hit, cousin of M2's N+1). And DI lifetimes: injecting a short-lived (scoped) service into a long-lived (singleton) one — a **captive dependency** — causes subtle, hard-to-find bugs.

### The build

An ASP.NET Core minimal API serving the incidents contract, one LINQ pipeline that demonstrates deferred execution, and DI lifetimes chosen on purpose.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Minimal API vs controllers.** _Axis:_ concise vs conventional structure. _Forcing:_ For one resource, which is clearer, and what does the controller ceremony buy at scale?
2. **DI lifetime per service.** Options: transient · scoped · singleton. _Axis:_ correctness vs reuse. _Forcing:_ Which lifetime for a per-request thing, and what breaks if you capture it inside a singleton? (The captive-dependency trap.)
3. **`IEnumerable` vs `IQueryable`.** _Axis:_ in-memory vs deferred-to-database. _Forcing:_ Which one defers to the database, and how does that interact with enumerating twice?
4. **DTO mapping.** _Axis:_ as in M1 — coupling vs boilerplate. _Forcing:_ Same boundary question — expose the entity or map to a DTO?
5. **Nullable reference types.** _Axis:_ compile-time null safety vs friction. _Forcing:_ What class of bug does turning them on catch before runtime?

### Research targets

- _Minimal APIs._ — **25 min.** Done when you can declare an endpoint and its response shape.
- _DI and service lifetimes + captive dependency._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name each lifetime and describe the captive-dependency bug. _Seed:_ the Microsoft DI docs.
- _Deferred execution: `IEnumerable` vs `IQueryable`._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain when a LINQ query actually runs and why enumerating twice hits twice.
- _Nullable reference types._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say what they catch.

### Out of scope (fence)

Full EF Core migrations (M10-adjacent), Blazor, gRPC, the wider ASP.NET ecosystem, authentication (M4). → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run M1's curl suite verbatim. **The number:** does every response match the contract byte-for-byte? Then turn on enumeration/SQL logging and confirm your LINQ query runs the number of times you expect — not two.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the curl suite passing; the query running once, not twice.

- **Interview (90s):** "I served my incidents contract in ASP.NET Core — the fourth ecosystem after Spring, FastAPI, and GraphQL, which proves the contract was real. The .NET-specific story is LINQ deferred execution: a query runs when enumerated, so enumerating twice is a hidden double hit — the N+1 in a new costume — and I chose DI lifetimes deliberately to avoid a captive dependency."
- **Design review:** a peer injects a scoped service into a singleton. Explain the captive-dependency bug and the fix.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I rebuilt the same API in C#/.NET to keep it in my range — the fun gotcha is LINQ not running until you enumerate it."
- **Reach-back (contract thread):** four ecosystems now honor this contract. Which parts survived all four unchanged — and what does that tell you about where the contract ends and the implementation begins?

## M39 — Break It, Then Fix It · web app security · (M)

> **Story.** As someone on the Security+ track, I want to build deliberately vulnerable variants of my own endpoint, exploit them locally, then fix them, so that I understand the top web vulnerabilities from both sides and can defend a real codebase. **MVP.** Take your incidents endpoint; in a local sandbox, introduce a handful of classic vulnerabilities, demonstrate each exploit against your own code, fix each, and add a regression test. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - For each vulnerability, you can exploit it on your own local build, then show the fix defeats the exploit.
> - A regression test guards each fix.
> - A static-analysis pass flags the deliberate flaws and comes clean on the fixed version.
> - You can name which class each vuln belongs to and its standard defense.

**Anchors:** M4 (auth) and M26 (you already met XSS); the networking/security primer; your Security+ track. _Strictly defensive: your own code, your own sandbox. Honest-verb discipline: appsec fundamentals, not a pentester title._

### Briefing

The fastest way to _defend_ against an attack is to understand it — safely, on your own code. This module takes your incidents endpoint and deliberately makes vulnerable versions so you see the exploit and the fix from both sides. The classic categories (roughly the OWASP Top 10 shape), defined as you go: **injection** (SQL injection — untrusted input changing a query's meaning), **cross-site scripting** (XSS — untrusted input running as script in a browser, from M26), **server-side request forgery** (SSRF — tricking the server into making requests it shouldn't), and **secrets-in-code** (credentials committed where they leak). The center of gravity is **the top web-vulnerability classes and their defenses** — validation vs encoding, parameterization, allowlisting, secret management — learned by exploiting and fixing your own code.

### The trap

**Validation-versus-encoding confusion, and the denylist that felt complete.** People conflate _validating_ input (is it acceptable?) with _encoding_ output (rendering it safely) — different problems, and you usually need both. And **denylists** (block these bad characters) are always incomplete; **allowlists** (permit only these known-good) are the robust move. Trusting a denylist is the trap.

### The build

Introduce a handful of vulnerabilities into your own incidents endpoint — SQLi, XSS, SSRF, secrets-in-code, and one auth flaw (a JWT pitfall from M4) — exploit each locally, fix each, and add a regression test. _This guide names the classes and defenses; the exploits and fixes against your sandbox are yours._

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Validate vs encode.** _Axis:_ accepting safe input vs rendering safely. _Forcing:_ Which problem does each solve, and why do you usually need both?
2. **Allowlist vs denylist.** _Axis:_ robust vs always-incomplete. _Forcing:_ Why can't a denylist ever be finished, and what does an allowlist guarantee?
3. **Parameterization vs escaping (injection).** _Axis:_ structural fix vs patch. _Forcing:_ Why does a parameterized query make injection _structurally_ impossible where escaping only tries to sanitize?
4. **CSP strategy (XSS).** _Axis:_ defense-in-depth vs simplicity. _Forcing:_ What does a Content-Security-Policy add _on top of_ output encoding?
5. **Secret storage.** _Axis:_ convenience vs not leaking. _Forcing:_ Where do credentials belong if not in code, and what leaks if they're committed?
6. **Real fix vs mitigation.** _Forcing:_ Which of your fixes removes the vulnerability versus merely making it harder?

### Research targets

- _Injection and parameterization._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why parameterized queries defeat SQLi structurally.
- _XSS and output encoding + CSP._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name the output-encoding fix and what CSP adds. _Seed:_ the OWASP XSS cheat sheet.
- _SSRF and allowlisting._ — **25 min.** Done when you can describe the attack and the allowlist defense.
- _Secrets management._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say where secrets should live.
- _The current OWASP Top 10 for 2026._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can name the major categories. _Seed:_ the OWASP Top 10; PortSwigger Web Security Academy (defensive).

### Out of scope (fence)

Building a real attack tool for use elsewhere (never — your own sandbox only), advanced exploitation, a full pentest methodology, WAF configuration, the whole OWASP catalog. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

For each vulnerability, attempt the exploit before and after the fix. **The number:** how many exploits succeed before the fix and fail after (should be all)? Plus a static-analysis pass (e.g. semgrep) — does it flag the deliberate flaws and clear the fixed version?

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** an exploit working on the vulnerable build and defeated on the fixed one; the regression test; the static-analysis delta.

- **Interview (90s):** "I took my own incidents endpoint and built deliberately vulnerable versions in a sandbox — SQLi, XSS, SSRF, a secret in code, a JWT flaw — then exploited and fixed each with a regression test. The concept I'd share is validation versus encoding: they solve different problems, you usually need both, and allowlists beat denylists because a denylist is always incomplete."
- **Design review:** a peer sanitizes input with a denylist and calls it secure. Explain why allowlisting plus output encoding is the robust combination, and where their denylist leaks.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been learning appsec by attacking my own code — building the vulnerabilities on purpose, then fixing them, is the fastest way to actually understand OWASP."
- **Reach-back (security thread):** M4 guarded identity, M34 watches the network, this hardens the app surface. Which of your five deliberate vulns maps to a defense you'd add to your M1/M2 endpoints for real?

## M40 — The Lazy Pipeline · Python · (S)

> **Story.** As someone deepening Python fluency, I want to build a memory-lazy data pipeline, so that I understand iterators, decorators, and context managers — the Pythonic machinery under real data code. **MVP.** A pipeline that processes a large file lazily (without loading it), with a hand-written timing decorator and a memoization decorator, and a context manager owning a resource. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - The pipeline processes a file far larger than the memory it uses (laziness proven by measurement).
> - A hand-written `@timed` decorator reports each stage's time.
> - A `@memoize` decorator caches by argument, and you handle the hashable-key requirement.
> - A context manager cleanly acquires and releases a resource even when an error is thrown.

**Anchors:** the Python and data-science books; M36 (streams) and M14 (the same laziness instinct); M6 (the hash thread, via memoize's keys).

### Briefing

Python's real power for data work is a few features that let you process more data than fits in memory, cleanly. Define as you go: an **iterator** (an object you step through one item at a time), a **generator** (a function that _yields_ items lazily, producing each only when asked, so it never holds the whole sequence), a **decorator** (a function that wraps another to add behavior — timing, caching — without changing it), and a **context manager** (the `with` block — sets something up and _guarantees_ teardown, even on error). The center of gravity is **laziness (generators) and Python's closure-based machinery (decorators, context managers)** — the same streaming instinct as M36, in Python.

### The trap

**Mutable default arguments, late-binding closures, and the three-layer decorator.** A default like `def f(x, cache={})` is created _once_ and shared across every call — a classic silent bug. Closures created in a loop capture the _variable_, not its value, so they all see the last one (late binding). And a decorator that takes _arguments_ needs _three_ nested layers of function — which trips everyone the first time.

### The build

A lazy pipeline reading a big file via generators (no full load), a hand-written `@timed` decorator, a `@memoize` decorator (whose cache-by-argument requires hashable keys — the hash thread), and a context manager owning a resource. A small CLI ties it together.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Generators vs lists.** _Axis:_ constant memory vs having it all in hand. _Forcing:_ At what file size does loading a list stop being viable, and what does `yield` change?
2. **Decorator with arguments (three layers) vs simple decorator.** _Axis:_ configurability vs simplicity. _Forcing:_ Why does taking an argument add a layer of nesting, and can you trace what each layer returns?
3. **Memoize cache: dict on a closure vs a class.** _Axis:_ simple vs stateful/inspectable. _Forcing:_ Where does the cache live across calls, and what must be true of the arguments to key on them? (Reaches M6's hashability.)
4. **Type hints + mypy or not.** _Axis:_ caught-early vs faster to write. _Forcing:_ What does static checking buy on a pipeline?
5. **Where the GIL matters here.** _Forcing:_ Is this workload helped by threads, or is that a different problem? (Fence the GIL deep-dive.)

### Research targets

- _The iterator protocol and generators._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain lazy production with `yield`.
- _Decorators and closures — why three layers for arguments._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can write and trace a decorator that takes an argument. _Seed:_ a solid decorators explainer.
- _Context managers (`__enter__`/`__exit__`)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can guarantee teardown on error.
- _The mutable-default-argument and late-binding-closure gotchas._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain both bugs.

### Out of scope (fence)

`async`/`await` and asyncio (note, park — a whole area), multiprocessing/GIL deep-dive, metaclasses, a full ETL framework. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run the pipeline on a file much larger than your intended memory — generator version vs a list-loading version. **The number:** peak memory of each (via `/usr/bin/time -v` or `tracemalloc`) — laziness made visible as flat-versus-huge memory.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the memory delta between generator and list versions; the decorators working; teardown on error.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a lazy log pipeline in Python — generators so it processes a file bigger than memory, a hand-written timing decorator, and a memoize decorator. Two Pythonic gotchas I can explain: mutable default arguments are created once and shared, and a decorator that takes arguments needs three nested layers. I proved the laziness by measuring peak memory staying flat while the list version blew up."
- **Design review:** a peer loads a whole file into a list and uses a mutable default arg as a cache. Name both bugs and the generator/closure fixes.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been getting properly fluent in Python's laziness — generators let you stream a file bigger than your RAM, and it's the same idea as a data stream."
- **Reach-back (floor + data threads):** laziness here is the streaming instinct of M36 and M14 in Python. What's shared about _not_ materializing the whole sequence, and where does memoize touch the hash thread (M6)?

## M41 — The Watchdog · bash scripting · (S)

> **Story.** As someone with an SRE identity, I want a robust health-check watchdog script, so that I can write shell that's actually safe — quoting, error handling, cleanup — the daily language of ops. **MVP.** A bash script that polls my incidents endpoint, parses the JSON, logs with rotation, retries with backoff, and cleans up on exit — shellcheck-clean. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - It polls the endpoint and reports health; JSON is parsed correctly (with `jq`).
> - Logs rotate rather than growing unbounded; failed checks retry with increasing delay.
> - A `trap` cleans up temp files/state on SIGINT.
> - `shellcheck` reports zero findings.

**Anchors:** M1/M38 (the endpoint it watches, and its error shape); your SRE lane and CI/CD background.

### Briefing

Bash is the glue language of operations — health checks, deploys, cron jobs on every server. It's also famous for silent, dangerous bugs, so writing it _safely_ is a real skill and a natural fit for your SRE lane. Define as you go: **word splitting** (the shell splits an unquoted `$var` on spaces, so a value with a space becomes two arguments — a huge bug source), **`set -euo pipefail`** (flags that make the shell fail loudly on errors, undefined variables, and failed pipes — with pitfalls of their own), **`trap`** (run cleanup when the script exits or is interrupted), and **`jq`** (parse JSON on the command line). The center of gravity is **safe shell — quoting, error handling, cleanup — around a real watchdog.**

### The trap

**Word splitting/quoting, and `set -e`'s comforting lies.** Unquoted variables split on whitespace and glob, silently breaking on filenames with spaces. And `set -e` (exit-on-error) has surprising exceptions — it doesn't fire inside an `if`, in a pipeline without `pipefail`, or in a command substitution — so trusting it blindly gives _false_ safety.

### The build

A watchdog polling your incidents endpoint (M1/M38), parsing JSON with `jq`, logging with rotation, retrying with backoff, cleaning up via `trap`, and running parallel checks with `wait`. Understand `set -euo pipefail`, and quote everything.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **bash vs POSIX `sh` vs "this should be Python now."** _Axis:_ ubiquity vs portability vs the graduation threshold. _Forcing:_ At what point does a script outgrow bash and want a real language?
2. **Error strategy.** Options: `set -e` alone · `set -euo pipefail` · explicit checks. _Axis:_ terse vs actually-safe. _Forcing:_ Where does `set -e` silently _not_ fire, and what covers those gaps?
3. **`jq` vs `awk`/`grep` for JSON.** _Axis:_ correct JSON parsing vs improvisation. _Forcing:_ Why is parsing JSON with `grep` fragile?
4. **cron vs systemd timer.** _Axis:_ classic vs modern scheduling. _Forcing:_ What does a systemd timer give you that cron doesn't (logging, dependencies)?

### Research targets

- _Quoting and word splitting._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain why every variable expansion gets quoted. _Seed:_ Greg's Wiki (BashPitfalls).
- _`set -euo pipefail` — what each does and its pitfalls._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name a case where `set -e` doesn't fire.
- _`trap` for cleanup._ — **25 min.** Done when you can clean up temp files on interrupt.
- _`jq` basics._ — **25 min.** Done when you can extract a field from a JSON response.

### Out of scope (fence)

A full deployment system, a config-management tool, complex `getopts` argument parsing, a TUI. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run `shellcheck`. **The number:** zero findings? Then send SIGINT mid-run and confirm the `trap` cleans up — no leftover temp files or locks. shellcheck-clean plus clean teardown is the bar.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** a shellcheck-clean run; a clean teardown on Ctrl-C.

- **Interview (90s):** "I wrote a health-check watchdog in bash, shellcheck-clean, that polls my endpoint, parses JSON with `jq`, rotates logs, retries with backoff, and cleans up via `trap`. The safety points: quote everything or word splitting breaks on spaces, and `set -e` isn't the safety net people think — it has real exceptions, so I combined it with `pipefail` and explicit checks."
- **Design review:** a peer's script uses unquoted variables and trusts `set -e` alone. Show a filename-with-spaces failure and a case where `set -e` silently doesn't fire.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been writing bash the safe way for my SRE prep — the eye-opener is how many silent bugs hide behind an unquoted variable."
- **Reach-back (contract + ops):** this watches the same incidents contract from M1, from the ops floor. What does a health check need from the contract's _error shape_ (M1's design) to tell "down" from "up" reliably?

## M42 — The Credential Dashboard · Power BI · (S)

> **Story.** As someone building a standalone BI credential, I want one polished Power BI dashboard over exported data, so that I have a clean artifact demonstrating data-viz and DAX — kept separate from my engineering portfolio, and a deliberate exception to my open-source grain. **MVP.** A Power BI dashboard over a data slice, with a small model, a few DAX measures, slicers, and one drill-through, publish-ready. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - The dashboard presents a coherent story over the data.
> - A few DAX measures compute correctly, and you can explain filter context on one.
> - Slicers filter the visuals; a drill-through navigates to detail.
> - The report is visually restrained — taste, not clutter.

**Anchors:** the data-viz instincts from your React work; M2/M8 (the analytics data this can consume). _A named MS exception, kept separate from your engineering-portfolio narrative per your own positioning._

### Briefing

Power BI is Microsoft's business-intelligence tool for turning data into interactive dashboards, and it's a widely-requested job skill — worth one clean artifact. Define as you go: a **measure** (a calculation, written in the **DAX** language, that recomputes based on what's filtered), a **calculated column** (a value computed per-row at load — different from a measure), and **filter context** (the set of filters — from slicers, rows, visuals — active when a measure is evaluated; the one concept that trips every DAX beginner). The center of gravity is **the data model and DAX, especially filter context** — why a measure shows different numbers in different visual contexts.

### The trap

**Filter context, and measure-versus-calculated-column confusion.** A DAX measure's result depends on the filters active where it's shown, so the "same" measure gives different numbers in a total row versus a filtered cell — and beginners get "wrong" numbers because they don't think about filter context. And confusing a measure (dynamic, recomputed) with a calculated column (static, per-row at load) means the wrong tool and wrong results.

### The build

A dashboard over a data slice (e.g. exported from your M2/M8 analytics), a small star-ish model, three or four DAX measures, slicers, one drill-through, publish-ready. The data model (tables and relationships) is a spec you design.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Import vs DirectQuery.** _Axis:_ loaded-in speed vs live-queried freshness. _Forcing:_ Which fits a static credential artifact, and what does each cost?
2. **Star schema vs flat table.** _Axis:_ clean model vs one big table. _Forcing:_ What does a star buy DAX and your relationships?
3. **Measure vs calculated column.** _Axis:_ dynamic vs static per-row. _Forcing:_ For a given metric, which recomputes with filters, and which is fixed at load? (The trap.)
4. **Relationship cardinality.** _Axis:_ correctness. _Forcing:_ One-to-many or many-to-many, and what breaks if you get it wrong?
5. **Report restraint (a taste budget).** _Axis:_ informative vs cluttered. _Forcing:_ What do you leave _off_ the page?

### Research targets

- _Filter context and DAX._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain why a measure changes value by context. _Seed:_ SQLBI (Russo & Ferrari).
- _Measure vs calculated column._ — **25 min.** Done when you can state the difference and pick correctly.
- _Star schema basics._ — **25 min.** Done when you can model facts and dimensions.
- _Import vs DirectQuery._ — **25 min.** Done when you can say when each fits. _(Guy in a Cube for the practical side.)_

### Out of scope (fence)

Power BI service administration, row-level security, dataflows, complex DAX (time intelligence beyond basics), Power Query M-language depth. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Pick one DAX measure and explain, out loud, what filter context acts on it in two different visuals and how you know the number is right. **The number:** does the measure produce the correct value in a _total_ and in a _filtered cell_ (proving you understand filter context)?

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the dashboard with slicers and drill-through; one measure explained across two contexts.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a Power BI dashboard with a small star model and a few DAX measures. The one concept that matters is filter context — a measure recomputes based on what's filtered where it's displayed, so the same measure shows different numbers in a total versus a filtered cell, and understanding that is the difference between right and 'why is this wrong.'"
- **Design review:** a peer used a calculated column where a measure was needed and got a static wrong number. Explain the distinction and filter context.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I put together a Power BI dashboard as a standalone credential — the whole game is filter context, which is why DAX feels weird until it clicks."
- **Reach-back (data thread):** this is the OLAP/analytics end of the data-lifecycle — the same data M2 stored and M10 migrated. What does presenting data for _humans to explore_ demand that serving it over an API (M1) doesn't?

# PART G — Craft & Frontier

_Four modules resurrected from the v2 lineup — the craft and identity work: reactivity rebuilt to show what React hides, a proper design file, and the two that closed the v2 plan and pull hardest on your tools-for-thought instincts — a local-first spaced-repetition deck and a shared multiplayer room. The last two are the sync line: they deliberately duplicate their CRDT plumbing so you see the kernel before you extract it._

## M43 — Reactivity From Scratch · plain JavaScript · (S)

> **Story.** As someone who learns by building the machine under the abstraction, I want to build a tiny reactive system in vanilla JavaScript, so that I understand what React (and M5's RxJS) does for me — automatic UI updates when state changes. **MVP.** A `reactive()` and `effect()` in plain JS (Proxy-based) driving a small DOM widget, with updates batched so many state changes cause one render. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Mutating a reactive value automatically re-runs the effects that depend on it — no manual update calls.
> - The DOM widget updates when its state changes.
> - 1,000 rapid mutations cause a _single_ render (batching works).
> - Event handling uses delegation.

**Anchors:** _Waiting, All the Way Up_ (the event loop — the batching is its payoff); M5 (RxJS — this is a sibling floor); M36 (rebuilding an abstraction from scratch).

### Briefing

When you change a variable in React and the screen updates, something is quietly watching that variable and re-rendering — that "something" is **reactivity**, and building a tiny version in plain JavaScript shows you exactly what React and RxJS do for you. Define as you go: **reactivity** (state changes automatically trigger the code that depends on them), a **Proxy** (a JS object that intercepts reads and writes, so you can _notice_ when a value is read or changed), an **effect** (a function that re-runs whenever the reactive values it read change), and the **microtask queue** (where the browser runs small jobs right after the current code — where you batch updates so a burst of changes causes one render, not many). The center of gravity is **the reactive core** — tracking which effects read which values and re-running them on change, batched — the floor under React and M5's RxJS.

### The trap

**`this`, stale closures, and microtask timing.** Plain-JS reactivity runs into `this`-binding surprises and closures capturing stale values — and, the interesting one, _when_ to flush updates: run effects synchronously on every change and you re-render 1,000 times for 1,000 mutations; batch them onto the microtask queue and you render once. Getting batching right is the whole payoff.

### The build

A `reactive()` (wrap state in a Proxy that tracks reads and triggers effects on writes) and `effect()` (register a function, auto-track its dependencies, re-run on change) driving a small DOM widget, with event delegation and microtask-batched updates. The shape is your spec: `reactive(obj) -> proxy`; `effect(fn)` runs `fn` and re-runs when its reads change.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Tracking mechanism.** Options: `Proxy` · getter/setter · pub-sub. _Axis:_ transparent interception vs explicitness. _Forcing:_ Which lets you notice a plain property read without changing how you write state?
2. **Batching.** Options: synchronous · microtask · `requestAnimationFrame`. _Axis:_ immediate vs coalesced (the event loop, made executable). _Forcing:_ Why does flushing on a microtask turn 1,000 mutations into one render? (Reaches M5/M30.)
3. **Event delegation vs per-node listeners.** _Axis:_ one listener vs many. _Forcing:_ Why attach one handler to a parent instead of one per child?
4. **State shape.** _Forcing:_ Flat vs nested reactive objects — what does nesting cost your tracking?
5. **How much of React to reinvent.** _Forcing:_ Where do you _stop_? (Fence hard — this is a learning toy, not a framework.)

### Research targets

- _`Proxy` and dependency tracking._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain tracking which effect read which value. _Seed:_ MDN `Proxy`; the Vue reactivity explainer.
- _The microtask queue and batching._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain flushing effects once per tick. _Seed:_ Jake Archibald, "In the Loop."
- _Stale closures and `this`._ — **25 min.** Done when you can explain both surprises.
- _Event delegation._ — **25 min.** Done when you can route child events through a parent.

### Out of scope (fence)

A virtual DOM / diffing, a component system, JSX, a full framework, SSR. Keep it a reactive core plus one widget. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Put a render counter in your effect; fire 1,000 mutations in a tight loop. **The number:** how many renders result — 1,000 (unbatched) or 1 (batched)? The drop to 1 is the lesson.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** a value mutating and auto-updating the widget; the render counter showing 1 after 1,000 mutations.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a tiny reactive system in vanilla JS — `reactive()` with a Proxy that tracks which effects read which values, and `effect()` that re-runs on change. The payoff is batching: run effects on every mutation and 1,000 changes cause 1,000 renders; queue them on a microtask and it's one. That's what React and Vue do for you under the hood."
- **Design review:** a peer's reactive system re-renders synchronously on every change and janks. Explain microtask batching as the fix (the same event-loop knowledge as M5/M30).
- **Meetup (30s):** "I rebuilt the core of React's reactivity in plain JavaScript — a Proxy watching your state — and now I actually know what the framework does for me."
- **Reach-back (floor thread):** M5 handed you RxJS, M36 rebuilt an Observable, this rebuilds reactivity — three views of "state changing over time." What does the framework hide that all three reveal, and what did microtask batching here share with M36's push protocol?

---

## M44 — The Design File · UI/UX in Figma · (S)

> **Story.** As a design-engineer, I want to design a real screen in Figma with proper structure, so that I can produce a build-ready spec and speak the designer's language — the other half of design engineering. **MVP.** A designed screen in Figma using frames, auto-layout, components with variants, styles/tokens, constraints, and a click-through prototype. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - The screen uses auto-layout so it adapts rather than being pixel-pinned.
> - Repeated elements are components with variants, not copies.
> - Colors and type are defined as reusable styles/tokens.
> - Constraints make it respond to resizing; a prototype links screens for a click-through. (Output is a Figma file, not code.)

**Anchors:** _Pixels With Intent_ (the visual-craft sensibility); M15/M28 (which this can spec); your design-engineering lane.

### Briefing

Design engineering is your lane, and it has two halves — _building_ UI (much of Volume I) and _designing_ it. **Figma** is the tool designers use, and fluency in it lets you both produce your own specs and collaborate credibly with designers. Define as you go: a **frame** (a container — an artboard or screen), **auto-layout** (Figma's system where elements arrange and resize like flexbox, instead of fixed coordinates), a **component** (a reusable master; instances update when the master changes) and **variants** (versions of a component — a button's default/hover/disabled), **design tokens** (named, reusable values for color/spacing/type — a design system's vocabulary), and **constraints** (how an element responds when its frame resizes). The center of gravity is **structured, systematic design** — auto-layout, components/variants, tokens — so a design is a maintainable spec, not a static picture.

### The trap

**Auto-layout nesting, and the detach spiral.** Auto-layout is powerful, but nesting it (layouts inside layouts) has a learning curve, and when it fights you it's tempting to _detach_ an instance from its component to force a change — which breaks the system, because that instance no longer updates with the master. A file full of detached, diverging copies is the trap.

### The build

Design a real screen (a card layout, a dashboard, a settings page) — frames, auto-layout, components + variants, styles/tokens, constraints, and a click-through prototype. Output is a Figma file; your token set and component set are the design system's spec.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Token structure.** _Axis:_ systematic vs ad hoc. _Forcing:_ How do you name and organize color/spacing/type so they're reusable?
2. **Type scale.** _Axis:_ harmonious vs arbitrary. _Forcing:_ What set of sizes, and by what ratio?
3. **Component granularity.** Options: a new component · a variant of an existing one. _Axis:_ reuse vs proliferation. _Forcing:_ Is this a different _thing_ or a _state_ of the same thing?
4. **Constraints vs auto-layout for responsiveness.** _Forcing:_ Which handles "resize with the frame," and where does each fit?
5. **Grid/layout system.** _Forcing:_ What column grid anchors the screen?

### Research targets

- _Auto-layout and constraints._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can build a screen that adapts on resize. _Seed:_ Figma's official auto-layout guide.
- _Design tokens and type scale._ — **25 min.** Done when you can define a reusable token set.
- _Components vs variants._ — **25 min.** Done when you can decide correctly between them.
- _Visual hierarchy (the theory half)._ — **25 min.** Done when you can name a few hierarchy principles. _Seed:_ Laws of UX / NN/g (Refactoring UI is excellent but paid).

### Out of scope (fence)

A full design system with dozens of components, dev-mode handoff specifics, plugins, complex prototyping animation, branding from scratch. Keep it one well-structured screen. → parking lot.

### Measure ritual

Run the prototype click-through, then resize a frame. **The number:** does the layout adapt correctly (auto-layout + constraints working) rather than break, and do your components stay in sync (no detached copies)? Plus a contrast check on your palette (accessible?).

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** the prototype click-through; a frame resizing cleanly; components updating from their master.

- **Interview (90s):** "I designed a screen in Figma with real structure — auto-layout so it adapts, components with variants instead of copies, and design tokens for color and type. The discipline I'd flag is not detaching instances to force a change, because that breaks the system — the whole point of components is that they stay in sync."
- **Design review:** a peer's file is static rectangles with detached, diverging copies. Explain auto-layout, components/variants, and why detaching is the trap.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been leveling up the design half of design engineering — a proper Figma file with auto-layout and a token system, so my designs are specs, not pictures."
- **Reach-back (design line / instruments):** a file like this becomes the spec for M15's layout or M45's UI. What does designing it _first_ give the build that jumping straight to code doesn't — and how does it connect to M28's award-tier intent?

## M45 — The Deck That Syncs · local-first spaced repetition (the Matuschak angle) · (M)

> **Story.** As a tools-for-thought builder inspired by Matuschak's memory work, I want a spaced-repetition deck that works offline and syncs across devices without a central server-of-record, so that I understand local-first software and CRDTs — sync that's a real merge, not a last-write-wins gamble. **MVP.** Flashcards that persist locally (IndexedDB), sync across two tabs/peers via a CRDT, work offline, with a simple spaced-repetition scheduler deciding what's due. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Cards persist locally and the app works fully offline.
> - Edits made on two peers while disconnected _merge_ on reconnect (convergence), not clobber each other.
> - A spaced-repetition scheduler (SM-2-lite) surfaces due cards.
> - You can create a conflict deliberately and show it converging.

**Anchors:** the sync line (with M46); the sovereignty thread (data you own); the instruments thread; Matuschak's notes as the philosophical north star.

### Briefing

Andy Matuschak's work on memory systems and tools-for-thought — spaced repetition, the mnemonic medium — is a north star for the software you want to build. This module builds a spaced-repetition deck — flashcards that resurface just before you'd forget them — with a twist that teaches deep ideas: it's **local-first**. Define as you go: **local-first software** (your data lives on your device and works offline; sync is an enhancement, not a requirement — you _own_ your data, unlike cloud apps where the server is the truth), **spaced repetition** (an algorithm scheduling reviews at growing intervals — SM-2 is Anki's classic), **IndexedDB** (the browser's on-device database for structured data), and a **CRDT** — a _Conflict-free Replicated Data Type_ — a data structure designed so independent edits on different devices can _always_ be merged automatically into the same result, with no central coordinator. The center of gravity is **local-first architecture and CRDT-based sync** — data you own that merges instead of clobbering.

### The trap

**"Sync is not a merge button."** The naive idea of sync is "whoever saved last wins" (last-write-wins) — but that's a _data-loss policy_ with good PR: two people edit offline, and one's work silently vanishes on reconnect. Real sync must _merge_ concurrent edits, which is exactly what CRDTs are for and why they're subtle. Treating sync as a simple overwrite is the trap.

### The build

Cards persist in IndexedDB, edits sync across two tabs/peers via a CRDT library (Yjs or Automerge — a decision), the app works offline, and an SM-2-lite scheduler picks what's due. Create a conflict deliberately and demonstrate convergence. A card is CRDT-managed state; the sync protocol is the library's.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Yjs vs Automerge.** _Axis:_ the two main JS CRDT libraries — performance/model differences. _Forcing:_ Which fits your data and mental model? (Read each's approach before choosing.)
2. **LWW map vs richer CRDT types per field.** _Axis:_ simple vs true merge. _Forcing:_ Which card fields genuinely need concurrent merge, and which can safely last-write-wins?
3. **Sync transport.** Options: WebRTC peer-to-peer · a small websocket relay. _Axis:_ no-server purity vs simplicity. _Forcing:_ How do two peers find and reach each other with no server-of-record?
4. **IndexedDB vs SQLite-wasm.** _Axis:_ native browser vs richer queries. _Forcing:_ What do your local queries need?
5. **SM-2 vs FSRS.** _Axis:_ classic-and-simple vs modern-and-better. _Forcing:_ Which do you implement now (SM-2), and what's FSRS (awareness)?

### Research targets

- _Local-first software — the principles._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can state the "own your data, works offline, sync as enhancement" idea. _Seed:_ the Ink & Switch "Local-first software" essay.
- _CRDTs and convergence._ — **45 min (deep).** Done when you can explain why independent edits always merge to the same state. _Seed:_ a Martin Kleppmann CRDT talk.
- _IndexedDB._ — **25 min.** Done when you can persist and query cards on-device.
- _The SM-2 spaced-repetition algorithm._ — **25 min.** Done when you can compute the next review interval. _Seed:_ the Anki/SuperMemo SM-2 description.

### Out of scope (fence)

Building your own CRDT from scratch (use a library — note the fascination, park; it's a research area), real end-to-end-encrypted sync, a full Anki clone, mobile apps, a sync server-of-record (the point is _no_ server-of-record). → parking lot. **Kernel note:** M45 and M46 both do CRDT sync — you'll deliberately duplicate that plumbing; extract the shared kernel only after the duplication is visible in _both_ (your stable-below/fluid-above principle).

### Measure ritual

Kill the network, edit the same card (or add cards) on two peers, reconnect. **The number:** does the state _converge_ to a consistent merged result on both peers (no lost edits), and can you narrate _why_ the CRDT guarantees it? Convergence-after-conflict is the bar.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** offline edits on two peers merging on reconnect; the scheduler surfacing due cards.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a local-first spaced-repetition deck — cards live on-device in IndexedDB, work offline, and sync across peers with a CRDT so concurrent edits _merge_ instead of clobbering. The idea I'd share is that naive sync is last-write-wins, which is a data-loss policy in disguise — two offline edits and one silently vanishes. A CRDT is a data structure built so independent edits always converge, no central server needed."
- **Design review:** a peer built sync as last-write-wins. Walk through the offline-edit data-loss scenario and why a CRDT is the fix.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I've been building a local-first flashcard app — works offline, syncs without a server, CRDTs so your edits never get clobbered. It's the Matuschak tools-for-thought direction."
- **Reach-back (sync line + sovereignty + instruments):** M46 is the other half of the sync line, and you deliberately duplicated the CRDT plumbing — where's the shared kernel you'd extract now? And how does "data you own that works offline" express the sovereignty thread?

---

## M46 — The Shared Room · CRDT multiplayer world · (L)

> **Story.** As a builder exploring multiplayer and tools-for-thought, I want one shared 2D room where avatars move and a layer is collaboratively editable, so that I feel the presence-versus-persistence distinction and multiplayer state — the CRDT game. **MVP.** A shared 2D room — avatars move on arrow keys, presence shows who's connected, and one shared layer (tiles or sticky notes) is collaboratively editable and persists. **Acceptance criteria.**
> 
> - Two windows show each other's avatars moving in near-real-time.
> - Presence (who's here, cursors) is _ephemeral_ — it vanishes when a peer disconnects, no ghosts.
> - The shared editable layer is _persistent_ CRDT state that survives and merges concurrent edits.
> - It stays coherent under injected latency.

**Anchors:** M7 (the ephemeral/persistent distinction — this is that trap in a shared world); M19 (the game loop); M45 (the CRDT sync you built); the sync line and instruments threads. Use your Aseprite pixel sprites for the avatars.

### Briefing

A shared multiplayer space — a tiny virtual room where you see other people's avatars move — is a delightful way to feel two ideas that matter well beyond games. This builds one shared 2D room, using the CRDT sync from M45, and teaches the crucial **presence-versus-persistence** distinction. Define as you go: **presence** (ephemeral, live-only state — where each connected avatar is, who's typing — true only while a connection is alive, meaningless after it drops), **persistent state** (the world itself — placed tiles, sticky notes — which must survive disconnects and restarts), an **awareness protocol** (the standard way CRDT libraries share ephemeral presence _separately_ from the persistent document), and the **game loop** (input → update → render → repeat, from M19). The center of gravity is **the ephemeral/persistent split and shared multiplayer state** — presence handled one way, the world another.

### The trap

**Putting cursors in the document.** Store ephemeral presence (avatar positions, cursors) in the persistent CRDT document and two bad things happen: the document grows forever with position updates, and disconnected users' avatars _ghost_ — they linger, because their last position persists. Presence must be ephemeral (the awareness protocol); only the world belongs in the persistent document. This is M7's chat trap — ephemeral vs persistent — now in a shared world.

### The build

One shared 2D room — a canvas (or a light game framework) game loop, avatars driven by arrow keys (your Aseprite sprites), presence via an awareness protocol (ephemeral), and one shared editable layer (tile placement or sticky notes) as persistent CRDT state. Two windows are the demo. Presence state (positions) is ephemeral/awareness; world state (tiles) is the persistent CRDT document.

### Decision map

▸ _Write your DSA before building._

1. **Awareness vs document for avatar positions.** _Axis:_ ephemeral presence vs persistent state. _Forcing:_ Where do positions belong so the world doesn't bloat and avatars don't ghost? (The trap, as a decision.)
2. **Transport.** Options: WebRTC · a websocket relay. _Axis:_ peer-to-peer vs a coordination point. _Forcing:_ How do peers connect, and what carries the awareness updates?
3. **World representation.** _Forcing:_ How do you model the tile map / sticky layer as CRDT state?
4. **Loop structure.** Options: a `requestAnimationFrame` tick · event-driven updates. _Axis:_ smooth animation vs simplicity. _Forcing:_ Does the room animate continuously (rAF, from M19/M17) or update on events?
5. **Remote-avatar smoothing.** Options: none · interpolation. _Axis:_ simple vs smooth motion. _Forcing:_ Network updates are jumpy — do you interpolate between them? (Fence full client-side prediction.)

### Research targets

- _Awareness/presence protocols._ — **25 min.** Done when you can share ephemeral presence separately from the document. _Seed:_ the Yjs awareness docs.
- _Ephemeral vs persistent state._ — **25 min.** Done when you can classify positions vs world correctly. (Reaches M7 directly.)
- _The game loop._ — **25 min.** Done when you can structure input → update → render. (Reaches M19.)
- _CRDT shared state._ — **25 min.** Done when you can put the world in a shared CRDT. (Builds on M45.)

### Out of scope (fence)

Server-authoritative state / anti-cheat, physics, a real game engine, voice/video, matchmaking, client-side prediction and rollback (note — park; the deep multiplayer end). Keep it one small room. → parking lot. **Kernel note:** this and M45 share CRDT plumbing — now that you've built both, the shared kernel is visible; extract it (stable-below/fluid-above).

### Measure ritual

Two windows with artificial latency injected. **The number:** do avatars move in near-real-time and the shared world state converge under concurrent edits, while presence _vanishes_ cleanly when a window disconnects (no ghost)? Convergence plus clean presence teardown is the bar.

### Prove it — the three rooms

**Demonstrate:** two windows with avatars moving; a shared tile edit converging; a disconnect cleanly removing presence.

- **Interview (90s):** "I built a shared 2D room — avatars moving in real-time, a collaboratively-editable tile layer — and the design point is presence versus persistence: avatar positions and cursors are _ephemeral_, handled by an awareness protocol, while the world is _persistent_ CRDT state. Put presence in the document and you get ghost avatars and unbounded growth — it's the ephemeral/persistent distinction from a WebSocket chat, in a shared world."
- **Design review:** a peer stored avatar positions in the persistent document. Explain the ghosting and unbounded-growth problems and the awareness-protocol fix.
- **Meetup (30s):** "I built a little multiplayer room where you see everyone's avatars move — the fun lesson is which state should vanish when you leave (your cursor) versus persist (what you built)."
- **Reach-back (sync line + instruments — closing the volume):** this completes the sync line with M45, and you've now built the CRDT plumbing twice — state the shared kernel you extracted. And the ephemeral/persistent split traces to M7's chat and the game loop to M19 — name what those three share.

# Epilogue — the campaign, in full

Two volumes, forty-six modules. Volume I built the spine — APIs and contracts, change over time, data, a supply-chain vertical, frontend craft, the machine, and range. Volume II added twenty-one more: capture and text, responsive surfaces, code beyond the browser, networks seen and unseen, the foundations under the algorithms, the capstone instrument, the employability bridges, and the craft-and-frontier work that pulls hardest on who you actually are.

You run all of it yourself now — no live coach, just the loop and its gates. Read the story, name the trap, write the DSA before you compare, work the research under its timebox, build to the MVP and no further, measure one number, speak the three rooms, and debrief out loud. The gates are your spot-checks; a gate that wouldn't pass tells you exactly what's unfinished.

Watch the threads tie the whole thing together, because they're the story you'll tell in rooms: one contract honored across four ecosystems and guarded and watched; recursion in three costumes; time as a first-class axis, four ways; the floor under every convenience, walked all the way down to kilobytes and back up to a rebuilt Observable; and — new in this volume — the instruments that make computation legible, the sovereignty of computing you can see all the way down, and the sync line where you built the same kernel twice on purpose so you'd know it when you saw it. Those last three aren't just modules. They're your thesis, made buildable.

The premise hasn't changed since the first page of Volume I: readiness is inventory, not mood, and you make your own luck by getting ready now. Forty-six features is a lot of luck to manufacture. Cherry-pick against the market, run the module that matches the posting in front of you, and let the inventory meet the opportunity.

Pick one. Two-minute rubber-duck debrief when you finish it: _what did I decide, and why._
